# PREFACE

---

<margin>
Stechert. <note>Likely referring to G.E. Stechert & Co., a well-known international book importer of the era.</note>
MAR 11 1915
</margin>

SOME time ago, the Administration of the Vatican Library—continuing to honor the desire expressed in 1898 by the International Congress of Saint Gall <note>A historic meeting of librarians and scholars dedicated to the preservation and restoration of ancient manuscripts</note> for the conservation of ancient manuscripts—offered its services to the Venerable Chapter of the Cathedral of Vercelli for the necessary restoration of several very precious codices <note>Handwritten books, especially those of ancient origin</note> belonging to that highly significant Chapter Library, which were in a poor state of preservation. Taking advantage of this rare opportunity, during which some of those manuscripts were to be temporarily transferred to the Vatican Library, it seemed appropriate to propose to the Venerable Chapter a phototypic reproduction <note>A high-quality photographic printing process used to create exact facsimiles of historical documents</note> of the famous Anglo-Saxon codex, which is one of the most precious treasures in which that Library takes pride.

The Venerable Chapter most willingly agreed to the proposal, being very eager to ensure, through this means as well, that scholarship is protected against the risks arising from the ever-dreaded loss of those precious parchments, and to facilitate even more widely the study of such important texts.

<term>Vatican Library</term> (the central library of the Holy See), <term>International Congress of Saint Gall</term> (an 1898 assembly on manuscript care), <term>Vercelli</term> (a city in Northern Italy), <term>Chapter Library</term> (a library belonging to a cathedral's governing body), <term>phototypic reproduction</term> (an early high-fidelity photographic facsimile), <term>Anglo-Saxon codex</term> (specifically the "Vercelli Book," a 10th-century collection of Old English poetry and prose), <term>parchments</term> (animal skins prepared as writing surfaces).

<margin>
<note>Library acquisition stamp: March 11, 1915</note>
</margin>

<meta>Accession number 174311</meta>

# PREFACE

---

Since the administration of the Vatican Library was entrusted with the care of this publication, it reached out to one of the most talented scholars of the Anglo-Saxon <note>Also known as Old English, the earliest recorded form of the English language, spoken from the mid-5th to the late 11th century</note> texts contained in the Vercelli Codex <note>The "Vercelli Book" (Codex Vercellensis), a 10th-century manuscript containing Old English poetry and prose, mysteriously located in northern Italy</note>, asking him to prepare a suitable introduction. The distinguished Professor Maximilian Foerster of the University of Leipzig kindly agreed to this request. The plan for the publication, including all its minute details, was subsequently established by the distinguished Professor in full harmony with the Administration of the Vatican Library.

We have no doubt that those countries most closely interested in the study of the Anglo-Saxon language and other related languages will be grateful to the Venerable Chapter of the Cathedral of Vercelli for the authorization so liberally granted, and to the distinguished Professor Foerster for his learned and extensive illustration <note>In this context, "illustrazione" refers to the scholarly commentary and descriptive analysis of the text</note> of the manuscript and the texts contained within it.

It only remains for us to express the hope that this reproduction—even in the limited number of copies we were able to have printed—once made available to scholars in various libraries, will prove to be of great advantage to those devoted to these specialized philological <note>The branch of knowledge that deals with the structure, historical development, and relationships of a language or languages</note> disciplines.

->F. E.<- <note>Likely Franz Ehrle (1845–1934), a Jesuit priest and scholar who served as the Prefect of the Vatican Library</note>

<term>Vatican Library</term> (the central research library of the Holy See), <term>Vercelli Codex</term> (a famous 10th-century Old English manuscript), <term>Anglo-Saxon</term> (the Germanic language of England before the Norman Conquest), <term>University of Leipzig</term> (one of the world's oldest universities and a center for Germanic studies), <term>Vercelli</term> (a city in the Piedmont region of Northern Italy), <term>Philology</term> (the study of language in oral and written historical sources)

# INTRODUCTION

---

## I.

### **Description of the Manuscript.**

The manuscript, of which we present here the photographic reproduction, is kept in the Chapter Library of Vercelli, <note>A library belonging to a cathedral chapter, the body of clergy serving the cathedral</note> cataloged as "Codex CXVII." It currently consists of 136 parchment <note>Animal skin prepared for writing; the text uses the term "membranacei"</note> leaves, each of which contains between 23 and 32 lines of writing. There are, however, several leaves written only in part—such as folios <note>The term "folio" refers to a leaf of the manuscript; "a" refers to the front (recto) and "b" to the back (verso)</note> 16a, 24b, 29a, 54a, 71a, 101a, and 120b—or even left entirely blank, such as the final two pages (folios 136a and 136b). The average size of the leaves is 31 by 20 centimeters; the portion occupied by the text is mostly 24 by 15 centimeters; therefore, our reproduction is reduced by one-third compared to the original size. The parchment is extremely thin, of the variety that was primarily used in England, so that the writing—as can also be seen from our facsimile (for example, on folio 33a)—sometimes shows through on the opposite side and from it

<note>Footnote at the bottom of the page:</note>

<sup>1</sup> Philologists <note>Scholars who study the history of language and literature</note> usually designate this manuscript simply by the name *Codex Vercellensis* or *Vercelli-Book*. But to avoid any danger of confusion, into which even Grein fell <note>original: *Bibliothek der angelsächsischen Poesie* (Library of Anglo-Saxon Poetry), 1857, vol. I, p. 364</note>, it is necessary to point out that theologians also speak of a *Codex Vercellensis*, but they mean by this name the famous Book of the Gospels <note>An "evangeliario" or Evangeliary, a book containing the Gospel readings for the year</note>, perhaps written by Bishop Saint Eusebius (died 371 AD), which today is the most precious relic of the treasury of the Vercelli Cathedral (for literature on this subject, see L. Traube, <note>original: *Vorlesungen und Abhandlungen* (Lectures and Treatises)</note> vol. I, 1909, p. 247, and F. Ehrle and P. Liebaert, <note>original: *Specimina codicum latinorum Vaticanorum* (Specimens of Latin Vatican Manuscripts)</note> Bonn, 1912, plate 5<sup>c</sup>).

<summary>This page provides a physical description of the Vercelli Book (Codex CXVII), noting its dimensions, parchment quality, and current location. It also clarifies the scholarly distinction between this Anglo-Saxon codex and a much older Latin Gospel book also held in Vercelli.</summary>

Wattenbach, *Medieval Paleography* <note>original: *Schriftwesen des Mittelalters*</note>, ² 1896, p. 212), of which, however, folio 94 today preserves nothing more than a trace. Several erasures and scrapings, as well as certain blackenings caused by the application of chemical reagents, ² make the reading of the writing difficult or impossible: this is the case on folios 1 recto, 1 verso, 25 recto, 26 recto, 36 verso, 37 verso, 38 recto, 38 verso, 42 verso, 54 recto, 55 verso, 57 recto, 65 recto, 75 verso, 77 recto, 84 recto, 86 recto, 86 verso, and 135 verso. <note>The letters "a" and "b" in the original refer to the front (recto) and back (verso) of a leaf</note>

The 135 large-format leaves are distributed in the usual way into quires or gatherings, <note>Groups of folded sheets sewn together to form a book</note> which, in the form in which we possess them today, consist of two leaves (Quire XVII), six (Quires III, XI, XII, XIV), seven (Quires VI, XIII, XV, XVI, XIX), eight (Quires IV, V, VII, VIII, IX, X, XVIII), or even nine (Quires I and II). The odd number of leaves in certain quires is explained by observing that small pieces of parchment, insufficient for a whole leaf and only enough to form a single isolated leaf, were used several times. Such leaves were then connected to the quire by means of a fold <note>A "stub" or narrow strip of parchment used to hook a single leaf into the binding</note>. ³ In a single case (at folio 136), the leaf was simply attached to the back part of the quire. But the current arrangement of the quires does not correspond

# I. — DESCRIPTION OF THE MANUSCRIPT

more in every respect to the original, since in ten cases—as clearly appears from the content and the form of the relevant texts <gloss>¹</gloss>—leaves have been lost, namely some that followed folios 42, 55, 63, 83, 85, 100, 103, 111, 118 and probably also after folio 75 (the two middle leaves). And since we mostly find that the lost leaves are generally two per quire <note>A quire is a gathering of parchment sheets folded and sewn together</note> (VIII, X, XI, XIV, XVI), we must say that quires VI, XI, and XIV had eight instead of six or seven, quire XVI had nine instead of seven, and quire VIII, and likely also quire X, had ten leaves instead of eight. Specifically, the quires are distributed as follows: quire I = folios 1-9; II = folios 10-18; III = folios 19-24; IV = folios 25-32; V = folios 33-40; VI = folios 41-47; VII = folios 48-55; VIII = folios 56-63; IX = folios 64-71; X = folios 72-79; XI = folios 80-85; XII = folios 86-91; XIII = folios 92-98; XIV = folios 99-101; XV = folios 105-111; XVI = folios 112-118; XVII = folios 119-120; XVIII = folios 121-128; XIX = folios 129-135. The individual quires, only in the process of time, I believe, and by another writer, were numbered and marked <note>This refers to "signatures," which are marks used by binders to keep the book in order</note> by placing a Roman numeral at the top of the first page of each, and then at the bottom of the last page one of the letters from A to T. But not all the numbers and letters are preserved or legible today. In quires VIII and XVI, both of these markings are missing, as the entire outer double leaf has been lost from them. Quire XI is missing the final leaf and therefore also the corresponding signature L. Nothing more can be discerned of the number in quire I, since the entire first page has become illegible; but the signature A placed on folio 9 *verso* <note>original: 9 b; "b" refers to the back of a page, or verso</note> is enough to assure us that this quire is truly the first. It seems that quires XVII and XIX lack the corresponding letters (R and T); they do, however, have their Roman order number at the beginning. In all other cases, the order numbers and letters for each quire still remain. <gloss>²</gloss>

# THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK

No trace of an earlier pagination is visible; the current numbering of the leaves from 1 to 135 dates only from the 19th century. That the scribe did not disdain to use <term>incised lines</term> <note>Grooves pressed into the parchment with a stylus to guide the handwriting</note> for the writing and the margins is also apparent from our facsimile, for example at folios 24 *a*, 25 *b*, 26 *b*, 32 *a*, 61 *a*, and 131 *a*.

In general, it can be said that the scribe proved to be very diligent and accurate in his work, such that the writing is always clear and legible, and only rarely did he have to resort to erasures or corrections; there are also few errors and omissions. <gloss>¹</gloss>

On the other hand, he cared little for giving the manuscript an artistic form. No more than three times (folios 49 *a*, 106 *b*, 112 *a*) does one encounter decorated initials, which, however, are of very little value from an artistic standpoint; all the more so since the initial **M** on folio 112 *a*, being an exact reproduction of the same letter on folio 106 *b*, gives us a new argument for a notable poverty of invention. He made use of even the modest decoration resulting from the use of a few red strokes very rarely: that is to say, only in some titles and initials on folios 71 *b*, 73 *b*, and 75 *b*. In every other case, to give prominence to the titles and initials, he used only capital letters—large, certainly, but entirely unadorned and coarse—two of which, however, are dotted (folio 51 *b*) or hatched (folio 47 *b*) in a way that recalls the <term>Irish style</term> <note>Also known as 'Insular' style, a method of decoration characterized by intricate patterns and dots common in manuscripts from Britain and Ireland</note>.

The current leather cover dates to the 19th century and bears on the spine in black characters the words: "Book | of Homilies | in an unknown language | 41 | 10th Century | CXVII" <note>original Latin: "Homiliarum | Liber | ignoti idiomatis | 41 | Secolo x | CXVII"</note>.

hand. Truly, I would not know how to explain what this number signifies, unless it is a simple <term>pen trial</term> <note>Practice marks made by a scribe to test a newly sharpened quill</note>, like the *xb* repeated several times at the top of folios 119 *a*, 123 *a*, and 126 *a*.

<meta>This page transitions from the physical layout of the manuscript to the more complex problem of dating the Vercelli Book, specifically examining the handwriting (paleography) and internal clues within the text.</meta>

->II. - AGE AND CHARACTER OF THE SCRIPT<-

## II.

### The Age and Character of the Script.

Unfortunately, we lack any external data to establish the age of our codex. Moreover, nothing useful can be inferred even from the book’s subject matter, whether prose or poetry. This is because the frequent mentions of the end of the world—which is twice expressly declared to be imminent in Homily XI ("the end of us all is approaching very variously," <note>original: *ure ealra ende swidhe mislice toweard nealaecedh*</note> folio 73 *a*) and Homily XV ("in the last time," <note>original: *on dham nexstan tide*</note> folio 80 *b*)—as well as the laments found on folio 73 *a* regarding the looting and destruction of churches and monasteries by "heathen men" (that is, the Danes), do not, on their own, serve as anything more than a way to determine *the age of composition* of those specific homilies or their Latin sources. These passages could very well have been preserved even by a much later scribe.

We are therefore reduced to arguing simply from the general impression made upon us by the <term>paleographical</term> <note>The study of ancient handwriting styles to determine age and origin</note> characters of the codex. But unfortunately, if there is one branch of the science of writing that has been neglected, it is that of Anglo-Saxon paleography, which has only recently been outlined in its most essential features by Wolfgang Keller. This is why past opinions on the date of the Vercelli manuscript were so divergent, always oscillating between dates that were either too early or too late. After Jacob Grimm—the first¹ to express an opinion on the subject—established its date, which was certainly too early,

---

¹ In truth, as early as 1748, the learned Italian biblical scholar Bianchini had attributed our codex to the 10th century, even though he could not read the text: "there is no doubt that it was written no later than the 10th century." <note>original Italian: *nè v’ha dubbio, che non sia stato scritto al più tardi del X secolo*</note> However, he did so in a private letter, which was only published in 1824 by De Gregory in *History of Vercellese Literature*, Vol. IV, page 556 (Turin, 1825); and which, moreover, has gone unnoticed by scholars until now. — J. Grimm, *Andreas und Elene* (1840) page XLV.

<summary>This page discusses the challenges in dating the Vercelli Book, noting that internal textual references to Viking raids or the apocalypse only date the original compositions, not the physical manuscript itself. It highlights the early scholarly efforts by Jacob Grimm and the overlooked 18th-century opinion of Bianchini.</summary>
<keywords>paleography, dating manuscripts, Wolfgang Keller, Jacob Grimm, Bianchini, Danish raids, Old English homilies, Vercelli Book</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dating of the Vercelli Book. It moves from mentioning Jacob Grimm’s early 9th-century estimate to the "opposite extreme" of 11th-century dates proposed by later 19th-century scholars, before settling on Wolfgang Keller’s more precise late 10th-century dating based on comparative paleography.</meta>

to the "beginning of the 10th or even the 9th century," Kemble,¹ Wülker,² Cook,³ Krapp,⁴ and M. B. Smith,⁵ clearly fell into the opposite extreme, assigning it to the beginning or even the first half of the 11th century. Keller was the first to make an objective resolution of this difficult problem possible through a systematic comparison of dated documents in the British Museum. Based on his comparative study, we can now establish with precision that the script of the Vercelli codex belongs to that type of <term>Irish-Anglo-Saxon cursive</term> <note>Also known as Insular minuscule, a script style developed in Ireland and then used in England</note> which, starting from the middle of the 10th century in southern England, and not without continental influence,⁶ began to take on a broader, thicker, and more regular form. However, it did not yet reach that compact and strong style, nor that entirely perpendicular pen stroke, which crept into English national writing at the beginning of the 11th century due to the influence of the <term>Frankish minuscule</term> <note>A clear, standardized script developed in France under Charlemagne, which eventually replaced local styles across Europe</note> used in Latin texts. We can, therefore, with sufficient certainty, place our manuscript in the second half of the 10th century. Keller actually believes he can provide a more precise determination: "roughly the time between 960 and 980." This is how he expresses it on page 39 and following of his *Anglo-Saxon Paleography* (Berlin 1906): "Wülker, in the preface to the facsimile (p. vii), says: 'The manuscript, by reason of its type of script, is mani-

# II. — AGE AND CHARACTER OF THE SCRIPT 13

...ipt, belongs rightly to the beginning of the 11th century. But for my part, evidence to the contrary includes the mostly pointed apex of the descenders <note>the lower parts of letters like p, q, or g that fall below the baseline</note>, the frequent use of the round ***s***, and the ***y*** with diverging strokes without a dot—though the other forms are indeed found, namely the aforementioned letters with and without a dot, and the F-shaped form, but they are very rare—furthermore, the three-stroke ***a***, the horizontal stroke of the ***t*** and ***g*** being slanted, and finally our large ***e***. Taken together, these data seem to me not to lead us back to the first half of the 11th century, but rather toward the time between 960 and 980." Recently, in his article on Anglo-Saxon script published in Hoops' *Encyclopedia of Germanic Antiquity* <note>original: *Reallexicon der germanischen Altertumskunde*</note> (Strasbourg 1911), he attempted on page 102 to approximately establish the date in the period "roughly between 970 and 980." But while Keller, by placing the codex in the 10th century, has certainly hit the mark, his overly specific date—restricted to a period of only twenty or even ten years—cannot help but arouse some skepticism.

In my view, such a precise dating, given the significant lack of external evidence <note>historical records or dates actually written in the book by the scribe</note>, could at most be ventured only if we possessed a copious amount of dated material from the same school of scribes, without gaps or interruptions. However, what Keller offers us for a comparative study is drawn from a wide variety of different scribes, without considering that the evolution of writing does not proceed at the same pace in every individual location. The chronological order within the scope of the second half of the 10th century that Keller assigns to the main poetic manuscripts—based on the greater or lesser apparent antiquity of their script—namely: the Exeter Manuscript, the Vercelli Manuscript, the Caedmon Manuscript, and the Beowulf Manuscript; this order, I say, could only have objective value if it were certain that the scale of *apparent* antiquity corresponds exactly to the sequence of *actual* antiquity. Now, that the reality is quite different is shown even by our Anglo-Saxon manuscript of Beowulf, where the second scribe—the one who copied the second half of the poem—displays a style of writing that appears older

<summary>The author critiques Wolfgang Keller's narrow dating of the Vercelli Book (960–980 AD), arguing that script styles evolve at different rates across different locations and schools. He notes that "apparent" age in a script does not always reflect its actual historical date, citing the Beowulf manuscript as an example where a later scribe used an older-looking style.</summary>

# THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK

...than that used by the first scribe; yet he could not, naturally, have written before them.

Similarly, in the York Chapter Gospel Book <note>The York Gospels is a 10th-century illuminated manuscript kept at York Minster.</note>, the first page of the Gospel of St. Matthew appears "in a characteristic type of English script that may have been introduced in Canterbury around 1020, while the rest of the manuscript presents an older type of writing." ¹ Finally, beyond local differences, one must also account for the variation in handwriting from generation to generation—something still visible today: an old man writes differently than his son in the same decade. Therefore, in determining dates, it is best to leave a margin of error of at least a generation, or 30 years.

Consequently, the archaic features correctly observed by Keller in the Vercelli codex <term>Codex: A manuscript book of ancient texts.</term> might stem from a somewhat conservative school or the advanced age of the copyist, without it being strictly necessary to assume such an early date as Keller does. Furthermore, I would be inclined to believe the copyist was a man of mature age, because the extreme rarity of obvious writing errors and, above all, the great uniformity of the characters maintained throughout such an extensive manuscript suggest a well-trained scribe.

Based on these considerations, we would do better to remain more general and place the manuscript either in the "second half of the 10th century," as Holthausen does, ² or "toward the end of the 10th century" following Brandl. ³

It should also be particularly noted that the Latin citations are still traced in insular script <term>Insular script: A medieval writing style originating in Ireland and Great Britain, distinct from the styles used on mainland Europe.</term>, and not in Frankish cursive <term>Frankish cursive: Also known as Carolingian minuscule, this was a standardized script from the European continent that eventually replaced the local Insular styles in England.</term>, which became increasingly common toward the end of the 10th century.

### II. – AGE AND CHARACTER OF THE HANDWRITING

Runic characters <note>Runes were the alphabetic letters used by Germanic peoples before the adoption of the Latin alphabet. In Anglo-Saxon manuscripts, they were sometimes used as symbols or for puzzles.</note> are used for the acrostic of a name on folio 54 *a* and folio 133 *a*. An isolated runic sign is also found on folio 99 *b*, line 15—specifically, the rune for "M" representing the word *man* <note>original: "man"</note> in the phrase *no man could see the joy...* <note>original: "gefean man meahte... geseon"; this corresponds to the "Blickling Homilies", edited by Morris, page 223, line 35.</note>.

As we have already mentioned above, the entire codex <term>Codex: An ancient manuscript in book form.</term> was written by a single copyist. At most, one might wonder if, and to what extent, the few corrections—which appear generally above the line in a somewhat smaller script—derive from another hand.

The vast majority of these overwritten words, syllables, and letters ¹ certainly belong to the original scribe of the codex, as evidenced by the corresponding shapes of the letters. However, a few—such as the *g* on folio 88 *a* line 16, *ra* on folio 94 *a* line 18, the prefixed *m* on folio 108 *a* line 8, the phrase *might and majesty* <note>original: "maegen e thrymme"</note> on folio 128 *a* line 20, and the phrase not belonging to the text (perhaps a pen trial?) *write thus* <note>original: "writ thus"</note> at the bottom of folio 63 *b*—could very well have been added by a different hand.

To me, it is highly doubtful that the quire signatures <term>Quire signatures: Roman numerals or letters marked at the bottom of a page to help the binder assemble the "quires" (folded bundles of parchment) in the correct order.</term> originate from the original scribe. ² The Roman numerals used for this purpose show, in the cases of V and X, a shape quite different from that which is customary for our scribe. ³ In any case, the letters at the end of each quire are written with a different way of holding the pen—with the pen held almost perpendicularly—and they partly show forms foreign to our scribe; compare, for example, the *A* at the bottom of folio 9 *b*, the *e* on folio 40 *b*, and especially the *f* (folio 47 *b*) and the uncial *G* <note>Uncial: A script written entirely in capital letters, commonly used by Latin scribes in the early Middle Ages.</note> (folio 55 *b*).

# THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK

The mutilated verse of a psalm (Psalm 26:9) with neumes <term>Neumes: An early system of musical notation used before the modern five-line staff.</term> is certainly a late interpolation <term>Interpolation: Text added to a manuscript by a later hand that was not part of the original work.</term> that a 13th-century scribe inserted into the blank space of folio 24 *b*; the same must also be said, naturally, for the late pen trials <term>Pen trials: Scribbles or practice marks made by a scribe to test a pen nib or the flow of ink.</term> on the final page. However, regarding the opinion of Wülker—that even the essential text of the codex was written "by at least two hands (but probably three)" <gloss>1</gloss>—this has already been challenged by Napier in the *Journal for German Antiquity* <note>original: "Zeitschrift für deutsches Altertum"</note> vol. XXIII (1889), who attributed the entire codex to a single writer. Even if the handwriting appears from time to time to be a bit smaller (for example, on folio 47 *a*, 131 *a*, or 132 *b*), or a bit more crowded, or a bit more slanted and careless (as on folio 133 *b* through 135 *b*, containing the poem *Guthlac*), <gloss>2</gloss> these are simply the kinds of fluctuations that can be observed today in any writer's hand over a long period of time.

Abbreviations are used very sparingly, as is common in Anglo-Saxon texts. The most frequent of all is the small slanted line representing *m* or *n*, which is mostly used at the end of a word, but

---

<gloss>1</gloss> Thus in the *Vercelli Codex* <note>original: "Codex Vercellensis"</note> (Leipzig, 1894), page VII. He expresses his opinion more extensively in his *Outline of Anglo-Saxon Literature* <note>original: "Grundriss der angelsächsischen Literatur"</note> (Leipzig, 1885), page 239: "The codex was written not by one writer, but by two or three, especially quire *D* (folios 25-32) and quire *P* (105-111), but part of quire *O* is also by another hand. One can clearly distinguish a sharper, finer script and a broader one; but it seems to me that a third hand should also be recognized." Furthermore, he does not dare, as he says in a note, "to determine with absolute precision where one hand begins and the other ends. Different writing materials, different lighting, a different condition of the eyes, and other circumstances can influence the strokes of the same hand. It is well known that in slightly dimmer light, one unintentionally writes more thickly. Moreover, in our manuscript itself, one often finds only a couple of lines in a smaller script, and then, immediately after, the usual thickness returns with the same strokes. See, for example, the poem *Andreas*, folio 31 *a*. It is not conceivable that a second writer wrote three or four lines, and then immediately afterward the first one took the pen back from him." Cook (*The Dream of the Rood*, page VII), Holthausen (*Elene*, page IX), and Brandl (*Old English Literature* <note>original: "Altenglische Literatur"</note>, Strasbourg 1908, page 946) followed him in this opinion of multiple writers for the codex.

## III.

### **Language of the Manuscript.**

In Vercelli, as is evident from the inscription on the spine of the binding, for a long time no one was able to decipher the language of the manuscript; however, Professor Blume <note>Friedrich Blume (1797–1874) was the German legal scholar who identified the manuscript as Anglo-Saxon in 1822.</note> was the first to correctly judge it to be Anglo-Saxon. Our increased knowledge of Anglo-Saxon phonology <term>Phonology: The study of the system of relationships among the speech sounds that constitute the fundamental components of a language.</term> today places us in a position to be able to establish its linguistic character with somewhat greater determination, and to also recognize which particular dialect our manuscript belongs to.

It is composed in the West Saxon dialect, spoken in the southwest of England, in its more recent form which developed starting from the middle of the 10th century. Observing it more closely, one also recognizes that the language of the codex is not pure Late West Saxon, but is mixed here and there with linguistic elements of Early West Saxon; it also contains some elements from Kent, but above all, numerous Anglian elements <note>Anglian refers to the dialects spoken in the center (Mercia) and north (Northumbria) of England.</note>. Such a mixture of dialects cannot be explained except through an accurate examination of the linguistic character of the individual texts. It is sufficient to mention here that there are many plausible explanations that can be given. The common opinion assumes that the dialectal mixture is due to the copyist; and indeed, many clues would lead to the conclusion that the majority of the poems and several prose texts of our manuscript—despite their current West Saxon "clothing"—were originally written in an Anglian dialect, that is, in the English of the center or the north. In translating them into West Saxon, something of the primitive dialect likely remained; hence the Anglian elements of the Vercelli manuscript. But that our copyist was the author of this West Saxon refashioning does not seem very credible. In fact, the traces of Early West Saxon that occur in some texts would rather suggest...

<summary>This page discusses the identification of the Vercelli Book's language as Anglo-Saxon and its specific dialect, a Late West Saxon base mixed with older Anglian and Kentish elements.</summary>
<keywords>Vercelli Book, Friedrich Blume, Anglo-Saxon, West Saxon dialect, Anglian dialect, linguistic analysis, historical linguistics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the linguistic origins of the Vercelli Book, specifically addressing the presence of multiple Anglo-Saxon dialects within the text. It bridges from the previous page's observation that the mixture of dialects might be attributed to the copyist's own background or the sources they used.</meta>

->THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI CODEX<-

...suppose that our scribe already had before him a southern revision depending on an Early West Saxon text.

How dialectal traces from Kent found their way in is anything but easy to explain. In any case, they are too scarce,¹ for us to suppose, along with Brandl,² that the final transcriber was a native of Kent. It would be easier to suppose that some texts first passed through a copy made in the region of Kent; but perhaps even without resorting to this hypothesis, it would be enough to admit that the Saxon copyist of the Vercelli manuscript belonged to some border district near Kent.

A new explanation could be obtained by another route if it could be demonstrated that such a mixed dialect was spoken, or at least written, in some part of England. In the current state of English linguistic studies, it is not easy to prove that this form of language was truly a spoken local dialect. But it can instead be shown that the same mixture of dialects is encountered in a group of codices written in the monastery of St. Mary at Worcester and containing the works of Wulfstan I (1003-1016), bishop of the same city <note>Wulfstan, also known as Wulfstan Lupus, was a prominent archbishop and homilist famous for his "Sermon of the Wolf to the English."</note>. Among these, the copies of the sermons of the said bishop stand in the front rank; these were made by the scribe Wulfgeat and are contained in the three Oxford codices Junius 121, Hatton 113, and Hatton 114. The language of these was demonstrated by Enrico Dunkhase³ to have a West Saxon character with a mixture of elements from Mercia <note>Mercia was a powerful kingdom in central England; its dialect is distinct from the West Saxon of the south.</note> and some Kentish forms. Since these sermons coming from Worcester involve texts where the author and the transcriber belong to the same dialectal region, the linguistic mixture cannot have arisen from the transcription...

---

¹ From the work of Jane Weightman, *The Language and Dialect of the Later Old English Poetry* (Liverpool, 1907), page 70, it is found that in the poetic parts of the Vercelli codex the proportion between Anglian forms and Kentish forms is approximately 9 : 1.
² *History of Old English Literature* <note>original: "Geschichte der altenglischen Literatur"</note>, page 1110.
³ Enrico Dunkhase, *The Language of Wulfstan's Homilies in Wulfgeat's Manuscripts* <note>original: "Die Sprache der Wulfstan'schen Homilien in Wulfgeats Handschriften"</note> (Jena, 1906).

The author argues that this mixture might reflect a specific regional dialect or scribal tradition rather than just a series of copying errors.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of the Vercelli Book, transitioning from the comparison with Bishop Wulfstan’s texts in Worcester to a broader discussion of the manuscript's "Worcester-style" orthography and its eventual provenance.</meta>

### III. — LANGUAGE OF THE MANUSCRIPT

from one dialect into another; but rather, such a linguistic form must have been familiar to the writer, even if only as a written language. We may therefore, wishing to express ourselves with all caution, affirm that this mixture of West Saxon with elements of Mercia and Kent, if it is not a specific Worcester dialect, is at least a Worcester-school orthography <term>orthography: a standardized system of spelling and writing</term>, and we can say that the language of our Vercelli manuscript agrees with this written language of Worcester.

If it could be demonstrated—which so far we cannot due to a lack of preparatory studies—that this written language was restricted to the diocese of Worcester and no further, the place of origin of the Vercelli manuscript would be as good as found. However, I believe I have indications that this style extended through a wide zone across England as far as Essex. In any case, we are permitted to say that from a linguistic point of view, nothing opposes the hypothesis that our codex was written in Worcester. It was there, specifically in the second half of the 10th century under Oswald (959-992) <note>St. Oswald was a key figure in the 10th-century monastic reform in England who fostered a major center of learning at Worcester.</note>, at the time of our manuscript's origin, that a fruitful literary life flourished. ¹ Naturally, this hypothesis is not contradicted by the fact that some texts within the same Vercelli codex may have been originally written in other dialects.

### IV.

## **Provenance of the Manuscript.**

Where the Vercelli codex was written and by what route it arrived in the library of Vercelli <note>Vercelli is a city in Northern Italy; how an Anglo-Saxon book ended up there is one of the great mysteries of medieval studies.</note> is a question to which a certain answer can no longer be given today.

In the section regarding language, we have indeed attempted to show that the transcriber of the codex must have been an Englishman

---

¹ W. Keller, *The Literary Efforts of Worcester in the Anglo-Saxon Period* <note>original: "Die literarischen Bestrebungen von Worcester in angelsächsischer Zeit"</note> ("Sources and Research on the Linguistic and Cultural History of Germanic Peoples, Issue 84"), Strasbourg, 1900, p. 11 ff.

<summary>The author argues that the Vercelli Book's unique mix of dialects represents a specific "Worcester-style" of writing from the late 10th century. This linguistic evidence suggests the manuscript was likely produced in Worcester during a period of high literary activity under Bishop Oswald.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origin of the Vercelli Book, moving from linguistic analysis to a geographical conclusion. It argues against a continental origin and begins exploring how the manuscript migrated to Italy.</meta>

-># THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK<-

of the south, but this by no means proves that it was actually written in a monastery in southern England, nor even that the manuscript was produced in England at all. However, it is certainly not very likely that such an extensive book could have been written in a language that was not understood by the people near the writer. And if we add that the *Homilies* <note>The Vercelli Book contains 23 prose homilies, or sermons, intended for religious instruction.</note>, which constituted the main part of the manuscript, were intended for the practical purpose of supporting public preaching, we have a fresh reason to reject, with some certainty, the hypothesis that the Vercelli codex was produced on the continent. ¹ Consequently, we are permitted to state that the origin of the manuscript in England, and specifically in southern England, is a well-established fact.

It is more difficult to explain how the manuscript passed from England to Vercelli, both because it bears no clues or evidence of the fact—forcing us into simple conjecture— and because, given the relations between Vercelli and England, several ways were possible. But all the theories proposed so far are either unprovable or lack plausibility. ²

<meta>This page continues the investigation into how the Anglo-Saxon Vercelli Book arrived in Italy, moving from the general mystery of its journey to specific historical theories involving high-ranking clergy and medieval politics.</meta>

->### IV. – ORIGIN OF THE MANUSCRIPT<-

The hypothesis that has found the most supporters is the one originally proposed anonymously in 1845 within a review of the *Ecclesiastical Architecture of Italy* published by H. G. Knight (London, 1843). This theory appeared in the pages of the *Quarterly Review*¹ and was later more extensively championed in 1888 by Professor Albert Cook in the library bulletin of the University of California.² According to this view, the codex was brought from England to Vercelli by the Italian Cardinal Guala Bicchieri,³ who died in 1227. This cardinal, descending from a noble family of Vercelli, combined great prudence and learning with a love for art and science. He was sent by Pope Innocent III as a legate <note>A papal legate is a personal representative of the Pope sent on a specific diplomatic mission.</note> to England. During the three years (1216–1218) of his diplomatic activity there, he managed to acquire powerful political influence. Most notably, upon the death of the English King John Lackland <note>John I, known as "Lackland" (Italian: *Senzaterra*) because he was the youngest son and not expected to inherit land.</note> in 1216, he navigated the conflict between political parties so skillfully in favor of the new king—the barely nine-year-old Henry III—that he left the monarch forever in his debt.⁴ Even before this

---

just as Bishop Cyneweard in 975 "departed from Britain" <note>original Old English: *of Brytene gewât*</note>. But this proves nothing, even if this phrase did not mean "he died"—which is nevertheless the most probable meaning. This is because Florence, a chronicler of Worcester (*Chronicon*, edited by B. Thorpe, "English Historical Society," 1849, vol. I, p. 145), gives 975 as the year of his death, and we find documents issued by his successor Sigegar as early as the year 979 (Kemble, *Codex Diplomaticus*, no. 621). Using the same reasoning, one could attribute the transport of our codex to Vercelli to almost any Anglo-Saxon bishop—as with Cyneweard—and with even greater reason to any English pilgrim to Rome <note>The Italian term *romeo* refers specifically to a pilgrim traveling to Rome.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Cardinal Guala Bicchieri’s influence. It describes his return to Vercelli from England and his efforts to establish the Basilica of St. Andrew, a project often linked to his English connections and the possible arrival of the Vercelli Book.</meta>

On his journey, the wealthy cardinal had set out to immortalize his
name in Vercelli by means of a foundation: by a deed of March 10, 1215,
he had obtained from the episcopal chapter a plot of land for the found-
ation of a monastery of canons regular <term>canons regular: priests living in a community under a religious rule, often serving a specific church</term>. Barely three months after
his return, on February 20, 1219, he had the consolation of laying,
together with the Bishop of Vercelli, the first stone of the new building, which
was dedicated to the apostle St. Andrew. ^1 But only after five and a
half years of work, on December 7, 1224, was it possible to perform the solemn dedication
of the annexed church: the large and splendid Basilica of St. Andrew,
which remains today one of the most beautiful monuments in Vercelli.

This church, built in a transitional style <note>a style bridging the gap between Romanesque and Gothic architecture</note>, in many structural
and decorative elements recalls so closely the French types
of the north and English styles, that for a long time it was supposed to have been
built by an English architect whom Guala might have brought with
him from England. ^2

But the French archaeologist O. Enlart ^3 demonstrated in 1894 that
the stylistic characteristics of the Church of St. Andrew in Vercelli recall

### IV. — PROVENANCE OF THE MANUSCRIPT

clearly the Gothic style of Northern France, and that in any case the church was built by the Augustinian canons regular <term>canons regular: priests living in a community under a religious rule, often serving a specific church</term>, whom Guala summoned from St. Victor near Paris and installed in his new foundation. Consequently, if the church of Vercelli appears as an isolated and unique work in the history of Italian art, it is because Italian Gothic was entirely modeled on the style of southeastern France—which the Cistercians <term>Cistercians: a Catholic religious order of monks and nuns</term> of the 12th century used to introduce Gothic into Italy—whereas the architectural style of the Parisian Augustinians, which we encounter in the church of St. Andrew in Vercelli, remained without further imitation. Even the presumed traces of English style are explained in the same way, because English Gothic also depends upon the Gothic of Northern France.

Cardinal Guala also provided for the maintenance of his foundation while he was still staying in England, by inducing King Henry III to designate the rich revenues of the priory of St. Andrew in Chesterton, in the Diocese of Ely, for the planned Vercelli monastery of St. Andrew. ¹ By virtue of this grant of bene-

fice, enacted by a royal patent on November 8, 1217, and remaining with the Vercelli monastery until 1440, a lasting relationship was established between England and Vercelli. Thus—which for us is the most important point—throughout that entire period of the Middle Ages, the possibility of an exchange of books existed. This relationship became even closer because Guala attached a school to the rectory, which soon (1228) became a flourishing university and attracted several Englishmen. Among these was the famous Franciscan from Oxford, Adam Marsh <note>original: "Adamo de Marisco"; a prominent 13th-century scholar and theologian</note> (died 1257). Furthermore, after returning to his homeland, he always maintained a correspondence with Vercelli; in his collected letters, one finds a writing addressed to Abbot Thomas of St. Andrew in Vercelli, in which he asks for the delivery of Thomas’s new work on the *theological ministry* <note>original: "ministerium theologicum"</note>, and in exchange offers to send his own explanation of the angelic salutation <note>the "Hail Mary" prayer</note>. $^1$* Here, then, is an example of a regular exchange of writings between Vercelli and Oxford.

Certainly, in these facts, we find only a general indication of the path by which our Anglo-Saxon codex could have reached Vercelli; but we must also keep in mind that Cardinal Guala may have brought it to his homeland himself.

In the meantime, we know that in an inventory compiled shortly before his death, $^2$ among the books that he left to the Abbey of St. Andrew along with all his possessions, there were at least two *in English script* <note>original: "de littera anglicana"</note>, meaning "written in the English style of handwriting," and therefore likely originating from England. These two books—namely, a "Bible <note>original: "bibliotheca"</note> in English script, which the Lord Cardinal used in his chapel" and a "Book of Homilies <note>original: "Omeliarum"</note> from the Lord Cardinal’s chapel in good English script"—had been written specifically for the Cardinal's personal use. Although it is clear that these two manuscripts cannot in any way be identified with our codex, nevertheless—

->### IV. — PROVENANCE OF THE MANUSCRIPT<-

demonstrate in the cardinal a certain predilection for English manuscripts, which may also, to a certain degree, make the acquisition of an Anglo-Saxon codex—otherwise unintelligible to him—believable. Indeed, if that *English script* <note>original: "littera anglicana"</note> could be considered Irish-Anglo-Saxon writing—which, to tell the truth, does not seem probable to me¹—one could conclude that Cardinal Guala belonged to the small number of those who, at that time, were more or less able to read Anglo-Saxon characters; this would make the transport to Italy of a codex then considered in England to be of almost no value a bit more plausible.

From what we have presented, it appears possible that our codex was brought to Vercelli by Guala himself or arrived there as a result of the relations established between Vercelli and England. But not

---

¹ That the expression refers not to the language, but simply to the handwriting, is shown by other expressions in the list, such as "in the Bolognese script" <note>original: "de littera Boloniensi"; referring to a style of legal script from Bologna</note> (cf. Du Cange, under the entry *Scripts*; Delisle, *The Cabinet of Manuscripts of the Imperial Library*, vol. I [Paris, 1868], page 32; F. Ehrle, *History of the Library of the Roman Pontiffs*, Rome, 1890, vol. I, page 569; F. Ehrle and P. Liebaert, *Specimens of Latin Vatican Codices*, Bonn, 1912, page XXXI and following, and plates 43 and 44); "in the Parisian script" <note>original: "de littera Parisiensi"</note> (Ehrle and Liebaert, loc. cit., page XXVIII, plate 41); "in the Lombard script" <note>original: "de littera Lombarda"</note>, "in the good ancient Aretine script" <note>original: "de bona littera antiqua Aretina"</note>, and "in the ancient script" <note>original: "de littera antiqua"</note> (Ehrle, *History of the Library of the Roman Pontiffs*, vol. I, page 569). And precisely this last expression seems to me to be an argument that the aforementioned expression "English script" <note>original: "littera Anglicana"</note> does not properly mean the ancient Anglo-Saxon script. Even a Vatican catalog—to be sure, a much more recent one from 1375—uses the phrase "ancient script" <note>original: "littera antiqua"</note> to designate manuscripts from at most the end of the 12th century, containing the letters of St. Bernard (who died in 1153) and the *Scholastic History* (1170) by Peter Comestor (Ehrle, *History of the Library of the Roman Pontiffs*, vol. I, page 569, numbers 332 and 1076). If in this 14th-century catalog a minuscule script from at most two centuries earlier is designated by the name "ancient script," then the expression in Guala's inventory should at most refer to writing from the year 1025; thus, the ancient Insular <note>the distinctive "island" script of early medieval Britain and Ireland</note> script would have been called "ancient script" by him, not "English script." Furthermore, since the expressions "Bolognese script" and "Parisian script," where they are encountered, refer to contemporary varieties of the Gothic minuscule of the 13th and 14th centuries, the above-mentioned expression "English script" likely refers not to the ancient Irish-Anglo-Saxon cursive, but rather to an English variety of the Gothic minuscule—and thus a handwriting roughly similar to that represented by the Urbino manuscript 206, written in England in 1257 (sample in Ehrle and Liebaert, loc. cit., plate 40).

<meta>This page continues the critique of the theory that Cardinal Guala Bicchieri brought the Vercelli Book to Italy. is by no means a certain thing; indeed, as we shall soon see, it is not even probable. And if some scholars such as Pauli,¹ and recently J. E. Foster² and E. A. Savage,³ consider this hypothesis an established fact, and others like Cook⁴ and Krapp⁵ believe the matter already proven, we must all the more resolutely highlight the difficulties that oppose such a hypothesis.

In the first place, it should be noted that in the cardinal's aforementioned catalog, nothing is found that, even remotely, could refer to our Anglo-Saxon codex. It is an objection that cannot be escaped, except by assuming that the cardinal had given the book away even before the inventory was compiled.

There is then this other serious difficulty: our codex did not come down to us at all as a possession of the canons of St. Andrew, but rather as property of the cathedral chapter; furthermore, not even a shadow of an argument can be found to prove that the canons of St. Andrew even possessed it in an earlier period.⁶ In truth, it seems that the church and the hospital of St. Andrew no longer possess any ancient manuscripts; nor does anyone know where the books of the Abbey of St. Andrew have ended up;⁷ it is possible that they have

met the same end as the archive, which, during the wars of the revolution following secularization <note>The process of transferring church property to civil or state ownership.</note> (1802), was entirely lost except for a few documents. ¹ But this lack of information matters little to our inquiry, because we are able to demonstrate that our Anglo-Saxon manuscript did not belong to the canonry of St. Andrew even before secularization, but was located in the chapter library <note>The private library of the cathedral's clergy.</note> at least in the year 1748, and probably as early as 1602. In fact, from the year 1748, we possess a letter ² from the celebrated biblical scholar and paleographer <term>paleographer|a scholar who studies ancient and historical handwriting</term> Giuseppe Bianchini (1704–1764), sent from Verona to Cardinal Carlo Vittorio delle Lancie, in which he gives an account of the manuscripts of the "Eusebian archive of Vercelli" — that is, the chapter library — which he examined during his various travels. ...once clearly designated as follows: "No. 41 (CXVII) *A 10th-century codex. A book in an unknown language. It appears to be a book of homilies <note>Sermons or religious discourses.</note> for the liturgical year, as is evident from several rubrics <note>Instructions or headings in a manuscript, often written in red ink.</note> written in Latin (in the Germanic tongue)*." <note>original: "Codex saeculi X. Liber ignotae linguae. Videtur liber Homiliarius per anni circulum, ut constat ex nonnullis rubricis latine conscriptis (linguae theotiscae)"</note> If the codex had truly been brought to Vercelli by Cardinal Guala <note>Guala Bicchieri (c. 1150–1227), a powerful diplomat and Papal Legate who served in England and founded the Abbey of St. Andrew in Vercelli.</note>, it would be strange, to say the least, that in the year 1748 the manuscript was found not at the rectory of St. Andrew, but in the cathedral chapter library. <gloss>1</gloss> Furthermore, to prove that it was already at the cathedral by 1600, I have another piece of evidence—admittedly not as certain as the previous one, but difficult to explain in any other way. In an ancient catalog <gloss>2</gloss> of the Vercelli cathedral library, signed on June 5, 1602, by the Vicar General Giovanni Francesco Leone, there is at entry number 90 this title: *Gothic Book, or Longobardic (since I cannot read it)* <note>original: "Liber Gothicus, sive Longobardus (cum legere non valeo)"</note>, which would fit our Anglo-Saxon codex quite well, and in all likelihood actually refers to it. If this interpretation is correct, our manuscript would have already been in the cathedral library by around 1600, and not in the rectory of St. Andrew; a fact which somewhat weakens the hypothesis of its origin through Guala.

There is, finally, one last objection, which in my view is the most serious of all: it is impossible to find a reason, even partially satisfactory, that might have induced Guala to bring along a manuscript like this—large, heavy, and of a grand format, and thus inconvenient to transport—and, what is worse, entirely unintelligible to him and his associates. That Guala might have had a passion for the English language or Anglo-Germanic culture in general is a supposition that, despite his stay in England, holds little or no weight, given the conditions of that time. <gloss>3</gloss> Rather, he must

### IV. — PROVENANCE OF THE MANUSCRIPT

...having found himself exclusively in contact with the clergy and the high nobility who spoke French and were imbued with French culture. And that he was inclined toward French culture—which he had the opportunity to know directly when he was a papal legate <note>A personal representative of the Pope.</note> in Paris in 1215—is proven by this fact alone: that in his new foundation of St. Andrew, he introduced not local people, but French canons,¹ specifically the Augustinian Canons Regular <note>A religious order of priests living in community under the Rule of St. Augustine.</note> of St. Victor near Paris (1223); he appointed a Frenchman as the first abbot, the learned Thomas of Paris (who died in 1246), and had the church built by architects from northern France.

But we can even entirely set aside the persona of Cardinal Guala and state generally that in the 13th century, one could hardly have found a single member of the high clergy who was interested in an Anglo-Saxon manuscript or understood an Anglo-Saxon text—or was at least able to read the "insular" characters <note>A medieval script system used in Ireland and Great Britain, characterized by distinct letterforms often difficult for continental readers to decipher.</note> of the 10th century. It is well known that codices in Irish-Anglo-Saxon script were set aside in medieval libraries as *Scottish books* <note>original: "libri scottici"</note>,² precisely because they were illegible; and that the psalter <note>A volume containing the Book of Psalms.</note> of the Priory of St. Martin at Dover, glossed <note>Anointed with brief explanatory notes or translations between the lines.</note> in Latin but written with insular characters, was given this singular catalog entry: *Old psalter glossed in an unknown language* <note>original: "Psalterium vetus glosatum ydiomate incognito"</note>.³ Even native Englishmen could not perfectly understand the Anglo-Saxon language at the end of the 12th century, as evidenced by the many Latin glosses found in Anglo-Saxon manuscripts.⁴

All things considered, it appears highly doubtful that such a manuscript was brought to Vercelli specifically in the 13th century, and much less so in the 14th or 15th centuries. One would have to say that it—

For my part, I do not find proof of this in the fact that Matthew Paris <note>A famous 13th-century English chronicler and monk of St. Albans.</note>, <note>2</note> following the fashion of medieval chroniclers who willingly used the feast days of Saints to designate dates, has him return to England "around the feast of Saint Andrew" <note>original: *circa festum S. Furthermore, even if we were to consider the possibility that Guala had a special veneration for Saint Andrew, it does not make it very probable that he or anyone else would have noticed that our codex contains a poem in honor of Saint Andrew—especially since the name "Andrew" in the text (which is written continuously as if it were prose) is never found in a position that would catch the eye. And then, how difficult it is for a man who is not a specialist to uncover this name is also shown by the fact that a so...

### IV. — PROVENANCE OF THE MANUSCRIPT

...a skilled connoisseur of manuscripts, such as Giuseppe Bianchini <note>A renowned 18th-century Italian Oratorian priest and scholar known for his work on biblical texts.</note>, who certainly examined the codex with care, and yet did not even notice that it contained anything regarding Saint Andrew.

Having examined all these circumstances with every diligence, I conclude that:

1) if not entirely impossible, it is certainly highly improbable that our manuscript came to Vercelli through the efforts of Guala or the monastery he founded;

2) in general, the entire period from the 13th to the 15th century is to be considered the least likely time for an acquisition of this kind.

Positively, this is my conclusion: if the codex arrived in Vercelli as a book to be read and not as a mere curiosity, this could only have happened in the time between the year 1000 and 1175 at the latest. This is, in fact, the only period in which the script and the language of the codex could still be understood. And limiting ourselves to the greatest probability, we might shorten this by some 50 or 70 years; after 1125, the flourishing French theology dominated everywhere to such an extent that even in England, interest in and understanding of <term>Anglo-Saxon codices</term> <note>Manuscripts written in Old English, the language of England before the Norman Conquest of 1066.</note> became a matter for rare enthusiasts; furthermore, in the Middle Ages, a manuscript was valued less the older it was.

Greater light and certainty could be shed on this question if clearer and more abundant information could be obtained regarding the history of the very ancient <term>Chapter Library</term> <note>The "Biblioteca Capitolare," a library belonging to the cathedral's clergy (the "chapter"). Vercelli's library is one of the oldest in the world.</note>, which has until now been shrouded in obscurity, ¹ and especially regarding the origin of its many ancient codices.

### THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI CODEX

...comparable to that of our manuscript. We have one case in which, from the very material existing in that library today, we can draw such information. In fact, among the numerous codices, there is still one whose journey to Vercelli across the Alps can be proven. This is a codex containing a <term>Gregorian sacramentary</term> <note>A liturgical book containing the prayers spoken by the priest during Mass, attributed to Pope Gregory the Great.</note> from the 10th century, marked as No. CLXXXI, which bears this notice at the end, written by a slightly later hand:

> May the pious devotion of those present and of future generations know how Erkanbald, the unworthy steward of the holy community of Fulda, granted this missal book to Henry, the most venerable prelate of holy Würzburg, to serve God and His saints, on the condition that after the end of his life it be presented without delay to the service of God and Saint Boniface. <note>original Latin: "Nouerit astantium et futurorum populorum pia deuotio quemadmodum Erkanbaldus, sancti Fuldensis collegii prouisor indignus, Heinricho, sanctae Vuirziburgensis praesuli uenerabillimo, librum hunc missalem deo sanctisque suis seruiendum prestetit, eo dicto, ut post terminum uitae suae ad dei sanctique Bonifatii seruitium sine dilatione praesentetur."</note>

This notice can be dated with some certainty because we can clearly identify the people involved in the loan of the book. The first is the 18th abbot of the Benedictine monastery of <term>Fulda</term> <note>A major center of learning and manuscript production in medieval Germany.</note>, named Erkanbald, elected in 997 as head of the monastery and died in 1011; ² the other is the Bishop of Würzburg, Henry I, who held that office from 995 to 1018. ³ Therefore, the note recorded above must have been written between 997 and 1011. Just as we see them joined here in a loan of books, we also find them acting together in the history of the time; specifically when, by order of Emperor Henry II in the year 1003, they had to carry out a criminal sentence together at Schweinfurt, a castle in Franconia. It is possible that their friendship began on that occasion, which led to the granting of that sacramentary to Bishop Henry for the duration of his natural life...

---

...many of the numerous ancient codices from the 10th and 11th centuries in this library show such a uniform style that they can all be attributed to the same <term>scriptorium</term> <note>A room in a monastery dedicated to the writing and copying of manuscripts.</note>, and therefore to the one existing in the monastery of Atto. Some of the manuscripts that were certainly written in Vercelli at that time are named in Bruzza, *In Praise of the City of Vercelli, Oration* (Vercelli, 1842, p. 49, note 36).

## IV. — THE ORIGIN OF THE MANUSCRIPT

...during. Whether the book then returned to Fulda and from there took the path across the Alps, or whether it passed directly from Würzburg to Vercelli, is something that can no longer be determined today. But when one considers the relationships that existed in the 11th and 12th centuries between Germany and Lombardy, the passage of a codex from a German monastery to Vercelli should cause no wonder, especially since precisely at that time the bishopric of Vercelli formed the center of those struggles of the Lombard bishops against <term>Arduin, Marquis of Ivrea</term> <note>An Italian nobleman who was the last to claim the title King of Italy (r. 1002–1014) before the Holy Roman Emperors took full control.</note>, which several times necessitated the intervention of the Emperor. In one of these expeditions against Arduin (989) <note>While the text says 989, Erkanbald became abbot later, in 997; this likely refers to the continuous imperial campaigns of the era.</note>, the aforementioned Erkanbald, abbot of Fulda, had to accompany his emperor, <term>Otto III</term> <note>Holy Roman Emperor (996–1002) known for his "Renewal of the Roman Empire."</note>, to Italy. And in the year 1002, we find <term>Leo, Bishop of Vercelli</term> <note>A prominent poet and diplomat who served both Otto III and Henry II.</note>, with Emperor Henry II in Bavaria (Regensburg?), to ask him for help against Arduin.<sup>1</sup> Many German figures were invested with Italian bishoprics in the 11th century. And many other similar facts could still be cited. We could therefore state without hesitation that the connections between Vercelli and Germany were so lively and intense in the 11th and 12th centuries that the relations established later with England by <term>Cardinal Guala</term> <note>Guala Bicchieri (c. 1150–1227), a Vercellese cardinal who was a papal legate in England; for a long time, scholars believed he was the one who brought the Anglo-Saxon codex to Vercelli.</note> cannot even be compared to them.

But precisely the two places just mentioned, Fulda and Würzburg, were also active seminaries of the <term>Hiberno-Saxon mission</term> <note>The movement of Irish and English missionaries to continental Europe during the 7th-9th centuries, which spread Christian learning and established major libraries.</note>, and from the earliest times they maintained a close relationship with England for a long period. In Fulda, there even arose a flourishing school of calligraphy which made use of the <term>Insular script</term> <note>A medieval script system developed in Ireland and Britain, characterized by its unique "wedge-headed" letters and intricate initials.</note>;<sup>2</sup> so much so that Traube<sup>3</sup> says directly that Fulda had an "entirely insular culture." And Würzburg likewise had ancient connections with England, which even today possesses several manuscripts either originating—

# THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI CODEX

...coming from England or written in <term>insular script</term> <note>A medieval script style developed in Ireland and Anglo-Saxon England, known for its distinct, rounded letterforms.</note>. ¹ In particular, the manuscript Mp. th. qu. 2 from the Würzburg University Library deserves mention here; it was written in the 6th century in a monastery in Northern Italy, then around the year 700 it was held in a monastery in Worcester ² or its vicinity, and finally, in the second half of the 9th century, it reached the cathedral library of Würzburg. Considering, on one hand, that one of the Vercelli manuscripts—specifically the <term>Gregorian Sacramentary</term> <note>A liturgical book containing the prayers for the Mass, traditionally attributed to Pope Gregory the Great.</note> just mentioned—came from Würzburg or Fulda, and on the other hand, that Würzburg and Fulda frequently received manuscripts from England, it seems clearly possible that our Anglo-Saxon manuscript reached Vercelli not directly, but rather through the mediation of a German monastery steeped in insular culture, perhaps Fulda or Würzburg. Since the relations between Northern Italy and Germany were particularly close in the 11th and 12th centuries—the very period we previously judged to be the most likely time for the transfer of an Anglo-Saxon codex—the probability of this latter hypothesis increases even further. Indeed, one might reasonably conjecture that both of the aforementioned Vercelli codices arrived in Vercelli together and perhaps shared similar fortunes thereafter. At the very least, it is a happy coincidence that both the Würzburg codex of Cuthswith and our Anglo-Saxon manuscript point to the same region of England: the bishopric of Worcester. ³

To my knowledge, other manuscripts written in insular script include, for example, the Würzburg manuscripts Mp. th. f. 61, 62, 65, 69, 78, 79; th. q. 26 and 32 (all from the 8th century); Mp. th. q. 28*b*; and Mp. th. f. 13, 48, 66 (all from the 9th century).

## IV. - ORIGIN OF THE MANUSCRIPT

All things considered, I would not consider it unlikely that our manuscript reached Vercelli in the 11th or 12th century by way of Fulda or Würzburg. It is understood that this too is a hypothesis, but it is one supported by several points of probability, and it does not run into the same difficulties as the "Guala hypothesis" <note>A theory suggesting Cardinal Guala Bicchieri brought the manuscript from England in the 13th century; the author finds this less likely than an earlier arrival.</note>.

Finally, Paul Wülker has expressed a third hypothesis in these words: <note>The superscript "1" refers to the first footnote.</note>

> “As was reported to me in Vercelli, there existed for quite some time an ancient hospice for Anglo-Saxon pilgrims traveling to Rome. In fact, for anyone descending into Italy through the Mont Cenis pass or the Great and Little St. Bernard passes (and in the early Middle Ages, these were the routes available to an Anglo-Saxon), Vercelli lies precisely on the road to Rome. There, a small library might have existed at the hospice, and from it, the manuscript could have passed over time to the cathedral library.”

The hospice to which Wülker’s informants alluded is not, as Krapp thinks in the <term>place cited</term> <note>Latin: *loco citato* (abbreviated as *l. c.* in the text), referring to a previously mentioned work by scholar George Philip Krapp.</note>, page x, the Hospital of St. Andrew (today the Major Hospital) founded by Cardinal Guala in 1224, but rather the Hospital of St. Brigid of the Scots, <note>The superscript "2" refers to the second footnote.</note> which is said to have been founded as early as the second half of the 6th century. It is certainly documented as far back as the second half of the 12th century, and was united on August 27—

¹ *Compendium of the History of Anglo-Saxon Literature* <note>original German: *Grundriss zur Geschichte der angelsächsischen Litteratur*</note> (Leipzig, 1885), page 237; *The Vercelli Book* <note>original Latin title: *Codex Vercellensis*</note> (Leipzig, 1894), page VI; *Anglia* V, 454; XII, 629.

For such an ancient founding date of the Hospital of the Scots in the 6th century, we have, according to Bruzza, no other evidence than the indication of the Vercelli historian Cusano in his *Historical Discourses Concerning the Life and Deeds of the Bishops of Vercelli* <note>original Italian: *Discorsi istoriali concernenti la vita ed azioni de' Vescovi di Vercelli*</note> (Vercelli, 1676), page 205 and his unpublished *History of Vercelli*. — One should not confuse the Hospital of the Scots with another of the fifteen ancient hospices of Vercelli, the Hospital of the Pilgrims, known as St. James of the Cascine di Strà, which was founded by Guala’s nephew and was united with the Hospital of St. Andrew in 1557. # THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI CODEX

...joined in 1343 with the Hospital of St. Andrew. The Hospice of the Scots <note>In the Middle Ages, "Scoti" referred to the Gaelic people of Ireland and Scotland; these hospices served pilgrims from the British Isles.</note> may therefore be old enough to have served as the intermediary for our manuscript. But since I know nothing of the history of this institution and its property, I am not in a position to judge how probable Wülker’s hypothesis might be. In any case, the past existence of such a "Hospice of the Scots" seems to demonstrate that this influx of Anglo-Saxon clerics to Vercelli—or to Rome by way of Vercelli—was something far from negligible. It should be added that Vercelli is not only on the road to Rome, but also on the way to the world-famous Celtic monastery of Bobbio, which was founded by the Irishman Columbanus around 598 and maintained its relations with England for a long time. ¹

It is also worth remembering that in September 1050, a synod took place in Vercelli, ² which the English Bishop Ulf of Dorchester (1050–1052) is known to have attended to receive his consecration from the Pope. Now, the idea that Bishop Ulf himself might have brought our manuscript to Vercelli is a hypothesis that cannot be entirely excluded, especially since the language of the codex does not rule out the region of Dorchester. However, it does not seem very likely to me; based on what we know of Bishop Ulf, we cannot assume in him either a passion for literature in general, or a particular interest in...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether Bishop Ulf of Dorchester, who attended the Synod of Vercelli in 1050, might have brought the manuscript. ### IV. - THE ORIGIN OF THE MANUSCRIPT

...the ancient language of England. Indeed, not only was he found too ignorant for the episcopal office by the examining commission of Vercelli, but he was also entirely devoted politically to the French party; in this regard, it matters little whether he was—as Florence of Worcester <note>A 12th-century English chronicler.</note> claims—a Norman, or—as his name would lead us to guess¹—a Scandinavian.

Finally, I would like to point to a fifth possibility. While we previously believed we should limit the possible arrival of the manuscript in Vercelli to the 11th or 12th century, on the other hand, it must not be forgotten that during the Renaissance, the passion for ancient manuscripts was so lively that it sometimes led people to take notice of an ancient codex even if its language was unknown. According to a local tradition—which, moreover, seems quite unreliable—recorded by Neigebaur in the *Serapeum*, vol. XVIII (1857), page 184, it is conjectured that the aforementioned codex of the Gregorian Sacramentary <note>A liturgical book containing the celebrant's parts of the Mass.</note>, originally from Würzburg-Fulda, arrived in Vercelli at such a late date. “By what route this manuscript reached Vercelli, we have nothing to provide us with a clue; however, it is conjectured that some bishop from here was sent as a papal legate to Germany—such as, for example, Gianfrancesco Bonomio and Gianstefano Terrerio at the end of the 16th century—and might have brought the codex back with him.” If this local tradition were correct, it might also be possible that our Anglo-Saxon manuscript did not reach Germany until the Renaissance, and passed from there to Vercelli. But the manuscript itself offers us no argument to confirm this. In any case, let it be remembered once more that the codex certainly must have existed in the cathedral library by around 1600 (see above).

Gathering everything together now, here is what can be said regarding the question of provenance. According to the greatest probability, our Anglo-Saxon codex must have arrived in Vercelli in the 11th century

or the 12th century; but perhaps it could also have been acquired by humanist book collectors of the 16th century. In the intervening period, no one would have had any interest in an Anglo-Saxon manuscript. Its transmission can be traced either directly from England—in which case, without excluding other possible routes, the Scots hospice <note>An 'ospizio scoto' was a hostel or religious house specifically for pilgrims from the British Isles (at that time, 'Scoti' referred to both Irish and Scottish people).</note> in Vercelli might have served as a conduit—or through the intermediary of some continental monastery in contact with England, whether German, like Würzburg or Fulda, or even French, like the abbey of Luxeuil (founded by the Irishman Columbanus <note>An influential Irish missionary who founded several monasteries in Europe during the 6th and 7th centuries.</note> and in very close relationship with Bobbio) or Fleury, which enjoyed a preeminent position in the Benedictine order after the Cluniac reform. It is less likely that the codex reached Vercelli through the work of Cardinal Guala <note>Cardinal Guala Bicchieri (c. 1150–1227), a Vercellese diplomat famous for his role in English politics during the reign of King John.</note> at the beginning of the 13th century.

### V.
## **Reports and Publications of the Manuscript.**

The oldest trace of our manuscript seems to be the aforementioned entry in the book inventory of the Vercelli Cathedral, compiled in the year 1602 by Giovanni Francesco Leone, the bishop's vicar general.¹ There it bears the number 90 and the title: "Gothic or Lombard book (I cannot read it)" <note>original: "Liber Gothicus sive Longobardus, (eum legere non valeo)"</note>, which is, in all likelihood, the description used to designate our manuscript.

While this designation is not entirely free from ambiguity, it is certain that the following refers to our codex: that which the famous manuscript connoisseur, Giuseppe Bianchini of Verona, wrote in 1748 to Cardinal Carlo Vittorio delle Lancie. As this is the earliest clear mention of our manuscript, it will be appropriate to transcribe it here literally. ²

¹ Printed in G. De Gregory, *History of Vercelli Literature and Arts*, part IV (Vercelli, 1824), page 508.
² Printed in De Gregory, loc. cit., pages 556-558.
