### V.
## **REPORTS AND PUBLICATIONS OF THE MANUSCRIPT**

“Codex number 41 is most unique, because it is written in our local characters but in an unknown language, on well-preserved parchment; there is no doubt that it was written no later than the 10th century. Who knows if it might be in the *Teutonic* <note>original: 'Teotisca'. Historically, this term was used broadly to refer to Germanic languages, including Old High German and sometimes Anglo-Saxon.</note> language? I wished to spend half a day on this codex to see if I could gain some knowledge of its contents; and by great luck, the task succeeded according to my desire and the common wish of those gentlemen Canons, and specifically of Canon Fileppi... Returning then to Codex 41 in the unknown language: having turned its pages several times to see if I could understand any words, I eventually discovered that it contained a homiliary <term>homiliary: a collection of sermons or homilies arranged according to the church calendar</term> for the *circle of the year* <note>original Latin: 'anni circulum'</note>, and that the scriptural passages were reported in Latin according to our *Vulgate* <note>The standard Latin Bible used by the Roman Catholic Church.</note>, as were the titles. In one place I read: *here begins the narration of the miracles that were done before the coming of our Savior the Lord Jesus Christ.*¹ <note>original Latin: 'incipit narrare miracula, quae facta fuerunt ante adventum Salvatoris Domini nostri Jesu Christi'</note> In another: *another homily on the Day of Judgment.*² <note>original Latin: 'alia omelia de die judicii'</note> In this one I read these very clear words: *Manna Babylonia Cananea*,³ <note>The scholar is likely misreading Old English words here; 'Manna' likely refers to 'man' or 'mankind'.</note> and these passages: *Come, you who are blessed by my Father: take possession of the kingdom which has been prepared for you from the beginning of the world: depart from me, you who are cursed, into the eternal fire which is prepared for the devil and his angels.*⁴ <note>original Latin from Matthew 25:34 and 41</note> In another: *Homily for the Epiphany of the Lord.*⁵ <note>original Latin: 'omelia Epiffania Domini'</note> I will provide an excerpt of this homily, so that one may reflect on which language such a homiliary is written in. I transcribed it as best I could; and as for the characters, I repeat that I had no difficulty, because they are similar to those of other Latin codices of the tenth century.⁶

> “ *Jesus came from Galilee to the Jordan, so that he might be baptized by him.* <note>original Latin: 'Venit Jesus a Galilaea in Iordanem, ut baptizaretur ab eo'</note> The Savior came from the land of Galilee to the Jordan, that river, to John, so that he wished to be baptized there by John, who forbade *him, saying: John* <note>original Latin: 'eum dicens Ioannes'</note> hindered him and said to him: 'Behold, it is more fitting that you baptize me, and yet you come to me?' *Jesus answered and said* <note>original Latin: 'respondit Jesus et dixit'</note> to Peter, etc. ” <note>This translation represents the scholar's first, somewhat flawed attempt to interpret the Old English text.</note>

I was able to identify two other homilies in said codex, concerning the subjects addressed by the Bishop.”

## **THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK**

The first is titled: *on the Purification of Saint Mary*,¹ <note>original Latin: 'de Purificatione S. Mariae'</note> and the second: *on Saint Martin the bishop*.”² <note>original Latin: 'de Sancto Martino pontifice'</note>

It is thus clearly seen that Bianchini observed the codex quite accurately, because he quotes verbatim—though with some errors—the Latin titles of the five homilies found in the manuscript (folios 54*b*, 80*b*, 85*b*, 90*b*, 94*b*). He also points out Latin citations on two other pages (folios 84*b* and 85*b*), and even attempts to reproduce from folio 85*b* a pericope <term>pericope: a short passage or extract from a text, often used as a reading in a liturgy</term> in Old English, which—at least in De Gregory’s printed version—presents us with a horribly mangled form of Anglo-Saxon.

For the sake of science, our codex was first discovered by Doctor Friedrich Blume, a professor of law at Halle, who traveled through Italy in the years 1821–1823 in search of manuscripts regarding legal history. He collected the rich results of his travels in his four-volume work titled *Italian Journey* <note>original Latin title: 'Iter Italicum'</note> (Berlin 1824–1836). From October 27 until November 19, 1822,³ he stayed in Vercelli, and it was here that he was the first to correctly recognize our manuscript as being written in Anglo-Saxon. In the aforementioned *Italian Journey*, volume I (1824) page 99, he discusses it as follows:

> “Two books must have reached Vercelli across the Alps. The first (Codex CLXXXI) is a very beautiful Gregorian sacramentary <term>sacramentary: a book containing the prayers spoken by the priest during Mass and other rituals</term>... The other (Codex CXVII) contains legends or homilies in the Anglo-Saxon language. This is all the more noteworthy, as no other cathedral library in Italy contains anything but Latin or Italian manuscripts.”

Brief notices regarding this discovery were then published by G. H. Pertz⁴ in the *Archive of the Society for Older German History* <note>original German: 'Archiv der Gesellschaft für ältere deutsche Geschichtskunde'</note>, volume V (1824), page 585. A somewhat more extensive communication was made by Blume himself in the additions to the *Italian Jour-*

Knowing nothing of all this, the English philologist <note>A scholar of historical language and literature.</note> John Mitchell Kemble—who was educated in Germanic studies at Heidelberg, Munich, and Göttingen, and lived from 1807 to 1857—set out for Vercelli in the summer of 1834. However, he lingered so long on the journey ("some months," he says) in Germany that, in the end, the winter storms blocked his path through the Alps, and he was forced to return to England without having accomplished anything. There, he discovered that in the meantime, the *Record Commission* <note>A British government commission established to oversee the publication of historical records.</note> had already completed exactly what he had planned to do.

Maier’s copy, which must have been of very high quality and suggests he possessed a notable knowledge of Anglo-Saxon, led to the surprising discovery that the presumed "Homiliarium" <note>A collection of homilies or sermons.</note> also contained "important Anglo-Saxon poems." $^2$ Of these six poems, the—

> (see above), would not have been very suitable; he tells us so himself in the additions to his *Italian Journey* <note>original Latin: 'Iter Italicum'</note>, vol. IV (Halle, 1836), page 133:
> 
> "page 99. The Anglo-Saxon manuscript of homilies was recently, on behalf of scholars of English history, fully transcribed by Dr. Maier, now deceased; important Anglo-Saxon poems were found therein (Jacob Grimm)."

Blume, therefore, received this news through Grimm. But the idea that Grimm had "seen" Maier’s transcription, as Krapp <note>George Philip Krapp, a later editor of the manuscript.</note> suggests on page xix, seems to be an entirely unfounded opinion. According to the letter of the report just cited, Dr. Maier transcribed the *entire* codex and therefore *also the homilies*. This also agrees with the declaration of Charles Wycliffe Goodwin in *The Anglo-Saxon Version of the Life of St. Guthlac* (London, 1848, page iv), who says he owes the transcription of the final Vercelli homily on Saint Guthlac to Benjamin Thorpe. I have not succeeded in discovering where Maier’s copy is currently located. It appears from friendly communications with R. A. Roberts that it is not held in the "Record Office" in London. It likely remained among the papers left behind by Thorpe.

### V. - NOTICES AND PUBLICATIONS OF THE MANUSCRIPT

The **Record Commission** <note>A British government body established to publish historical records.</note> immediately arranged for a printing, the direction of which was entrusted to the scholar Benjamin Thorpe of Oxford (1782–1870), who also contributed to the correction of some texts. The six Vercelli poems, along with seven other Anglo-Saxon texts (mostly glosses <note>Brief marginal or interlinear explanations of words.</note>), were gathered into a volume of 165 pages, which was intended to be "Appendix B" of a report on the necessity of continuing the fourth reprint of Thomas Rymer's *Foedera* <note>original Latin: 'Foedera', meaning 'Treaties'; a major collection of historical documents</note> (first published in 1704–1735 in 20 volumes; the 4th edition of 1816–1830 was interrupted after the 3rd volume). The learned researcher of ancient legal texts Charles Purton Cooper (1793–1873), as secretary of the (second) Record Commission, proposed to bring this report to its completion.

However, as this report was never finished because the Record Commission was dissolved in 1837, the pages of "Appendix B" remained unpublished; indeed, they did not even have a title page. $^1$ Only from the so-called catchwords <note>Words printed at the bottom of a page to show the first word of the next page; here used for identification.</note>, in which is written: *Appendix B to Mr. Cooper's Report*, can one infer which work this "Appendix B"—as the first page is titled—was intended to be part of in the printing. In the absence of a true title, the work is usually cited, according to the expression in the catchwords, as *Appendix B to Mr. Cooper's Report*. Only in the year 1869 did the Master of the Rolls, Romilly <note>John Romilly, 1st Baron Romilly, who oversaw the public records in England.</note>, order the distribution of the existing copies. $^2$ These officially published copies bear on the spine of the binding, in gold lettering:

---

Kemble, in his *Codex Vercellensis* <note>original Latin: 'Codex Vercellensis', referring to the Vercelli Book</note>, page v, states that he himself already knew of the existence of the poems before setting out on his journey, but regarding such secondary circumstances, his memory is not always to be unconditionally trusted.

$^1$ That such was the original design is inferred from the fact that the current first printed sheet is marked *BB*; and at that time it was customary, as is still occasionally done in England today, to mark the title page with *A*.

$^2$ Wülker, *Anglia*, vol. V (1882), page 453, asserts that the *Appendix* was printed in only 250 copies. This explains why it is difficult, at least in Germany, to obtain it. I have used the copy from the Royal Library of Berlin. — When it was printed cannot be established, as it lacks a title page; but it cannot be later than 1835 or 1836. See note 99. Regarding the distribution of the copies in the year 1869, light is shed by the following notice which (according to a friendly communication from Alfred Pollard) is attached to the copy in the British Museum: "This volume contains a portion of the Appendices to a Report on Rymer's *Foedera* intended to..."

### THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK

The inscription reads: *Report on Foedera <note>Latin for "Treaties"; referring to a major collection of historical documents.</note>. App. B.* The work, as we possess it today, contains—without any other addition—the following poems printed according to the order they appear in the Vercelli manuscript: "The Legend of St. Andrew" <note>Now commonly known as *Andreas*.</note> on pages 47–89; "The Fates of the Twelve Apostles" on pages 90–92; "The Departed Soul's Address to the Body" on pages 93–97; "A Fragment, Moral and Religious" <note>Now known as *Homiletic Fragment I*.</note> on pages 98–99; "The Holy Rood, a Dream" <note>Now famously known as *The Dream of the Rood*.</note> on pages 100–104; and "The Invention of the Cross" <note>Now known as *Elene*.</note> on pages 105–138. To provide an idea of the handwriting in the codex <note>A codex is an ancient manuscript in book form.</note>, full-sized facsimiles of two pages are added to the volume, specifically folio 43 *a* and 75 *b*, as well as the initial letter H from folio 49 *a*.

Although the public distribution of the work did not occur until the year 1869, a few copies were given as personal gifts to learned individuals, both English and German, immediately after the printing was finished. Among the former, due to his friendship with Cooper, was J. M. Kemble,¹ who made use of it in the appendix and glossary of his version of *Beowulf*;² among the latter was Dr. J. M. Lappenberg, who likely owed his copy to his friendship with Benjamin Thorpe, who had already set to work in the year 1834...

> ...have been made to the late Commissioners on Public Records by Mr. Charles Purton Cooper, their Secretary. — As these Appendices have been in store since the year 1837, when the Record Commission expired, and the Report was not made, I have directed the Appendices, although imperfect, to be distributed in such a manner as may render them most useful for literary and historical purposes. — This volume is therefore transmitted to the British Museum Printed Book Department. (signed) Romilly, M. R., Public Record Office, 29 May 1869.

¹ On this matter, Kemble wrote in *Codex Vercellensis*, vol. I, page v: "Certain circumstances prevented the publication of the book, but some copies reached the hands of persons studying such matters, both here and in Germany. One of these was placed at my disposal (by the courtesy of Mr. Cooper), and it was of great help to me during the preparation of the second volume of *Beowulf*; but in general, the contents remained inaccessible and unknown."

² The preface is dated: "Munich, Nov. 1835." If we could be certain that those frequent references to the Vercelli Codex were not added only during the printing process, we would have proof that *Appendix B* was already printed in the year 1835. However, Kemble's work presents several other chronological difficulties.

### V. — REPORTS AND PUBLICATIONS OF THE MANUSCRIPT

to the English translation of his *History of England*.¹ <note>Original: *Geschichte von England*.</note> Toward the beginning of 1839, Lappenberg lent his copy to Jacob Grimm, who immediately made a copy of the poems. In that same year, Grimm published an edition of the two longest and most important poems, "Andreas" and "Elene," which featured an excellent preface and was released in Cassel in 1840.² Since that "Appendix B" was technically a private printing, Jacob Grimm deserves the credit for being the first to bring the Vercelli codex <note>A "codex" is an ancient manuscript in book form.</note> to the general attention of scholars.

The German edition was followed a few years later by the first English edition, edited by J. M. Kemble, a disciple and friend of Grimm. This work comprised volumes 5 and 6 of the then-newly founded "Ælfric Society." Part I (1843) contained "Andreas"; Part II (1856) contained "Elene" and the minor poems.³ Although this edition is not scientifically superior to Grimm’s edition regarding the two major poems,⁴ it nonetheless holds the merit of being the first to make the minor poems of the Vercelli codex accessible to the public. We have a complete edition of all the poems in the *Library of Anglo-Saxon Poetry* <note>Original: *Bibliothek der angelsächsischen Poesie*.</note> (Göttingen, 1857–1858) by C. W. M. Grein, which was revised by Richard Paul Wülker between 1883 and 1897. For partial editions of individual poems, see section VI.

Because the first editors—Thorpe, Grimm, Kemble, and Grein—had not personally seen the manuscript, and the entire reproduction of the text was therefore based on Maier’s transcription, the need for a new collation <note>A "collation" is a critical, line-by-line comparison of a transcript against the original manuscript to ensure accuracy.</note> of the codex was increasingly felt. Taking charge of this was—

During Easter of 1887, Federico Kluge¹ prepared a collation of the "Vision of the Cross" <note>Now more commonly known to students of English literature as "The Dream of the Rood."</note> for his *Anglo-Saxon Reader* <note>Original: *Angelsächsisches Lesebuch*.</note>, 1888, and also of the first 1,497 verses of "Andreas," a collation which Wülker also consulted.² Finally, Arthur Napier in the summer of 1888 undertook a new collation of the Vercelli poems (*Journal for German Antiquity* <note>Original: *Zeitschrift für deutsches Altertum*.</note>, vol. XXXIII, pp. 66-73), in which work he discovered the conclusion of the "Fates of the Apostles" <note>Original: *Fata Apostolorum*.</note> (28 verses), which had remained unnoticed until then.

The Anglo-Saxon prose sermons, which occupy nearly three-quarters of the manuscript, have waited until today to be published. Only two have been edited so far. Wülker printed the short Homily XIII in the journal *Anglia*, vol. V (1882), pp. 464-465; and Paul Gonser published the final fragmentary homily in his edition of the *Anglo-Saxon Prose Life of Saint Guthlac* <note>Original: *Angelsächsisches Prosaleben des heil. Guthlac*.</note> (Heidelberg, 1909). Even earlier, Ch. W. Goodwin had collected the variants of the Life of Saint Guthlac from the Vercelli manuscript in his work *The Anglo-Saxon Version of the Life of St. Guthlac* (London 1848). Goodwin made use of a transcription of the Vercelli fragment for this purpose, which he owed to the kindness of Benjamin Thorpe;³ that is to say, Thorpe, who had never seen the manuscript himself, gave him the transcription by Maier, or a copy made from it. From this fact, we are certain that Dr. Maier—as the comments of Grimm in Blume's work already led us to suppose—transcribed not only the poems but the entirety of our codex. A copy of all the sermons was later made by Napier in 1888, and by myself in 1912 based on...

Words from these homilies that had not yet been documented were identified by Napier in his *Contributions to Old English Lexicography* (*The Philological Society's Transactions*, 1906, pp. 265-358), after some samples had already been offered by Friedrich Kluge in the glossary of his *Anglo-Saxon Reader* <note>Original German: *Angelsächsisches Lesebuch*</note> (1st ed. 1888). In the autumn of 1888, the 83 pages of the manuscript containing the poems were photographed at half-size by Dr. Ludwig Lange of Elberfeld for a photographic reproduction, which followed in 1894 under the direction of Wülker.<gloss>1</gloss> The homilies are reproduced for the first time in the present facsimile, and thus are finally made accessible for scholarly research.

## VI.

### Contents of the Manuscript.

The manuscript contains six poems and 23 prose sermons. As will be more clearly seen from the overview provided later, the manuscript was assembled for an entirely <term>parenetic: intended to advise, urge, or offer moral encouragement</term> and liturgical purpose. In fact, the six poems not only belong to the religious genre, but also all deal with well-known sermon themes. They were all written—except perhaps for the fragment on Psalm 27:3 (folios 104a-104b) for which, due to its brevity, this cannot be directly proven—either for specific church solemnities, namely the feast of St. Andrew ("Andreas"), the feast of the Apostles ("The Fates of the Apostles"), and the Exaltation of the Cross ("Elene" and "The Vision of the Cross" <note>Commonly known to modern readers as "The Dream of the Rood."</note>), or for specific types of preaching, such as the "Address of the Soul to the Body" which belongs to the genre of Lenten sermons. But—

---

<note>¹ *Vercelli Codex: The Anglo-Saxon Manuscript at Vercelli in a Faithful Reproduction* <note>Original German: *Codex Vercellensis. Die Angelsächsische Handschrift zu Vercelli in getreuer Nachbildung*</note>, edited by Dr. Richard Wülker, Leipzig, Veit and Co. Bookstore, 1894.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the bibliographic history of the Vercelli Book and introduces its contents, noting that the manuscript was compiled for moral instruction and liturgical use.</summary>
<keywords>Vercelli Book, Arthur Napier, Friedrich Kluge, Max Förster, homilies, Old English poetry, Andreas, The Fates of the Apostles, Elene, The Dream of the Rood, Soul and Body, Lenten sermons</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the contents of the Vercelli Book, following the introductory remarks on the manuscript’s liturgical and moral purpose. The previous page ended while describing the poetic contents; this page transitions into a detailed description of the specific prose sermons (homilies) found in the codex.</meta>

->THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK<-

it should be noted that in the arrangement of the themes, no systematic order has been followed—for example, the cycle of the liturgical year—and that therefore the collection was probably formed little by little without a pre-established plan.

1. **f. 1a-9a**: First sermon.
It is a Passion sermon, which overall results from a version of St. John 18:11–19:42, with short intercalations and exegetical <term>exegetical: related to the critical explanation or interpretation of a religious text</term> integrations derived from the synoptic Gospels <note>The "synoptics" refers to the Gospels of Matthew, Mark, and Luke, which share a similar view of the life of Christ</note>. Since the first two pages are illegible, the beginning is missing for us.

2. **f. 9a-12b**: Second sermon.
A short homily on the terrors of the Last Judgment, which is found once more in our manuscript. In fact, setting aside the conclusion, we find exactly the same text—although in a more recent form, slightly abbreviated and much less rich in English elements—at folios 112a-116a (see No. 26), where it constitutes the second part of the twenty-first homily. However, the conclusion (starting from "as he himself said" <note>original: *swa sylfa cwaedh*</note> at f. 11b line 2 and from "let us not despair" <note>original: *uto[n] ne georwenan*</note> at f. 116a line 20) is entirely different in both texts. In a form that is sometimes quite different—meaning that it matches our Vercelli text sometimes word-for-word, and other times only loosely—this same sermon is found in the Anglo-Saxon collection of sermons that circulates under the name of Wulfstan, Bishop of Worcester (1003-1023), marked as No. 40 (edited by Napier, Berlin, 1883, pp. 182-189); in that version, the beginning and the end sound entirely different, but from page 182 line 10 to page 187 line 15, the text is identical. Noteworthy, although hitherto unobserved, is the fact that a part of the sermon (in Napier, page 186 line 3 to page 186 line 19) is in verse, which probably results from the <term>homilist: a person who writes or delivers a sermon</term> having inserted into his speech a fragment of poetry composed earlier. This is evidently the case with the Old English poem *On the Day of Judgment* <note>original: *Be domes daege*</note> included by Wulfstan (edited by Napier, page 136 line 25 to page 140 line 2). But even elsewhere, the language of the sermon has a poetic tone and momentum.

### VI. - CONTENTS OF THE MANUSCRIPT

---

Since one might conclude from an indication by Gustavo Grau in *Sources and Relationships of the Older Germanic Depictions of the Last Judgment* <note>original: "Quellen und Verwandtschaften der älteren germanischen Darstellungen des jüngsten Gerichtes"</note> (in *Studies in English Philology* <note>original: "Studien zur englischen Philologie"</note>, vol. 31, Halle, 1908) page 192, that our sermon follows the same tenor as the seventh Blickling homily <note>The Blickling Homilies are a collection of tenth-century English sermons</note> and a sermon from the Hatton manuscript, I expressly note here that this is not the case.

This sermon was printed by Max Förster, *The Vercelli Codex CXVII, along with the printing of some Old English homilies from the manuscript* <note>original: "Der Vercelli-Codex CXVII, nebst Abdruck einiger altenglischer Homilien der Handschrift"</note> (Halle, 1913) pages 87-95.

3) **f. 12b-16a**: Third sermon.
This is a sermon for the second Sunday of Lent <note>The forty-day period of fasting and reflection leading up to Easter</note> concerning confession, penance, fasting, prayer, and almsgiving.

The same text is also found in the following four manuscripts:
a) Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, Ms. 198 (formerly S. 8) from about the year 1000 (originating from Worcester) f. 132b (Wanley, *Catalogue* <note>original: "Catalogus"</note>, 1705, page 126); b) Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, Ms. 162 (formerly S. 5), 11th century, page 243 onwards (Wanley, page 118); c) Oxford, Bodleian Library 340 (formerly NE. F. 4. 10), 11th century, f. 108a (Wanley, page 12); d) Oxford, Bodleian Library Ms. 343 (formerly NE. F. 4. 12) approximately 1150-1170, f. 56b-58b with the title "Sunday in Lent" <note>original: "Dominica in quadragesima"</note> (Wanley, page 18).

The sermon contained in Ms. 343 of the Bodleian has been printed by A. O. Belfour, *Twelfth-Century Homilies in Ms. Bodl. 343* (Early English Text Society, Original Series, No. 137, London, 1909), pages 40-48.

4) **f. 16b-24b**: Fourth sermon.
A sermon of penance in consideration of the Last Judgment, featuring a long dialogue between the soul and the body. On this latter theme, see Brandl, *Old English Literature* <note>original: "Altenglische Literatur"</note>, page 1096 and Louise Dudley, *An Early Homily on the "Body and Soul" Theme* in *Journal of English and Germanic Philology*, vol. VIII (1909), pages 225-253 and

*The Egyptian Element in the Legends of the Body and Soul* (Baltimore, 1911).

The same sermon is found in manuscript 41 of Corpus Christi College, Cambridge (formerly S. 2) in the margins from pages 254 to 280; a portion of this, namely the dialogue between the good soul and its body, was published by Julius Zupitza in the *Archive for the Study of Modern Languages* <note>original: "Archiv für das Studium der neueren Sprachen"</note>, vol. XCI (1893), pages 379-381. Thorpe, *Ancient Laws and Institutes of England* (London, 1849), vol. II, page 394 onwards. Another passage conforms to a portion of Homily XXIX by the pseudo-Wulfstan <note>A term for works once incorrectly attributed to Wulfstan, the Archbishop of York</note> (edited by Napier, p. 140, lines 3-8, corresponding to our codex at folio 17b).

After the end of the homily on the half of folio 24b that remained blank, a 13th-century scribe inserted a verse from a psalm (26:9, in an altered form) with neumes <note>A system of medieval musical notation used before the invention of the five-line staff</note>:

> ℟. Be my helper, O Lord; do not forsake me, O God of my salvation. <note>original Latin: "Adiutor meus esto, Domine; ne derelinquas me, Deus salutaris meus."</note>

5) **f. 25a-29a:** Fifth sermon with the title: "Midwinter. Show us, O Lord" <note>original Anglo-Saxon/Latin: "To middan wintra. Ostende nobis, Domine"</note>.

It is a homily for the first day of Christmas that simply explains the Gospel text of the first Mass of Christmas Eve: Luke 2:1-14. The same sermon is found: *a*) in Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, Ms. 198 (formerly S. 8) folio 1 onwards, with the title: "Sermon on the Birth of the Lord" <note>original Latin: "Sermo in Natale Domini"</note> (Wanley, page 125) and *b*) in Bodleian Ms. 340 (formerly NE. F. 4. 10), folio 1a–5b with the title: "Sermon on the Birth of the Lord" (Wanley, page 9).

6) **f. 29b-52b:** First poem of the Vercelli Book ("Andreas").

It is a poem, designated by the title "Andreas," which in 1,722 alliterative verses <note>The standard form of Old English poetry, organized by repeating initial consonant sounds rather than rhyme</note> narrates the sufferings and miracles of the apostles Matthew and Andrew on the island of the cannibals, "Myrmidonia." The work, which some critics—though wrongly—attribute to the poet Cynewulf, has been,

# VI. - CONTENTS OF THE MANUSCRIPT

<meta>The text describes the origins and physical state of the Old English poem "Andreas."</meta>

In all probability, [the poem] was composed in the second half of the 8th century, originally in an Anglian dialect, though in our codex it appears in West Saxon. As a source, the poet had before him a Latin version of the *Acts of Saint Andrew and Matthew* <note>original: "Acta S. Andreae et Matthaei"</note> (edited by Max Bonnet, *Apocryphal Acts of the Apostles* <note>original: "Acta apostolorum apocrypha"</note>, Leipzig, 1898, p. 66 ff.), and not, as was first thought, the Greek text of these acts.

The poem is complete in our text; however, between folio 42b and 43a, one can sense from the verse structure, the grammar, and the context that there is a gap <note>a "lacuna" or missing section</note>, the size of which—calculated with the help of the source—demonstrates that a leaf must have been lost between folios 42 and 43. The poem has been printed by Benjamin Thorpe (1836), Jacob Grimm (1840), Kemble (1843), Grein (1858), Wülker (1888), and Baskerville (1885); these editions are all now superseded by the edition, enriched with an excellent introduction and commentary, by George Philip Krapp, *Andreas and the Fates of the Apostles* (Albion Series, Boston, 1906). A half-size facsimile of the entire poem was provided by Wülker in *The Vercelli Codex* <note>original: "Codex Vercellensis"</note> (Leipzig, 1894); a full-size page of the original (f. 43a) appeared as a traced facsimile in *Appendix B*, where the large initial on folio 49a is also reproduced. The hand-drawn facsimile of folio 43a is also repeated by Grimm.

See the literature on this poem in Wülker, *Outline of Anglo-Saxon Literature* <note>original: "Grundriss der angelsächsischen Literatur"</note>, p. 187; Brandl, *Old English Literature* <note>original: "Altenglische Literatur"</note> p. 140; and Krapp p. LXXIII ff.; furthermore, Grau, *Sources* <note>original: "Quellen"</note> p. 131–145 and Carl Richter, *Chronological Studies on Anglo-Saxon Literature* <note>original: "Chronologische Studien zur angelsächsischen Literatur"</note> (in Morsbach’s "Studies in English Philology," vol. 33, Halle, 1910), pages 37-40 and page 86.

---

7) **f. 52b-54a**: Second poem ("The Fates of the Apostles" <note>original: "Inno agli Apostoli" or "Hymn to the Apostles"</note>).
This is a poem of 122 alliterative verses which declares the place and circumstances of the martyrdom of each of the twelve Apostles; it then addresses a prayer for help to their entire blessed company, ending by giving the name of the poet in a runic acrostic: "Cynwulf" (sic!). Many attempts have been made to determine the purpose and character of—

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Vercelli Book's poetic contents, specifically focusing on "The Fates of the Apostles." The author argues for its status as an independent work inspired by Latin liturgical traditions, rather than a mere appendix to the previous poem, "Andreas."</meta>

->THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK<-

this poem: I believe I can state ¹ that it is primarily an Anglo-Saxon imitation of a Latin hymn to the Apostles <note>original: "Hymnus de apostolis"</note>. In fact, such hymns existed for the feast of the twelve Apostles—a celebration introduced to the West as early as the 7th century and observed either on the first of May or the 29th of June. Some of these remain in Latin with accounts of the martyrdoms of individual Apostles very similar [to our poem]; see, for example, Mone, *Latin Hymns of the Middle Ages* <note>original: "Lateinische Hymnen des Mittelalters"</note>, Vol. III (1855), Nos. 666-669; cf. *Analecta Hymnica Medii Aevi*, Vol. LI (1908), p. 121 ff. Since I view this poem as a proper "Hymn of the Apostles" in its own right, I cannot agree with the opinion of those critics ² who judge our verses to be nothing more than the conclusion to the preceding poem. ³

So far, research to track down the direct source of the poem has proven unfruitful, as none of the Latin lists of Apostles known thus far, ⁴ nor even the martyrology of Bede—the only one that can be taken into account for such a remote period—perfectly align with Cynewulf’s descriptions. Perhaps it is even unnecessary to search for a specific source, because the circumstances of the Apostles’ martyrdoms were undoubtedly so well known to medieval clerics—and Cynewulf must certainly have possessed a clerical education—that they could easily have composed such a brief summary from memory as our poem provides. Anyone wishing

² I cannot agree in any way even with the most recent supporter of this hypothesis, namely Gustav Grau, *Sources* <note>original: "Quellen"</note>, pp. 131-145, especially since everything he calls "sources" are nothing more than distant echoes and parallels. As for his opinion that medieval readers actually required a mention of the martyrdom of St. Andrew, I must point out that the English poet's Greco-Latin source, the *Acts of St. Andrew and Matthew* <note>original: "Acta S. Andreae et Matthaei"</note>, also says nothing of the Saint's death.
³ Nothing can be deduced for or against this opinion from the physical form of the manuscript tradition, as the individual paragraphs of *Andreas* begin with the same large capital letters as the various independent sections of the manuscript.

to believe that Cynewulf’s statement (line 2) that he had “widely gathered” <note>Old English: *samnode wide*</note> his material is something more than a mere poetic flourish, one might also suppose that he drew the individual facts of his hymn from the so-called collection of Abdias of the *Acts of the Apostles*, which enjoyed the greatest popularity during the Middle Ages, especially in England. ^1 In any case, everything that Cynewulf reports about the Apostles in his hymn can be found, in exactly the same manner, in that version of the Abdias collection which Abbot Aelfric—to judge from his various sermons on the Apostles—had before his eyes while composing. ^2

The first 94 verses have already been published by Thorpe (1836, pages 90-92), Kemble (1856, II, 94-99), Grein (*Bibliothek*, II, 7-9), and Wülker (*Bibliothek*, II, 87-91). The closing verses containing the runic acrostic <note>An acrostic where the poet hides his name using runes, ancient Germanic letters.</note> were first discovered in 1889 by Napier, who reported them in the *Journal of German Antiquity* <note>original: "Zeitschrift für Deutsches Altertum"</note>, vol. XXXIII, pages 70-73. We have the entire poem in print for the first time in Krapp, *Andreas* (Boston, 1896), pages 69-73. A small facsimile of the entire poem was published by Wülker in the *Codex Vercellensis* (Leipzig, 1894). See the literature regarding this poem in Wülker, *Outline of Anglo-Saxon Literature* <note>original: "Grundriss der angelsächsischen Literatur"</note>, page 242 ff., Brandl, *Old English Literature* <note>original: "Altenglische Literatur"</note>, page 1043 ff., and Krapp, page LXXIII ff.; furthermore, Grau, *Sources* <note>original: "Quellen"</note>, pages 131-145 and C. Richter, *Chronological Studies* <note>original: "Chronologische Studien"</note>, pages 40, 86, 101.

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Vercelli Book’s contents, specifically focusing on the prose homilies that follow the poetic works. It provides a technical analysis of a missing section in the sixth homily and introduces the seventh.</meta>

# THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK

this apocryphon <note>original: "esso apocrifo"</note> (edited by C. von Tischendorf, *Apocryphal Gospels*, Leipzig, 1876, page 51 ff.). At folio 55v line 15 ("After King Herod the king...") <note>Old English: *Mitte-ðe Herodes se cyning*</note> begins the literal translation of the original source at chapter 17, and ends at folio 56r line 15 with chapter 25 of the same. But in the middle, as is already apparent from the sense of the Old English text, a part of the narrative has fallen out; in fact, at folio 55v the sentence is interrupted during the account of the fugitives’ encounter with the lions (Pseudo-Matthew, chapter 18); then, having skipped chapters 19-21, folio 56r picks up the thread of the discourse with the second half of chapter 22 (the entry into the Egyptian city of Sotinen). In the Old English text, something must therefore have fallen out, the extent of which we can roughly calculate. If the version in the lost part ran as literally as in chapters 17 and 18, about 100 lines must have fallen out, which is to say two leaves. But if it abbreviated the text, as we see at the end regarding chapters 22-24, only about 50 lines would be missing, that is, one leaf. In support of the first hypothesis is the often-observed fact that the translation follows the original literally at the beginning, but as it nears the end, it abbreviates more and more. Also, the excessively restricted length of the homily would suggest that it is missing two leaves rather than one. However, the following argument supports the second supposition: the break in the sense shows that at the corresponding place in the gathering <term>gathering: a group of parchment sheets folded together to form a section of a book, also called a "quire"</term>—that is, after folio 63v, which is the last of gathering *H*—something must be missing. Now, since this gathering lacks both the numbering on the first leaf (which should be VIII) and the letter (*H*) on the last, it can be deduced that the entire gathering lacks the outer bifolium <term>bifolium: a single large sheet of parchment folded in half to create two leaves (four pages)</term>, meaning one leaf at the beginning and another at the end.

The homily has been edited by Max Förster, *The Vercelli Codex 117* (1913), pages 96-100.

10) **f. 59a-61a**: Eighth sermon, marked with the number "III" on folio 59a.
It is a penitential sermon with reference to the Last Judgment, the punishments of hell, and the joys of Paradise. The following is given as the source: *Saint Gregory the holy writer, who established and wrote this writing* <note>original Old English: "Sanctus Gregorius se halega writere, se dhis gewrit sette and wrat"</note>, but I cannot say where this homily is located in the works of Saint Gregory <note>Saint Gregory the Great (c. 540–604) was a Pope whose writings were immensely popular in Anglo-Saxon England and often cited as authorities, sometimes apocryphally</note>.
The same homily in Old English is also found in two other manuscripts: *a*) Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, Ms. 198 (formerly S. 8), folio 43b with the title "First Sunday after the Epiphany of the Lord" <note>original Latin: "Dominica I. post Theophaniam Domini"</note> [Wanley, page 125] and *b*) in Oxford Ms. Bodl. 340 (formerly NE. F. 4. 10), folio 33a-35b headed "First Sunday after Epiphany and whenever you wish" <note>original Latin: "Dominica I. post Theophaniam et quando volueris"</note> [Wanley, page 10].

11) **f. 61a-65a**: Ninth sermon, designated with the number "IIII" at folio 61a.
It is a penitential sermon, which deals with the triple death <note>a medieval theological concept distinguishing between the death of the body, the death of the soul through sin, and the eternal death in hell</note> and the five theoretical symbols of hell, depicting both the terrors of hell and the joys of Paradise. The homilist refers three times to a "book" <note>original: "libro"</note> as his source (folios 63a, 63b, 65a), by which a Latin sermon is certainly indicated.
The same homily is encountered in Bodleian Ms. 340 (formerly NE. F. 4. 10), folio 35b-40b, with the heading "Second Sunday after Epiphany and whenever you wish" <note>original Latin: "Dominica II. post Theophaniam et quando volueris"</note> [Wanley, page 107]. Some passages of it share a common source with certain passages of Pseudo-Wulfstan <note>a group of homilies once wrongly attributed to Archbishop Wulfstan of York</note> (edited by Napier, page 225 line 13 - page 226 line 28; page 146, line 8 - page 148 line 7).
As appears from a comparison with the second manuscript, a leaf is missing between folio 63b and 64a; that is, the section that corresponds to Bodleian 340, folio 38b, line 12 to folio 39a, line 26.
The homily has been edited by Max Förster, *Vercelli-Codex CXVI*, pages 100-116.

12) **f. 65*a*-71*a***: Tenth sermon, marked on folio 65*a* with the number "V".
It is the same homily that was printed by Napier—based on three other manuscripts—under the title *Teaching-speech* <note>original Old English: "Larspell"</note>, as number XLIX of the sermons of the pseudo-Wulfstan <note>The "pseudo-Wulfstan" refers to a group of Old English homilies once attributed to Archbishop Wulfstan of York (d. 1023) but now recognized as the work of other authors</note> (page 250 line 15 to page 252 line 12); it is also found fragmentarily among the Blickling Homilies <note>A famous collection of Old English sermons named after Blickling Hall, where the manuscript was once kept</note> (edited by Morris, London, 1880, pages 105-107) designated as No. IX, *Christ the golden-flower* <note>original Old English: "Crist se goldbloma"</note>; however, our sermon has a different opening. Furthermore, comparing our codex with the other manuscripts, it is evident that it possesses a text that is not very good, suffering especially from frequent omissions of words and sentences.

13) **f. 71*b*-73*b***: Eleventh sermon with the heading: *Homily for the first Rogation Day* <note>original Old English: "Spel to forman gangdaege"</note>, marked with the number "VI" at folio 71*a*.
This and the two following homilies are intended for the three days of Rogation <note>Rogation Days are a period of prayer and fasting in the Christian calendar, traditionally involving processions to ask for God's blessing on the land and crops</note>, which have been observed in the West since the 6th century before the feast of the Ascension. The first sermon contains an exhortation to persevere and to earn the kingdom of heaven, taking as its text *Ecclesiastes 3:4* (*a time to weep and a time to laugh* <note>original Latin: "tempus flendi et tempus ridendi"</note>). For the history of thought, the conclusion is noteworthy, speaking of the imminence of the end of the world; therefore, the sermon must have been composed before the year 1000. It deplores not only the looting and destruction of churches and convents by "pagan peoples"—certainly alluding to the Danish invasions—but also the plundering of the clergy (mostly, it is understood, the monasteries) due to the fault of kings, bishops, and <term>aldormen</term> <note>high-ranking royal officials or provincial governors in Anglo-Saxon England</note>. The Latin biblical citations often differ from the Jerome version <note>the Vulgate, the standard Latin Bible translated by St. Jerome</note>, and consequently must have been drawn from an older Latin homilist who, like Gregory the Great for example, made use of an earlier version.

14) **f. 73*b*-75*b*.** Twelfth sermon, titled: *Homily for the second Rogation Day* <note>original Old English: "Spel tho dham odhrum gangdaege"</note>.
It is a second sermon for the Rogation Days concerning the fear of God as the beginning of wisdom (Psalm 110:10 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 111:10</note>). By way of introduction, several considerations are presented regarding the Rogation Days, which are said to have replaced pagan festivals, and during which the Cross must be carried,

On folio 74*a*, there is an interpolated passage that is out of place in this context and concerns the season of Lent <note>original Latin: "quadragesimale"</note>; it is certainly an extract from some other sermon.

15) **f. 75*b*-76*b*.** Thirteenth sermon, titled: *Sermon for the third Rogation Day* <note>original Old English: "Spel to thriddan gangdaege"</note>.

This sermon, the third among those for the Rogation Days <note>Rogation Days are days of prayer and fasting for the harvest</note>, which is the shortest in the entire collection, declares in its opening that these days of procession are a preparation for the feast of the Ascension. In a second part (f. 76*a-b*), one encounters exhortations to prepare oneself for death; these are, for the most part, placed in the mouth of the body lying in the grave. From a syntactical perspective, the part of the sentence with which folio 75*b* ends cannot be connected with the beginning of the following leaf, so that there must either be a serious corruption in the textual tradition, or a leaf or two (the middle bifolium) has fallen out between the two passages. This second hypothesis is supported by the unusual brevity of the sermon and also the jump from one theme to another.

It was reproduced, also observing the line divisions, in 1882 by Richard Paul Wülker in the journal *Anglia*, Vol. V, page 464 onward. A portion (f. 75*b*) had already been published as a facsimile in *Appendix B*.

16) **f. 76*b*-80*b*.** Fourteenth sermon, titled: *Hortatory speech for such a time as one wishes* <note>original Old English: "Larspell to swylcere tide swa man wile"</note>.

It is a parenetic <note>A parenetic sermon is one focused on moral exhortation and advice</note> sermon regarding the proper way to offer sacrifice, and also, citing the parable of the unmerciful servant (Matthew 18:23-35), regarding forgiveness and other Christian virtues.

17) **f. 80*b*-85*b*.** Fifteenth sermon with the title: *Another homily on the day of judgment* <note>original Latin: "Alia omelia de die iudicii"</note>.

-># THE ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK<-

...also of the separation of the righteous from the sinners, where the Virgin Mary, the archangel Michael, and St. Peter act as intercessors. The legend of the seven signs of Judgment is also found in two other Old English homilies, namely in the Oxford manuscript Hatton 116 (formerly Junius 24), pages 382-395 (printed by M. Förster in *Vercelli-Codex CXVII* [1913] pages 128-137 and in part by C. Hofmann in the *Munich Scholarly Post* <note>original German: "Münchener Gelehrte Anzeigen"</note>, vol. In the Vercelli sermon, the subject is presented in the form of a response from Christ to the apostle Thomas, who asks when the Antichrist will come. The revelation would therefore have come through the intermediary of the apostle Thomas, so that one would not be wrong to give it the title of the Apocalypse of St. Thomas. Now, such an origin cannot be the invention of the Anglo-Saxon homilist, who must rather have found it in his Latin original. Since in the decree attributed to Pope Gelasius (died 496) *on books to be received and not to be received* <note>original Latin: "de libris recipiendis et non recipiendis"</note> (edited by von Dobschütz, *The Gelasian Decree* [*Texts and Investigations* <note>original German: "Texte u. Untersuchungen"</note>, 38, 3, Leipzig, 1912], page 12), one encounters a *Revelation which is called the apocryphal Thomas* <note>original Latin: "Revelatio quae appellatur Thomae apocrypha"</note>, it must be supposed that the common Latin source for the aforementioned Anglo-Saxon homilies is precisely this Apocalypse of St. Thomas. In effect, as I have learned from the kind information of Erwin Preuschen, two Latin texts have recently been printed which represent this long-sought Apocalypse under two different recensions, namely one: *Letter of our Lord Jesus Christ to Thomas his disciple* <note>original Latin: "Epistula domini nostri Jesu Christi ad Thomam discipulum suum"</note> of a more extensive (or interpolated?) redaction published by Friedrich Wilhelm in *German Legends and Legendaries* <note>original German: "Deutsche Legenden und Legendare"</note> (Munich, 1907), pages 40*-42*; and a recension—

---

—with which Grau would like to explain this conformity of the homilies, I cannot approve of it, any more than many of his other opinions presented in this book—diligent, yes, and attractive, but lacking in exact method, and often obscure, imprecise, and incautious in its assertions.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Homily XV, comparing its Latin sources and versions, and begins the description of Homily XVI. It specifically discusses the relationship between the Anglo-Saxon text and the Latin recensions of the Apocalypse of Thomas.</meta>

...version edited by D. P. Bihlmeyer in the *Benedictine Review* <note>original: "Revue Bénédictine"</note>, vol. XXVIII (1911), pages 272-274. By comparing these Latin texts with our homily, one sees that it generally agrees more closely with the more extended version—for instance, it contains the entire introduction with the cries of woe—whereas in the details of the preceding signs, it aligns more closely with the shorter version. This fact can only be explained by assuming that the Anglo-Saxon author had before him a better and more primitive text than the extended version. Whether the scenes with Mary, Michael, and Peter, which follow in our manuscript, belonged to the extended version from the beginning or were interpolated <note>inserted or added later</note> only later—though the Anglo-Saxon must have already had them in his Latin source—is a question I am not in a position to resolve. Furthermore, the Old English version is mostly so literal that it can be helpful in several places for reconstructing the original Latin text. <note>1</note>

Our sermon, together with the one from the Hatton manuscript, has been printed by Max Förster, *The Vercelli-Codex CXVII* pages 116-128.

Between folio 83 and 84 there is a gap <note>a "lacuna" or missing section</note> in the text, and one might therefore suppose that after folio 83 verso a leaf has been lost.

18) **f. 85v-90v.** Sixteenth sermon with the heading: *Homily on the Epiphany of the Lord* <note>original Latin: "Omelia Epyffania Domini"</note>.

This is an Epiphany sermon concerning the baptism of Jesus (Matthew 3:13-17), which in the first part provides an extensive commentary on the gospel reading <term>pericope|A specific passage or selection from the Bible designated to be read in a church service.</term>, and in the final part deals with the mystery of the Trinity.

Between folio 85 verso and 86 recto, at least one leaf is missing, because both in terms of syntax and subject matter, the beginning of the new leaf does not connect with the end of the previous one. The missing leaf...

All three manuscripts were utilized by A. Napier for the correction of the Blickling text in his *Notes on the Blickling Homilies*, I, *St. Martin* in the American journal *Modern Philology*, vol. I (Chicago, 1903), pages 303-308. Napier has demonstrated that in the Vercelli manuscript, between folio 100 verso and 101 recto, a portion of the text is missing, which could have filled a couple of pages of the codex, and that therefore a leaf must have fallen out between folios 100 and 101.

### 23) f. 104v-106r. Fifth poem.

This is an ancient poem of 156 alliterative verses, perhaps dating back to the first half of the 8th century, and today generally titled: *The Dream of the Rood* <note>original Italian: "Visione della Croce di Cristo"</note>. In it, the wood of the Cross appears in a vision to the poet, to whom it narrates its history: the crucifixion, the deposition, the burial, the discovery of the Cross, and its current glory. All this is expressed in a language full of poetry and deep feeling, with strong references to the dominant ideas of the ancient Germanic <term>comitatus</term> <note>the traditional social bond between a lord and his loyal followers</note>.

This poem has been printed often: for the first time in 1836 by Benjamin Thorpe in *Appendix B*; excellently in Grein and Wülker’s *Library of Anglo-Saxon Poetry*, vol. II (1888), pages 116-125; and furthermore, with a good commentary, by Albert Cook, *The Dream of the Rood* (Oxford, 1905).

It is reproduced in facsimile by Wülker, *Vercelli Codex* (Leipzig, 1894). Other literature may be seen in Wülker, *Compendium*, pages 139 and 184 following, and in Brandl, *Old English Literature*, pages 1030-1032; also Grau, *Sources and Relationships* <note>original German: "Quellen und Verwandtschaften"</note>, page 175 (providing indications of alleged sources) and C. Richter, *Chronological Studies* <note>original German: "Chronologische Studien"</note>, page 74 following, 93, and 101.

24) **f. 106*b*-109*b*.** Nineteenth sermon.
It is a sermon for the Rogation Days <term>Rogation Days</term> <note>Days of prayer and fasting in the Western Church calendar, traditionally observed in spring to ask for God's blessing on the harvest.</note>, which, after a preamble on the Trinity (entirely following Augustine’s *On the Trinity* <note>original Latin: "De Trinitate"</note>) and on original sin, speaks of fasting during the three days of prayer. It mentions that this was introduced by the prophet Jonah and discusses the institution of the Rogation Days through the work of Mamertus, Bishop of Vienne <note>a 5th-century bishop in Gaul credited with starting these penitential processions</note>.
The same sermon is found in:
*a*) Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, Ms. 162 (formerly *S. 5*), page 403 and following, for the *Monday in the Greater Litany* <note>original Latin: "Feria II in Letania Maiore"</note> [Wanley, page 119];
*b*) Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, Ms. 303 (formerly *S. 17*), page 215 and following, with the title: *Sermo in Letania Maiore* <note>Sermon for the Greater Litany</note> [Wanley, page 135];
and *c*) Cotton Ms. Cleopatra B. XIII, f. 42*b* with the title: *Dominica ante Rogationum* <note>Sunday before Rogations</note> [Wanley, page 202].

25) **f. 109*b*-112*a*.** Twentieth sermon.
This is for the Rogation Days and treats almsgiving and the eight capital vices <note>These are the "deadly sins"; early medieval lists often counted eight rather than the later standard of seven.</note>. This latter part is a literal translation from the *Book on Virtues and Vices* <note>original Latin: "Liber de virtutibus et vitiis"</note>, chapters 27-34, by Pseudo-Alcuin <note>works traditionally attributed to the scholar Alcuin of York but likely written by another author</note> (Migne, *Latin Patrology* <note>original Latin: "Patrologia Latina"</note>, vol. CI, column 632 and following), who in turn transcribes the *Fifth Conference* <note>original Latin: "V collazione"</note>, chapter 16, of Cassian <note>John Cassian, whose writings on monastic life were foundational to Western spirituality</note>. The opening is the same as that of Homily XI in our codex; however, in the following sections, the two sermons proceed entirely differently, so the supposition of Wülker (*Compendium* <note>original German: "Grundriss"</note>, page 491) that they might be the same sermon appears unfounded.
Between folios 111 and 112, there is a gap in the text, as neither the sense nor the syntax of the end of 111*b* agrees with the beginning of 112*a*. It can also be seen from the source material that a passage literally translated from Pseudo-Alcuin is interrupted at f. 111. Considering now that Quire Q <term>Quire</term> <note>A quire is a "gathering" or a group of folded parchment sheets sewn together to form a section of a manuscript.</note> (f. 112-118) lacks its sequence number (*XVI*), we can surely state that the first leaf of the quire has fallen out before f. 112. As we shall see in entry No. 27, the final leaf of the quire, which should have carried the letter *Q*, is also missing. From this, it appears that the entire first double leaf of the quire is missing.
The same homily is also found in two manuscripts of Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, namely in Ms. 162 (formerly *S. 5*),

26) **f. 112a-116b. Twenty-first sermon.**
This is a homily that is not very cohesive but was certainly intended for the Lenten season <note>original Italian: "tempo quadragesimale"</note>. Without following a strict continuity of design, it treats the necessity of serving God and giving alms, without which fasting would be useless; furthermore, it discusses the "twelve virtues of the soul," the sacrifice of Christ, and pride—which caused even the angels to fall into sin—and the terrors of the Last Judgment. This last section, which spans folios 115a-116a, is found once before on folios 9a-12a of our manuscript as a separate sermon (number 2 in this list), and for this reason, one might wonder if this passage should be considered a new and independent sermon. However, we have two arguments against such a supposition: first, the scribe does not use any large initial letter, which he typically uses in this part of the codex to mark the beginning of every new work; and second, because otherwise the preceding sermon would lack any conclusion, a feature which is present in all the other homilies. Regarding the textual relationships between the two passages concerning the Last Judgment, see above under number 2, where there are also other details regarding the literature on this subject.

27) **f. 116b-120b. Twenty-second sermon.**
This is a brief extract from the two books of the *Synonyms* <note>original Latin: "Synonima"; a famous work by Isidore of Seville written as a dialogue between a man and Reason</note> by Isidore of Seville, who is cited several times as a source by the Anglo-Saxon homilist. The homilist preserves almost no trace of his author’s grammatical-stylistic tendency to show off synonyms; instead, he has very skillfully turned the edifying dialogue between Man and Reason (the *Logos*) into a short treatise on human guilt and on piety as the sole means of escape from the pains of hell. This treatise consists of maxims taken here and there, mostly translated literally and linked and coordinated together with great mastery. It is pos—

### VI. - CONTENTS OF THE MANUSCRIPT

It is possible that the Anglo-Saxon translator already had a ready-made Latin extract in front of him, as he expressly attributes the <term>parenetic</term> <note>exhortatory or moralizing advice</note> conclusion to Isidore, even though it is missing in the original.

The final letter of the <term>quire</term> <note>a gathering of parchment sheets</note> (*Q*) is missing on folio 118b, and therefore one might suppose that a leaf has been lost after this point. Grammatically and syntactically, such a gap cannot be proven because the last word of folio 118b, *Gehyradh* <note>Old English: "Hear"</note>, which is the beginning of a new proposition, connects very well with the opening phrase of the following page: *men tha leofestan* <note>Old English: "most beloved men," a common greeting in sermons</note>. However, in truth, unless one assumes a significant jump in ideas, the content does not connect well with what precedes it. It should also be noted that with folio 118, the use of the first book of the *Synonyms* ceases at paragraph 50, and on folio 119a, it begins with the start of the second book, so that more than half of the first book would have been skipped. Given this circumstance, it does not seem improbable that a leaf fell out at this point—that is, after folio 118—especially since we have confirmation in the absence of the corresponding initial leaf of the quire, as we already pointed out in entry No. 25. Therefore, we can state with some certainty that the sixteenth quire is missing the entire outer double leaf, which bore the number XVI and the letter *Q* at the bottom.

The homily was edited by Max Förster, *The Vercelli-Codex CXVII*, pages 137-148.

## II. ANGLO-SAXON VERCELLI BOOK

runic acrostic (verses 1258-1270), by a poet named "Cynewulf" who, certainly in the second half of the 8th century, wrote in the Anglian dialect; however, in our Vercelli manuscript, the text is found reworked according to more recent West Saxon forms. The poet utilized as a source a Latin version of the legend of the finding of the Cross, such as that printed under the title *Inventio S. Crucis* <note>Latin: "The Discovery of the Holy Cross"</note> or *Vita S. Quiriaci* <note>Latin: "The Life of Saint Cyriacus"</note> in the *Acta Sanctorum* <note>Latin: "Acts of the Saints," a scholarly collection of documents regarding Christian saints</note> (May 4th) and many times elsewhere, although the specific version that served as Cynewulf's model remains to be found.

This poem was printed by Benjamin Thorpe (1836), by Jacob Grimm (1840), by Kemble (1856), by Grein (1858), by Julius Zupitza (*Cynewulf's Elene*, Berlin, 1877, 4th edition 1899), by G. W. Kent (*Elene*, Boston, 1889, 2nd edition 1902), by Wülker (1888), and with excellent annotations by F. Holthausen (*Cynewulf's Elene*, Heidelberg, 1905, 2nd edition 1910). A half-size facsimile of the entire poem was published by Wülker in the *Codex Vercellensis* (Leipzig, 1894). Holthausen provided one page in a larger facsimile—though still smaller by one third than the original, a fact the editor failed to mention—in the second edition (1910). For further literature, see K. Jansen, *Cynewulf Research from its Beginnings to the Present* (Bonn, 1908), Brandl, *Old English Literature*, page 1043 following, and Holthausen in his edition, page x following; also Grau, *Sources*, pages 15-29 and Richter, *Chronological Studies*, pages 40-45, 86-88, 101.

*Heortologie* <note>A study of the history of religious festivals</note>, Freiburg, 3rd edition 1911, page 248) and therefore after Cynewulf, as also appears from the fact that the poem never gives Helena the title of saint, but only <term>eadhredhig</term> <note>Old English: "blessed" or "triumphant"</note> (verse 266), that is, *blessed*. Certainly, Cynewulf did not intend to glorify a person, but rather the discovery of the Holy Cross, which was already celebrated in the West in the 7th century as a feast day. That Cynewulf had this feast in mind is also clear from verses 1228 following, where a feast is explicitly ordered for the third of May. Now, this is the day of the feast of the *Invention of the Holy Cross* <note>original Italian: "Invenzione della S. Croce"; here "Invention" carries the archaic meaning of "discovery"</note> used in the West, while the feast of Saint Helena falls on August 18th. Therefore, the name that Thorpe (1836) had given to the poem was more appropriate, entitling it: *The Invention of the Cross*. Kemble made a sort of compromise between Thorpe and Grimm, and chose the double title: *Elene, or the Recovery of the Cross*.

<header>VI. - CONTENTS OF THE MANUSCRIPT</header>

### 29) f. 133*b*-135*b*. Twenty-third sermon (*Guthlac*).

This is a homily that recounts the temptation of St. Guthlac of Crowland (who died in 714) by two devils and his subsequent victory achieved with the help of St. Bartholomew; it was clearly intended for April 11th, the saint’s feast day. <note>His commemoration is already found in one of the oldest monastic calendars that has come down to us, namely the calendar of the West Saxon monastery New Minster in Winchester, compiled around 1050 and contained in the manuscript Cotton Titus D. XXVII (edited by R. T. Hampson, *Medieval Calendar* <note>original Latin: "Medii Aevi Calendarium"</note> [London, 1847], vol. I, p. 435 ff.; cf. on this W. de Gray Birch, *Register and Martyrology of New Minster and Hyde Abbey, Winchester* [London, 1892], p. 269 ff. and F. Piper, *The Calendars and Martyrologies of the Anglo-Saxons* <note>original German: "Die Kalendarien und Martyrologien der Angelsachsen"</note> [Berlin, 1862] p. 76). Therefore, I do not believe the opinion is correct that considers St. Guthlac as a simple "local saint of Mercia," as Brandl does in *Old English Literature* <note>original German: "Altenglische Literatur"</note>, p. 1115.</note> In reality, this is not an independent work, but rather an extract—ending with the closing prayer of praise <term>doxology: a short formula of praise to God</term> typical of sermons—from a more extensive Anglo-Saxon life of St. Guthlac, which is found complete in the London manuscript Cotton Vespasian, D. XXI (from the 11th century). This, in turn, is a free and condensed translation of the *Life of St. Guthlac* <note>original Latin: "Vita S. Guthlaci"</note>, written around 730 by a monk named Felix. Our fragment covers chapters 4 and 5 of the complete *Life*, and as for the text, it follows a more ancient version than that of the London manuscript, better reflecting the Anglian dialect <note>The dialect of the northern and central regions of Anglo-Saxon England, distinct from the Southern West-Saxon</note> of the original and remaining closer to the common primitive text. That our Vercelli fragment is a remnant of a larger composition is also evident from the phrase remaining in the first line: "in the aforementioned island" <note>original Old English: "in tham sprecenan iglande"</note>. <note>I cannot agree with the opinion of Gonser (p. 35) that "the introductory words, which should have spoken of the colonization of the island by Guthlac, have been lost." In other homilies in our manuscript, the text also begins without special introductory formulas—for example, in sermons XV and XXII. Our homilist may have simply borrowed the word *sprecenan* (mentioned) carelessly from his source material.</note>

The Anglo-Saxon version of the legend of St. Guthlac was first published by Ch. W. Goodwin, *The Anglo-Saxon*

<summary>This page describes the 23rd sermon of the Vercelli Book, which is a prose fragment detailing the life and temptations of the English hermit Saint Guthlac of Crowland. The author argues that this text is an extract from an older Anglian biography rather than a local Mercian production.</summary>

We owe a very accurate edition to Paul Gonser, *The Anglo-Saxon Prose Life of St. Guthlac* <note>original German: "Angelsächsische Prosaleben des heil. Guthlac"</note> (Heidelberg, 1909), where on pages 117-134, alongside the London text, the Vercelli fragment is also reproduced in its entirety. Further literature can be found in Wülker, *Compendium* <note>original German: "Grundriss"</note>, pages 491-493, and Brandl, *Old English Literature* <note>original German: "Altenglische Literatur"</note>, page 1114 ff.

30) **f. 136*a*-136*b*** are blank. On f. 136*b* one finds only, in a more recent hand and in capital letters, the words: WHEN HE HAD ARRIVED <note>original Latin: "CUM PERUENISSE"</note>.

Leipzig.

->Prof. MAXIMILIAN FOERSTER.<-

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the primary manuscript text, following the scholarly table of contents provided in the introduction. It is the first leaf, or folio 1 recto, of the Vercelli Book.</meta>

->1 r<-

# Homily 1
<note>original Latin: "Omelia 1"</note>

<unclear>The central portion of this page is entirely illegible due to severe environmental damage. The parchment has darkened and decayed over centuries, leaving a void where the opening lines of the first homily once stood.</unclear>

<note>This page is the tragic "front door" of the Vercelli Book. While we know from the modern header that this was intended to be the first homily—likely a reflection on the Passion of Christ or the Last Judgment—the text itself has been swallowed by time and decay. The dark, rectangular staining suggests the manuscript may have been exposed to moisture or poor storage conditions long before it was rediscovered in the Vercelli cathedral library.</note>

<summary>This folio marks the beginning of the Vercelli Book's first homily, but the page is so severely damaged and darkened that the original Anglo-Saxon text is completely lost.</summary>
<keywords>Vercelli Book, Homily 1, manuscript damage, folio 1r, illegible text</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page is folio 1 verso, the back side of the first leaf of the Vercelli Book. It follows the initial page of Homily 1, which was also found to be severely damaged and illegible.</meta>

->1 v<-

->Homily I<-
<note>original Latin: "Omelia I"</note>

**Homily** <note>original: "Omelia". A homily is a type of sermon or religious discourse intended to explain a passage of Scripture and provide practical spiritual guidance to a congregation.</note>

<unclear>The central portion of the page features a reproduction of a manuscript fragment that is heavily faded, damaged, and completely illegible. The parchment appears mottled with grey and white patches where the ink has vanished and the surface has decayed.</unclear>

<note>This page continues the unfortunate state of the manuscript's opening. Just as with the front of this leaf (folio 1 recto), the text here has been lost to the elements. The Vercelli Book is one of the "four jewels" of Anglo-Saxon literature, but its first few pages have suffered most from centuries of exposure, likely due to being on the outside of the book's gathered pages. While we can identify the section as the first homily from the Latin heading, the specific Old English words of the sermon are forever obscured on this page.</note>

<summary>This page is the verso side of the Vercelli Book's first leaf; like the recto, the primary text of the first homily is entirely lost due to severe parchment damage.</summary>

...[they] struck him. They said that he should defend himself. Then the Jews said: "It is **not lawful** <note>original Latin: "non licet"</note> nor permitted for us that we should kill any man." 

In the end, that word had to be fulfilled which the Lord Savior had spoken before: that the Jews, who by their physical birth were his own people, should deliver him to heathen men to be put to death. For Pilate and his <term>thanes</term> <note>original: "þegnas"; here used to describe Roman soldiers or officials</note> who crucified him were foreign men, Romans.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Passion of Christ, specifically the trial before Pontius Pilate. ...that one should give him up so that they were ready to take him; <unclear>then</unclear> Pilate commanded that he be scourged quickly. And they took that same [man] and fashioned a thorny crown, and set it on his head as a <term>mockery</term> <note>original: "bismringnesse"; a ritualized shaming or insult</note>. Because it is the custom of kings that they receive a crown <note>original: "beag"; literally a ring or circlet, used here for a royal crown</note> from an earthly lord, they then took him for that mockery and fashioned that sign with skill and set it on his head.

And they clothed him—our Lord Christ—around with a red garment evilly. This was because emperors were accustomed to have <term>fine-cloth</term> <note>original: "godwebbe"; a precious, often purple or scarlet dyed fabric reserved for royalty and high-ranking officials</note> garments of red. For that reason, they took him in mockery and arrayed our Lord Savior Christ with a red garment evilly, to serve as a sign. 

And they bowed down to him and greeted him through mockery and cried out: "Hail, King of the Jews" <note>original: "aue þu iadea deorum," a vernacular rendering of the Latin "Ave rex Iudaeorum"</note>; "Hail be you, King of the Jews!" And they struck our Lord on the cheek with their hands, and did many other mockeries to him, which he then very patiently suffered in his physical body; he truly suffered and endured that for us. 

Afterward, Pilate went out to the Jews and said to them: "Now you have him led out here; [standing] by this place, so that you may see that I cannot find any sin in him." Then he led our Lord Christ out before the Jews; he had the thorny crown on his head and was clothed with the red garment, and then he [Pilate] said:

### Omelia I

...to lead and to crucify him. Then they took him and led him to the place where they afterwards crucified him. And there [he carried] the cross <note>original: "licaban"; likely a scribal corruption or variation of "lædde he," meaning "he carried," referring to Jesus carrying his own cross</note>, a certain part of it, to the place where he was later to be hanged. Then after that, they crucified him on the hill <note>original: "clife"; literally "cliff" or "slope"</note> that they call **Golgotha**. And there [were] thieves on both sides of him, and they crucified our Lord Christ in the middle.

Then Pilate wrote a decree <note>original: "gewrit"; a formal legal writing</note> on a board, and it was set upon the cross, which was written thus: **Jesus the Nazarene** <note>original: "ihs nazarenus"</note>. That is, **Jesus the Nazarene, King of the Jews**. They wrote this in three languages: that was in **Hebrew**, and in **Greek**, and in **Latin**. And many Jews read that writing, because the place where he was crucified was very near the city of Jerusalem.

Then the **high priests** <note>original: "bisceopas"; the Old English text uses the word for "bishops" to describe the Jewish priestly leadership</note> said to Pilate: "Do not write that decree so that he *is* king, but write that he himself said that he was King of the Jews." Then Pilate said: "What I have written there, that shall remain written so."

Then the **soldiers** <note>original: "milite"; a direct loanword from the Latin "milites"</note>, when they had crucified Christ our Lord, divided his garments into four, to each of the four of them a share. And then there was his tunic, separately, which was undivided. This tunic was made in such a way that there was no seam in it, but it was all woven in one piece. Then they said: "Come now, let us not tear the tunic, but let us cast lots [to see] which of us shall have it." And then...

...all things were fulfilled that were written concerning his suffering. Those were good things; they willed that the holy scriptures should be fulfilled. Of all things, it was most necessary that the judgment should be fulfilled which was written earlier in the **Psalm** <note>Referring to Psalm 69:21</note> regarding that same thing: **In my thirst they gave me vinegar to drink** <note>original: "In situ meo potauerunt me," a variation of the Latin "In siti mea potaverunt me aceto"</note>. Then he bowed his head at that moment, and sent forth his spirit from his body. 

Then the Jews did not want the bodies to remain on the cross for any part of the day <note>This refers to the Jewish law that bodies should not remain on the cross overnight, especially on the Sabbath</note>, because they held that day holy. They went then forth to **Pilate** and asked him that he command the men's **shanks** <term>shanks: "scamcan," referring to the leg bones</term> to be broken on the cross, so that they might be taken down from the cross all the sooner. 

Then Pilate commanded it to be done. They ran and broke the shanks of the other **thieves** <term>thieves: "sceaþina," meaning criminals or those who do harm</term> who hung with Christ; they were both still living then. <meta>This page transitions from the narrative of Christ's burial and the Harrowing of Hell (the rescue of souls) to a sermon on the Second Coming. It warns the audience that the same Lord who was buried will return as a judge.</meta>

# MOST BELOVED MEN.

That great **Doomsday** <term>Doomsday: "dōm dæg," the final day of God's judgment</term> shall then be very terrible and awe-inspiring to all **creatures** <term>creatures: "gesceaftū," all created things, both living and inanimate</term>. On that day, bloody tears shall flow from the face <note>original: "nas," likely referring to the nose or face</note> as a blood-mingled sweat. Those who are now here in many a sin and in the useless hiding of gold and silver and **fine purple cloth** <term>fine purple cloth: "gōd-webbe," a term for precious, high-quality fabric often used in churches or by royalty</term> and [owners of] fields of grain <note>original: "þrosgefequona æceras," likely a corrupt transcription of 'þrosm' (vapor/smoke) or 'þreosinga' (shining), but 'æceras' clearly means fields</note>, it will be most like... as if it does not come to our use. On that day the light of the sun shall vanish, and the light of the moon, and the light of all the **stars** <term>stars: "tungla," a general term for heavenly bodies</term>. On that day the **Lord’s cross** <term>the Lord’s cross: "drihtnes rōd," often personified in Old English poetry and prose as a witness to the Passion</term> shall be seen flowing with blood among the clouds.

On that day the Lord’s **countenance** <term>countenance: "on-sȳn," face or appearance</term> will be very terrible and awe-inspiring, appearing in the form <note>original: "hīpe," likely a variation of 'hīwe' meaning appearance or color</note> in which those who previously scourged him with whips and struck him and spat their spit upon him [once saw him]. On that day all the men <term>men: "rincas," a poetic term often meaning warriors or noblemen</term> shall lament and weep, because they previously would not atone for their sins, but they there [find] no profit and fall into this [judgment]. On that day the **trumpets** <term>trumpets: "bȳman," the horns blown by angels to wake the dead</term> shall be blowing from the four corners of this **Middle-earth** <term>Middle-earth: "middangeard," the world inhabited by humans, situated between heaven and hell</term>, and then all shall arise, whatever was ever parched or consumed by fire, or whatever wild beasts ate, or birds carried away; all on that day shall arise.

On that day our Lord comes with great **majesty** <term>majesty: "mægen-þrymme," literally 'mighty-glory'</term> and reveals his countenance and his body. Then the weight <note>original: "pund," referring to a measure or a weight of judgment</note> shall be ripe for the sinful, and those who were **steadfast in the cross** <note>original: "rod-fæstan," likely referring to the faithful who remained 'fast' to the cross, though it can also mean 'crucified'</note> shall have all their harvest. And then the wretched <note>original: "ear," likely a shorthand for 'earman'</note> may see him whom they previously slew and hanged; and the cross-firm judgment then judges each of all men according to [their deeds]...
