--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the sermon on the Last Judgment, transitioning from the cosmic signs of the previous page to the grim physical reality of death and the moral preparation required to escape the torments of hell. The preacher emphasizes the transience of the body and the necessity of Christian charity.</meta>

### Homily 11

The old earth has decayed. That frail body where it rots is food for the lower creatures, for the crawling worms <note>original: "sylf-licndan ƿyrmū", literally "self-moving" or creeping worms, a common memento mori image in Old English literature</note>. Truly, then that happens swiftly: the **Great Separation** <term>micle gedal: the parting of the soul from the body at the moment of death</term>. 

If then the sinner is found in darkness—that is, the soul that is parched of good—having neglected God’s commands, then after that separation they must go to the eternal punishments of hell. There they shall dwell with devils in nothingness and in crime, in evil and in misery, and among worms, according to the deeds they performed in the world and in life. They shall dwell in the bursting and in the foulness and in all those woes which the devils prepared; through the power they once had, they prepared these torments for themselves. 

But let us be mindful of our souls, so that God may not turn from us on that day. Let us forsake murder and crime and greed, nor let us possess pride, unrighteousness, or illicit lusts <note>original: "unriht hæmedas", referring to adultery or any sexual practice outside of church law</note>. Let us avoid envy, ale-drunkenness, and gluttony, greed, and thievery; avoid lies and hypocrisy, slander and evil speech, hatred and anger, and all those wicked things that the devils themselves follow. 

But let us love our Lord with all our mind and might, and with all our heart and thought, and with all truth and wisdom. Let us love our **neighbors** <note>original: "ne hstan", literally those who stand nigh/near</note> just as we love ourselves. Let us show mercy to poor men and to strangers and to the sick, so that our Lord may likewise be merciful to us. And though any of us should be...

<summary>This page describes the decay of the physical body and the subsequent "Great Separation" of soul and body, warning that those who neglect God's commands face eternal torment in a foul hell. It concludes with an exhortation to reject various sins and embrace the love of God and neighbor through acts of charity.</summary>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on moral preparation for the afterlife. Having described the decay of the body and the threat of hell on the previous page, the preacher now focuses on the remedy: forgiveness, humility, and adherence to the "laws of the Gospel" rather than worldly vanity.</meta>

...whoever has sinned in word or in deed, let him honor the love which God teaches him and let him be corrected <note>original: "witnige", meaning to be disciplined or reproved for the purpose of correction</note>, just as the prophet said: **Forgive, and you shall be forgiven** <note>original Latin: "Dimittite et dimittetur vobis", a quote from Luke 6:37</note>. He turns toward us, for the sake of our love for those who have sinned against us, all that we have done rightly. 

Listen! In this world, it behoves us to remember how great the kingdom of heaven was promised after He received a human body for the eternal salvation of mankind, and for our sake against the oppression of the devils' servitude <term>deofla þeowdome: the state of being enslaved to sin and the devil before Christ's redemption</term>. And both of us <note>likely referring to the soul and the body</note> might reach that gift of eternal life, unless we are utterly undone by those things we wish to prepare <note>original: "gebyrman", likely meaning to ferment or leaven, here used metaphorically for brewing or preparing one's own fate</note>. 

As the prophet said: The Lord then heals and holds in the kingdom of heaven those whose lives in this world are humble toward God and men. Listen! One hears that they are blessed and happy before God, those who in the world are humble and gentle. Truly, they may intercede through those three things <note>likely referring to the Christian virtues of faith, hope, and charity, or the acts of fasting, prayer, and almsgiving</note>; but they are wretched and guilty before God, those who here in the world are proud and cursed. 

Just as all those disciples who dwell with God... because a man can first [be saved], but then afterwards run into hell, unless he seeks through mercy and humility to be led away from the devils' servitude. Lo, it behoves us to begin our laws, following the laws of the Gospel which are often read before us and which our teachers command and teach. Listen! We now in idle boasting adorn and glaze ourselves with gold and gems; but be humble so that we never...

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page concludes the first homily and begins the second. It continues the exhortation to prioritize the soul's eternal needs over worldly concerns, moving from a warning about death to a vision of the eternal life that awaits the righteous.</meta>

...must leave it <note>Referring to the physical world or the earthly body mentioned on the previous page</note>. And we must yield much to our Lord on high, being mindful of the needs of our souls, which must exist in eternity after this life, both with soul and with body, in whichever state we now earn for ourselves here. Let us have good repentance for our sins; even if it happens <term>þ̄</term> <note>abbreviation for "þæt" (that)</note> that any of us offends another in word or in deed, then <term>þōn</term> <note>abbreviation for "þonne" (then)</note> a remedy <note>original: "bot," meaning compensation, penance, or a spiritual "fixing" of a wrong</note> is best. For a person is never so greatly sinful that a remedy is not always allowed to them. 

Therefore, let us now hasten to God before death seizes us, for it approaches us very closely. And let us be wise, and steadfast, and kind-hearted, and generous, and righteous, and eager to give alms, and pure-hearted, and peaceful, and God-fearing, and eager to learn, and stable, and obedient to God and to our lords, and observant of God's will. Then, as a reward, He shall prepare that eternal life for those of you who have shown these virtues. Let us now hasten toward that while we may still control our honors. 

There, they shall never experience the parting of leaves <note>original: "leaf-gedal," a poetic metaphor for death or the final separation of soul and body</note> nor hateful strife; there, day never comes after day, nor night after night, but there is eternal light, and bliss, and eternal glory, and eternal prosperity with our Lord, the Redeemer of the world. That is indeed the same God who lives and reigns with the Father and with the Son and with the Holy Spirit, to whom is glory and honor through all worlds of worlds, forever without end. Amen.

<meta>The previous page concluded Homily II with a doxology praising the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. **B**rothers, most beloved, I declare that there are three things first in the world that are necessary for every person to have: first <note>original: ".i." (id est), used here as a list marker for "firstly"</note>, right faith; the second is hope; the third, good love. In that faith, it is that he believes in God the Father Almighty, and in the Son, and the Holy Ghost, and in the divided Trinity <note>This refers to the three distinct Persons of the Godhead</note>, and in the enduring Unity <note>This refers to the single divine Essence</note>. Then hope is that he worthily hopes for the eternal rewards. Then good love is that he be filled with divine love and [the love of] his neighbor. For we are all, in baptism, consecrated as children of God to the end that we may truly be brothers in unity <note>original: "an-sund," meaning whole, healthy, or uncorrupted</note>; let us love with that good love according to God, for we must always dwell in divine love and that of our neighbors, so that He may always dwell within us. For as John <note>original: "iohann’"</note> said: "God is true love, and he who dwells in true love, he dwells in God and God dwells in him." <note>A translation of 1 John 4:16</note>

My brothers, six things are necessary to have for holy Christian piety <note>original: "æfæstnysse," referring to the legal or formal practice of religion</note>, which are for everyone in these holy days <note>Likely referring to a specific penitential season such as Lent</note>. These are: first, confession; second is repentance; third is watchfulness <note>original: "ƿæcce," referring to keeping spiritual vigils</note>; fourth is fasting; the fifth thing is prayer; the sixth is alms-giving. Confession is to be made concerning all the sins that a person ever committed, whether in deed <note>original: "an ge-þohte," literally "in thought," but often used to denote the commission of a planned act</note>, or in speech, or in mind. Truly, concerning all these chief teachings <note>original: "heafod-læreƿas," literally "head-teachers," but here referring to primary doctrines or essential instructions</note>, without some of them no man may be saved without confession <note>original: "un-scrift-lice," referring to the state of being unshriven or without penance</note>. <warning>The bottom few lines of the manuscript text are somewhat faded and the ink is lighter.</warning>

It is thus read in the beginning, even as <term>Isidore</term> <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), an influential scholar whose writings were essential to the medieval Church.</note> said:
through prayers one is cleansed, and through readings the brothers are <term>edified</term> <note>original: "intimbred"; literally 'built up' or 'constructed,' as in building a house of faith.</note>. Truly, it is to be done so that continual prayer profits much with God; just as <term>Paul</term> <note>The Apostle Paul; the text refers to the sentiment found in James 5:16, often attributed broadly to apostolic teaching.</note> the apostle said, that the prayer of the righteous profits much before God. Truly, Moses prayed and he turned away God’s anger from the people of Israel when they prayed to an <term>idol</term> <note>original: "deowolgylde"; literally 'devil-tribute' or 'devil-worship.'</note> and forsook God.
Elijah the prophet prayed and it did not rain for three years; and he with his prayers took and locked heaven for three years and six months. And again he prayed so that the heaven gave rains and the earth her fruits. Jonah prayed in the whale's belly, and he was released from there. Daniel prayed in the lions' pit and was <term>armed</term> <note>original: "wæpnode"; suggests that prayer served as his spiritual armor or protection.</note> to be heard. King Hezekiah prayed in his <term>sickness</term> <note>original: "uncumneſſe"; refers to his mortal illness described in the Book of Kings.</note> and God added fifteen years to his life. Truly, whoever wishes to be speaking with God, he must frequently pray and read; because when we pray, then we speak with God, and when we read, then God speaks with us. My dearest brothers, I here bring after this an <term>exhortation</term> <note>original: "manung"; a formal spiritual instruction or admonition.</note> concerning almsgiving, which is the <term>richness</term> <note>original: "ryſelnes"; literally 'fatness,' signifying the best or most fertile part of a spiritual life.</note> and the purity of all good works. Concerning the teaching of alms, <term>Augustine</term> <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430), a primary Father of the Western Church.</note> said:
almsgiving is a very holy work; it increases present goods and it gives forgiveness of sins.

<meta>This page continues the sermon's transition from the fleeting nature of the physical world to the stark, eternal realities of the afterlife. The preacher employs a rhetorical device common in Old English homilies, contrasting "here" (the mortal world) with "there" (the afterlife), specifically focusing on the punishments awaiting those who indulge in pride, greed, and neglect of the poor.</meta>

This world is nothing but a passing thing, however much one may think they need it. In comparison to the other world, for the blessed everything there is joyful; but for those who follow [evil], there is only sorrow. No evil can exist there <note>Referring to the Kingdom of Heaven.</note>. Because this world is vanishing, here there is only a little sorrow, but there <note>In the place of punishment.</note> is perpetual misery. Here there are small pleasures, but there are the eternal ones, greater than can be imagined. Here there is joyless laughter, but there is the wrath of the estranged. 

Here, those who gained possessions <note>original: "gecnaþ"; literally "to know" or "to recognize," but often used in the sense of "to claim" or "to acquire" in legal or homiletic contexts.</note> through unrighteousness: there our <term>greed</term> <note>original: "hwaᵹla"; a difficult reading, likely related to "wæla" (wealth) or "hæfela" (possessions).</note> and the body's luxury will be turned into severe cold. For those who refused the naked their needs and would not recognize the poor man in God's name: there our mind’s pride, by which people are puffed up, will be brought low in eternal fire. 

Those who are filled with ignorance of the Almighty God’s commandments will be as fuel <note>original: "eylmes"; likely related to "æled" (fire/burning) or embers.</note> in eternal fire. Those who... <note>The text here becomes quite obscure in the OCR, but describes those consumed by worldly desires.</note> will be enveloped in the greater darkness. Those who here defile their bodies with unrighteous stains, or [harm] other men with [wicked] deeds, it will be witnessed there how they must endure the greatest misery. They shall be heated <note>original: "ge hætte"; punished by heat/fire.</note> in the darkness of the pit. Those who roughly and lustfully <note>original: "rü gallice"; roughly or lewdly, referring to those who reject the moral restraints of the church.</note> turn away from God’s justice, they will be in [great torments]. Those who lustfully [behaved] in unrighteous ranks and were filled with pride, they shall be in [great]...

There is no man's **pride** <note>original: "onmedla"; arrogance or worldly pomp</note> so great in this world that he shall not taste of death; and with the soul alone, we must render a true account at the **Great Judgement** <note>original: "myclan dome"; refers to the Last Judgement at the end of the world</note>. The soul is more precious than this life; and it alone follows all the good deeds before God on Doomsday. There, the father may not help the son, nor the son the father, nor any kinsman help another; instead, every single person shall be judged according to his own works.

Furthermore, let us consider how God has created this **world** <term>Middle-earth</term> <note>original: "middan-geard"; the inhabited world of men, positioned between heaven and hell</note> for us to dwell in. It is higher than the inhabitants of hell and lower than heaven; and this world is thus between the two, in the middle. ### Homily IV

...alone with a single will. Because you heard the commands there, and the teaching of the holy ones <note>original: "haligra lare"</note>. With that, when the dead flesh is taken away, no sweetness <term>þrynse</term> <note>possibly related to "þryness" or a corruption of "swetness"</note> can please that heart. A very loathsome speech falls from a foul voice, and it rots among them. Sometimes it has a very loathsome strength, just as he withers. Through this time he has blackness, though at times he has cold speech. Just as the offspring <note>referring to worms or insects of decay</note> find the foul blackness, so the body... worms <unclear>æbðgyc</unclear> eat the body... both are very wretched and bent.

They wait for that which was mine, for my belongings. You had the age to despise me. And when your teacher <term>lareow</term> <note>spiritual instructor or priest</note> came to him, wanting to provide salvation for their soul, he always lay there in filth <unclear>un-puht</unclear>. Oh, why should I have these empty vessels <note>original: "ueme fete," likely "æmtige fatu," a metaphor for the body as a container for the soul</note> with my wicked life? If he thought he would escape, you are led there and are nothing at all.

Then the King’s Father said: "Go, soul, into that nothingness..." <unclear>þernopuht-hor</unclear> <note>likely "un-riht" or a reference to hell</note>. Then the Judge says to the sinful souls: **"Depart from me, you cursed ones, into the eternal fire"** <note>original Latin: "discedite a me maledicti in igne æternū"</note>. That is what is prepared, what my Father prepared for the devil and his angels.

Then the devil has a bow <term>bogan</term> and arrows <term>scetela</term>. The bow is fashioned from... <unclear>p-e-þ-h-e</unclear>. These arrows are of as many kinds as there are many sins. Some [arrows] are fashioned from envy <term>niðe</term> and from <unclear>for</unclear>; some from anger <term>belge</term>; and others...

<meta>This page continues the allegorical description of the battle for the soul. ...heathendom. Some [arrows] come from theft, and from perversity. Some from drunkenness, and from devilish teachings. Some from adultery, and from lustful crafts. Some from witchcraft, and from violence. Some from greed <note>original: "siffnunpfe," likely a corruption of "gifernesse"</note> and robbery. Some from malice and from sinful weapons, and from murder or slaughter. Some from slanders and from the sowing [of discord]. There are so many arrows that no man is able to turn them all away. Every day it is the devil's will that none of those arrows are shot in vain, if he can find a place where he may plant them. And in hell the devils shoot with those arrows, and never cease. Every day he has his bow bent, and wishes to shoot us with those arrows that I named before.

Then there is great need, men <note>original abbreviation: "m̃"</note>, that we have those shields against them that the Lord has given us the wit <note>understanding or wisdom</note> to defend ourselves with. First is the shield of wisdom, and truthfulness <note>original: "þorfærþ"</note>... in good weapons, and in gentleness. Third is the shield of a strong mind <note>original: "modnisse"</note>, and the shield of right belief <note>faith</note>, and the shield of the fear of God. These are the fruits of the Holy Spirit: [fasting], and alms, and the shield of purity, and the shield of gentleness <note>original: "manswæumnesse," likely "mandwærnesse"</note>, the shield of patience, and the shield of steadfastness in good virtues. And that shield carries us to battle against the accursed devil who hates us. Then no sinful enemy <term>syn-sceaða</term> <note>literally "sin-scather," a common poetic term for a demon or a grave sinner</note> can shoot you, because God's angels are armed with those shields to help us against the accursed spirits. Men, if we have those shields which I have now named—of which there are more than any man can tell—which our Lord has given...

...stand with me. If the evil ones have the thought that they will not suffer <note>original: "þrowian," meaning to endure or suffer through a trial</note> through pride against that soon-to-be-vanquished <note>original: "tudum," likely a corruption of "tyder" (weak) or "tudre" (frail)</note> devil. Consider now how it is for you each day on your journey. Each day we have the help of the tongue to call out from above, so that those good things may fall upon us and reach our hands that day. The devil strives to tempt us evilly—that annoying devil who often falls into the games of hell, coming with his black garments <note>original: "scruþþum scuortum," referring to the dark appearance or shrouds of demons</note> to shove us down. 

Therefore, there is great need, men, that we apply the healing salve <note>likely referring to the "salve" of confession or the grace of the Holy Spirit</note> and love those who fear God, following his commandments. Then He will give us eternal life in that glory where He lives and reigns world without end. Amen.

### For Midwinter. Show us, O Lord <note>original Latin: "Ostende nobis domine," a common liturgical opening</note>

HERE it is told through the holy gospel about the holy worthiness of this holy season <note>the "holy season" refers to Midwinter, the Old English term for Christmas</note> which is now approaching through the works of faith, so that those who serve the holy season fittingly and purely in the name of God, for His praise and glory, and for us people for eternal health and comfort. For the King of Glory <note>original: "wuldres cyning," a common poetic title for Christ</note> humbled himself so that he descended from heaven to earth, so that he was born in a human body. Because our Father did not wish to let his creation perish—those whom he created in his own unique likeness—he wanted to release them from the captive grip of the devil, and from the devil’s slavery. Just as Luke the Evangelist wrote and spoke about it: In that time, a great decree <note>original: "gebannum," meaning a proclamation or public command</note> was sent out from the Emperor <note>original: "casere," derived from Caesar</note> that the whole circuit of the world <note>original: "middan earde," literally 'middle-earth,' meaning the inhabited world</note> should be recorded. That census was first begun by the governor who was called Quirinius <note>the OCR reads "syria" and "mannus nama," likely referring to Cyrenius/Quirinius, the governor of Syria</note> the king's name. Then everyone went, each one to his own city, so that they might obey the command that was then proclaimed: that every man should pay tribute <note>original: "gafol gyldan," to pay a tax or rent</note> to the Emperor. 

Then Joseph went up from Galilee, from the city called Nazareth, to David's city, whose name is Bethlehem, because he was of David’s kin, so that they should there [enroll] according to that command and the decree that was then proclaimed, along with Mary, who was there betrothed to him as his wife. And then she [bore] a child, [conceived] of the Holy Spirit. When they came to Bethlehem, the days were fulfilled that she should bring forth the child. And then she brought forth a son, her firstborn, until there [mercy...]

By this it was signified that he came in a human body, of which the Lord himself indeed said: "All power is given to me in heaven and on earth" <note>original Latin: "Data est mihi omnis potestas in caelo et in terra"</note>. That is: all my power is in heaven and on earth. So he spoke concerning his power: "I fill heaven and earth" <note>original Latin: "Caelum et terram ego impleo"</note>. their sins and offenses through right belief and through good exhortations <note>original: "hrepunga"; literally "touchings" or "stirrings," referring to the movement of the soul by grace or preaching</note>. And in the days of the Emperor, all those who previously had turned away from their lords and would not obey them were subdued into rightful service and rightful obedience. Whoever would not have lords was then punished with death; and in that was signified that all of Christ’s mankind should be subjected to the one obedience of God and perform the one will of God. And whoever will not receive right belief, they shall be punished in the torments of hell.

And in Augustus’s days such great peace <term>sybb<explanation>This Old English word means more than just the absence of war; it implies a state of harmony, kinship, and legal security.</explanation></term> was established in middle-earth <note>original: "middangeard"; the world inhabited by humans</note> that men were [amazed], because he ruled the kingdom of middle-earth so well in peace and governed as a king with glorious judgments. In that great peace was signified the coming of the True Peace into middle-earth—that was our Savior Christ, who reconciled us with the angels and interceded for us with God the Father, and invited us to peace. In that was signified that we must, in this life, pay the tribute of right belief to God—that is, in words and in thoughts.

heaven, and broader than the earth, and deeper than the sea.

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page marks a significant transition in the Vercelli Book. It concludes the series of prose homilies (ending with Homily V on the previous page) and begins one of the most famous Old English epic poems, *Andreas*. While the previous text was a sermon on the Nativity and the joys of heaven, this page shifts abruptly into the "heroic" style similar to *Beowulf*, recasting the apostles as warrior-thanes of God.</meta>

Listen! <note>original: *Hƿæt*. This is the traditional opening of Old English epic poetry, used to command the audience's attention. It is famously the first word of *Beowulf*.</note> We have heard of the glory of **noble princes** in days of old, of heroes under the stars, the **thanes of the Lord**. Their courage did not fail in the clash of weapons when banners collided, after they had separated—just as the Lord himself, the high King of the Heavens, had assigned their lot. These were famous men across the earth, bold leaders of the people, brave in battle when they wore their helmets on the battlefield.

Among them was **Matthew**, who, among the Jews, first began to write the Gospel in words with wondrous skill. Holy God appointed his lot out on that island where no foreigner could enjoy a home or prosperity. Often the hand of slayers on the battlefield dealt him hard blows, for all that mighty land was enveloped in murder and the malice of the enemy—that dwelling-place of men, the home of heroes. 

In that place, there was no abundance of bread, nor water to drink for enjoyment, but they consumed the blood and the **flesh-clothing** <note>original: *flæsc-homan*, a kenning (metaphorical compound) for the human body.</note> of men coming from afar; they fed throughout that nation. Such was their custom: that they made a meal of every foreigner—as meat right then and there—who sought that island from the outside. Such was the peaceless practice of those wretched people, their violent strength, that they put out the **sight of eyes**—the **head-jewels** <term>heafod-gimme</term> <note>A classic Old English poetic metaphor where eyes are compared to jewels set in the head.</note>—very cruelly with the points of spears, those gallows-minded men. 

Afterward, sorcerers, through their delusive craft, mixed for them a bitter drink together, a monstrous potion, which steered the inner thoughts and the heart within the breast. Their minds were turned so that they no longer cared for the joys of men, those sword-greedy heroes; instead, they were oppressed by hay and grass <note>original: *mete-læsce*. The text describes the victims losing their humanity and becoming like cattle, driven only by a base, drugged hunger.</note>, exhausted by the need for food. 

Then was **Matthew** come to that sinful city, into the stronghold where there was a great outcry throughout **Mermedonia** <note>A legendary city of cannibals, often identified in medieval tradition with a location in Scythia (modern-day Ukraine/Russia).</note>, a troop of wicked men, a company of the devil’s thanes.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Old English poem *Andreas* from the Vercelli Book. The previous page introduced the land of Mermedonia, a place of cannibals where Saint Matthew has been sent to preach. The text here picks up as the Mermedonians capture Matthew and prepare to subject him to their cruel customs, specifically the ritual blinding of foreigners.</meta>

...and they cast the **thread of the princes** <note>original: *æðelinga þræd*. This refers to the casting of lots, a common theme in hagiography where the apostles' destinations are determined by divine chance.</note>. Then they immediately humiliated him under the linden-shield; they were not slow to reach the beach, to that wasteland. There they bound the hands of the holy man and fastened him with the **fiend’s skill** <note>This suggests both physical bondage and the dark, demonic nature of the Mermedonians.</note>, those heroes hell-bent on destruction.

In that place, they prepared to destroy his **eyes** <note>original: *æsce*, literally "ash-spear." The text implies they used spears or sharp points to blind their captives.</note> with spear-points; yet he still hid his **war-heart** <note>original: *hild-heortan*. A heroic term used to describe the internal courage and defiance of a warrior, here applied to a martyr's endurance.</note> within his breast. He turned then, even though he suffered the pain of that capture. Great-minded, he gave praise to the **Lord of Glory**, the Guardian of the Kingdom of Heaven, from his prison. He made his praise known in his **spirit-lock** <note>original: *fyrhð-locan*, a poetic "kenning" or metaphor for the mind or heart as a container for the soul.</note>, that he through his steadfast strength... they there greatly... through the hatred of men, the fierce leader... the one destined for death endured the **wickedness** <note>original: *fækne*.</note>.

With words he spoke: "How these foreigners have bound me, my life's path. They also counsel how I, in my misery, might be among these sudden-enemies in this thing. My mind is troubled; now a portion of men’s deeds dwindles, just like **dumb cattle** <note>Matthew laments that the Mermedonians treat human beings like livestock intended for slaughter.</note>. You alone know, Guardian of Mankind, the thoughts of the mind. If it be your will, Lord of Glory, that I should lose my life by these spears' peace-breaker, I am ready to endure it. But I pray to you, that you, the Giver of Intelligence, do not allow these crowds of people to do this.

If you, Almighty God, grant me light in this life, then I shall not be **blinded** in these cities. After this sword-hate, through **heathen-speech** <note>original: *hæþen-cwide*. This refers to the blasphemous or cruel decrees of the Mermedonian captors.</note>, the hateful people, the nation-destroyers, should no longer mock me; I will suffer my torment quickly. I, with a firm mind, trust in you alone, the Guardian of the World, that I might ask for the blessed grace of the Father of Angels—that you do not abandon me to the intense heat of these enemies, O Judge of Deeds..."

<summary>This page describes the capture and ritual blinding of Saint Matthew by the cannibalistic Mermedonians, followed by Matthew’s poignant prayer to God for spiritual strength and physical sight.</summary>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Old English poem *Andreas* from the Vercelli Book. On the previous page, Saint Matthew was blinded and imprisoned by the cannibalistic Mermedonians. He has just finished a prayer of lament and faith to God. This section describes God’s response and the arrival of the next morning.</meta>

...[them]. After these words, the **King of Glory** <note>original: *wuldres cyning*. A common epithet for God in Old English Christian poetry, framing Him as a celestial Germanic lord.</note> came from the heavens, the God of holy state, to the prison. There it was revealed within that holy God provided help. Then was heard the voice of the Heaven-King, a sound under the clouds, a voice of command to the kinsman-thane. To the mind-brave kinsman-thane under the **prison-bars** <note>original: *hlin-locan*. Literally "lattice-locks," referring to the wooden or iron grates of the dungeon.</note>, the battle-bold one, He announced health and comfort with a bright **gleam** <note>original: *fāne*. This likely refers to a divine light or banner accompanying the visitation.</note>.

"I give to you, Matthew, my peace under the sky. Do not be too fearful in your fright, nor mourn in your heart. I dwell with you, and I shall release you from these **limb-bonds** <note>original: *leoðu-bendū*. A poetic "kenning" for chains or fetters that bind the joints.</note> and all the multitude who dwell with you in **narrow-need** <note>original: *nearo-nēdū*. A term describing the crushing psychological and physical pressure of imprisonment.</note>. For you, **Paradise** <note>original: *neorxna-wang*. The specific Old English word for the Garden of Eden or the heavenly meadows.</note>, the brightest of fruits and fairest of ancestral wealth, the most joyful of lights, is brightly opened through holy powers. There you may enjoy glory for a wide life <note>meaning "forever."</note>, according to your will. Endure the cruel spear; the time is not great that these liars may use their **toil-craft** <note>original: *swinc-cræft*. This refers to the tortures or "work" of the executioners.</note> to strike you. I [will protect] you and them forever. In this heathen city, [He] shall release you from this hateful crowd. Until that time, there is a short while, a **count of twenty-seven nights** <note>God sets a specific duration for Matthew's suffering before Saint Andrew will arrive to rescue him.</note>, until you may go from this distress, greatly strengthened. Afterward, it shall happen that [Andrew] shall come to guard you with good protection."

Then the Holy Protector of all creatures, the Guardian of Angels, departed to the heavenly homeland. He is the rightful King, wise and steadfast, ruling in every place.

Then Matthew was greatly inspired by the new voice. The **night-helm** <note>original: *niht-helm*. A beautiful poetic metaphor where the darkness of night is seen as a helmet covering the world.</note> glided away, quickly departed; light came after, the dawn. The host assembled, the heathen battle-warriors, a pressing crowd. They shook their **spears** <note>original: *iaras* / *garas*.</note>, enraged in mind, under their **shield-cover** <note>original: *bord-hreoðan*. A poetic term for a shield-wall or the protection of a shield.</note>. They wished to find out whether those who were fast in the prison's chains, in that joyless dwelling for a time, still lived.

->Ritmo I, v. 87-131<-
->(S. Andrea)<-

<vocab>Andreas, Old English poetry, Vercelli Book, Saint Matthew, carcerne, wuldres cyning, hālig god, niwan stefne</vocab>

<summary>God appears to the imprisoned Saint Matthew in a flash of light, promising him that his suffering will end in twenty-seven days and that he will eventually inherit Paradise. As dawn breaks, the Mermedonian captors gather at the prison to see if their captives have survived the night.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Old English poem *Andreas*. On the previous page, God appeared to Saint Matthew in his prison cell in Mermedonia, promising him comfort and eventual rescue. This section describes the grim customs of the cannibalistic Mermedonians as they count down the days until Matthew's scheduled execution, followed by God’s command to Saint Andrew to go and rescue him.</meta>

.4.

They waited, wondering what they might receive for food after the period of their food-need was fulfilled. They held within those borders the violence of princes <note>original: *æþelinga þræc*. This refers to the cruel power the Mermedonian leaders exerted over their captives.</note>. It was their custom that they should provide for themselves food in that nation at the meal-time. The cold-hearted company, one group after another, dwelled there together. The **murderers** <note>original: *bonan*. This term emphasizes the lethal nature of the Mermedonians.</note> did not hesitate there; the mild **Creator** <note>original: *Meotud*. A common Old English word for God, often associated with fate or measurement.</note> [knew] their minds. Then they, the miserable ones, called out with teachings; they [kept] the **wise-minded** <note>original: *mōd-glēawne*. This refers to Saint Matthew, emphasizing his spiritual wisdom despite his physical captivity.</note> one, the holy one, under **dark-locks** <note>original: *heolstor-locan*. A poetic term for a dark, secret place of imprisonment.</note>, a battle-warrior waiting there, where the bright **King of Angels** <note>original: *coning engla*. A title for God that mirrors the Germanic structure of a king and his warrior-thanes (angels).</note> would [reveal] his covenant within. 

Thirty nights had passed and gone from the time they had planned to break his **flesh-covering** <note>original: *flæschoman*. A common Anglo-Saxon kenning for the human body, viewing the flesh as a garment for the soul.</note> and seek his life-blood, for they were hungry for bone. In their cruelty, they intended to divide him among the people, a feast for the warriors. They did not mourn the suffering of the bodies, how the spirit's journey would be wretchedly avenged after its departure. 

Thus they always, after thirty nights had passed, felt a great need to partake with **bloody jaws** <note>original: *blōdigum ceaflum*. A vivid, animalistic description of the cannibals.</note> of the bodies of the dead as food. Then the Savior of the World, the Bright Lord, remembered with great thoughts how he [Matthew] dwelled in pain among the foreign people, bound in **hateful bonds** <note>original: *lāðum bendum*.</note>. 

Then the Holy One, the Lord of Heaven, spoke to the holy **Andrew** <note>original: *Andræas*. The protagonist of the poem, currently in Achaia.</note> there, and taught him with joyful words. The **King of Glory** <note>original: *coninga wuldor*, the Savior of mankind, unlocked his mind-hoard <note>original: *mōdhord onlēac*. A beautiful Old English metaphor for speaking or revealing one's thoughts.</note>; the Teacher of Wisdom spoke with words:

"You shall go and carry peace, a journey to fulfill, where the **man-eaters** <note>original: *wylf-æcan*. Literally "wolf-eaters" or "wolf-like ones," describing the savage nature of the Mermedonians.</note> guard the land with murderous crafts. Such is their custom..."

...that they will not spare the life of any stranger among that people <note>original: *feorh falgian*. The poet emphasizes that the Mermedonians grant no mercy or "life-protection" to outsiders.</note>, once that sin-filled multitude encounters a victim in that tribe, in those wretched borders. There shall the **parting of the soul and body** <note>original: *þorph geðal* (standard OE *sawl-gedal*). A common Anglo-Saxon poetic expression for death.</note> occur, a wretched slaughter of men soon after. 

"Though I tell you that your **victory-brother** <note>original: *sige-broðor*. This refers to Saint Matthew, framing the fellow apostle as a brother-in-arms in a spiritual war.</note> remains there among the city-dwellers. Now, especially, listen to me: he must suffer the hand of the heathens in that nation. You shall seek him out with all your strength, unless you come there sooner to help him."

Andrew answered him: "Alas, my Lord, how can I, your servant, perform this as you have asked? I am a stranger here, and I know not the way over the **circuit of the seas** <note>original: *holma begang*. A kenning for the vast, encircling ocean.</note> and all the paths of the world. The way is unknown to me; the paths of the sea are strange, and the terror of the waters is great. There, until I reach that land, the foreign people will be unknown to me, nor do I know the thoughts of those heroes, nor do I know the cold water-ways of the sea-shores."

Then the glorious Lord answered him: "Alas, Andrew, that you should ever hesitate, or think it too difficult for God to accomplish in this world-war! That you should think it hard to reach that generation, or to go to that city among the citizens, if the **King of Glory** <note>original: *Wuldor-cyning*. A majestic title for God common in Christian Anglo-Saxon verse.</note> commands it. 

Do not be so hesitant in your mind, if you wish to keep your covenant with your Lord. It is not at all becoming to delay. You shall make your way to the ship, and endure the **battle-terror** <note>original: *hilde-woman*. The poet uses military language to describe the upcoming journey through the stormy sea.</note> of the heathens; through the skill of battle and the strength of the tide, you shall go. You shall go in the ship with your followers, across the many waves, to the city..."

The inhabited world of men in Germanic cosmology, situated between heaven and hell.</note>, wherever you go."

->Ritmo I, v. 222-263<-
->(Saint Andrew)<-

**THEN** was the message announced to the noble warrior in the cities; his heart was not fearful, but he was resolute, daring in courage, hardy and bold of mind, no laggard in battle, but ready for the struggle of God's combat. He set out then at evening, at daybreak <note>original: *æfen mid ærdage*. The poet uses seemingly contradictory terms for "evening" and "early day," likely suggesting the transition of the night watch or a very early start before the sun has fully risen.</note>, over the slopes of the shore, toward the sea, eager. 

The **thane** <note>original: *þegn*. A noble follower or warrior; here referring to Saint Andrew as a soldier of Christ.</note> pondered in his mind how he might walk upon the water. The sea-murmur resounded; the ocean waves beat against the shore. The hero was in hope, once he found a bold ship upon the shore, at the water's edge. 

Then came the bright morning, the **brightest of beacons** <note>original: *beacna beorhtost*. A reference to the sun.</note>, gliding over the sea, holy out of the darkness; the **heaven-candle** <note>original: *heofon-candel*. A common Old English kenning for the sun.</note> shone over the sea-floods. Day drew near. Then he saw three majestic thanes sitting in a boat, brave and bold, as if they had just come over the sea. 

That was the Lord himself, the Ruler of hosts, the eternal Almighty, with his two angels. They were in the guise of sailors, appearing like noble men, like young warriors when they play upon the cold water in ships, across the embrace of the flood, over the distant wave. 

The wise man, standing on the shore, greeted them and asked: "From where have you come, sailing in your ship, men mighty in sea-craft, on this sea in a solitary **wave-floater** <note>original: *æg-flotan*. A kenning for a ship.</note>? Where have you come from over the tossing of the waves?"

Then Almighty God answered him, so that he who spoke the word did not know who this man was among those speaking there on the shore...

...spoke to them, seeking to find the land of the Mermedonians <note>original: *Mermedonia mægð*. The transcription "edoma mæge" is a distorted OCR reading of this name.</note>. To those who were embarked upon the path of the flood <note>original: *flode bæð*. A kenning meaning the sea, literally "the flood's bath."</note>, where the high-stemmed ship, the swift sea-steed <note>original: *sæmearh*. A common kenning for a ship, comparing it to a horse galloping over the waves.</note>, waited, quickly surrounded by the tide, until they reached the land of these people, driven by the wind as we were. They suffer death in that city by choice, those who lead the sword thither. Do you now desire, over the wide sea, to waste your life in that conflict?" 

Then Andrew gave him an answer: "Our desire urges us toward that land, with great courage, to that famous city, dearest lord, if you will show us your kindness upon the salt sea."

The Protector of Angels, the Savior of men, answered him from the ship's prow: "We will gladly and freely carry you with us over the **fish's bath** <note>original: *fisces bæð*. An Anglo-Saxon term for money or small silver coins.</note>, as the ship-wardens require over the ship’s side, within the deck-timbers."

Then Andrew spoke words to him in answer to his request: "I have no **plated gold** <note>original: *fated gold*. Gold that has been beaten into thin plates for decorating jewelry or armor.</note>, nor any store of wealth, neither riches nor wisdom, nor the joining of wire-rings <note>original: *wira gespann*. Refers to gold-wire jewelry or mail-armor links, common forms of portable wealth in the early medieval period.</note>, neither land nor locked treasures, that I may satisfy your desire or praise your voyage with words." 

Then the Prince of Men, where he sat upon the gangplank <note>original: *bolcan*. The deck or gangway of the ship.</note>, spoke to him across the tossing of the waves.

He told the stone to let them gather their limbs, receive a soul and a young spirit, and come forth. 

The ancient wise ones, who had been [dead] for a long time, were to reveal themselves to the people so they might understand the power of God. The stone then departed to travel, just as the mighty **Creator** of men had commanded it, over the mountain paths until it came to **Mamre** <note>the biblical site of the Cave of Machpelah where the patriarchs were buried</note>, shining brightly, exactly as the glorious Lord commanded.

There the bodies of the high fathers had been hidden for a long time. They then quickly stood up: **Abraham** and **Isaac**, and the third nobleman, **Jacob**, rose from the dust, awakened from that fast sleep. They prepared themselves for the journey; they wished to go to the judgment. They would reveal to the people who [God] was at the beginning of creation...

You may hear how he <note>Christ, disguised as a pilot</note> revealed wonders in words, just as a man might do for men. The heat of the spirit boiled in his mind. I know that many a man will be greatly blessed who dwells with that man on the paths of the waves. Then those who were weary could not hold back their hearts, wise in thoughts. Fire and light <unclear>distress</unclear> throughout the long day protected the holy teaching until a deep sleep came over [Andrew] on the **whale-road** <term>hron-rade: a famous Old English kenning (metaphor) for the sea</term>, near the King of Heaven.

<insert>T</insert>HEN the **Life-Giver** commanded [his angels] to lead him over the waves. He had written his teaching on the paths... <unclear>beyond the sea-stone</unclear> until that deep sleep came through the motion of the air. He arrived at the land, at the city, which the King of Angels [had shown him]. Then the [angels] traveled quickly on the upward path to their own home. They let the holy man sleep in peace, in a high sitting-place <note>the angels set Andrew down gently on the ground outside the city</note> under the shelter of the sky <gloss>literally "shelter of the sun/stars"</gloss>, waiting joyfully near the city walls until the **Lord**...

# VI

<margin>7/.</margin>

...how they [the angels] honored the holy man on their knees with no little strength. They sang victory to the Lord, a truthful praise; the judgment of the day was beautiful. There was **David** <note>The biblical King David, often depicted in medieval literature as a leader of the heavenly choir</note> with them, the blessed one among others, a multitude of **thanes** <term>servants or noble followers</term> when Christ had come, the King of Israel. Just as they direct the paths for those famous nobles, he [Christ] did not wish to approach the **earth-destroyers** <note>original: *eoricsceadan*; likely referring to the cannibalistic Mermedonians Andrew has come to convert</note>.

"The blessed heroes, we served in glorious paths; holy high angels [were there], for whom there is a pool of salvation, those who may enjoy those joys. There was the **joy of glory** <note>original: *wuldres wynn*</note>, a host of workers, noble in their going. There was no strife for anyone there; there beauty is wide-spread, beauty is opened to those who shall serve the **Prince** <note>original: *pioƿian* (to serve)</note> there. It shall never repent him when he goes hence from here."

Then the spirit-mind of the holy man was greatly blessed after he began to hear those words: that God would remember them so much, or [give them] all His power. Therefore the **protector** <note>Saint Andrew</note> was glad. 

"Now I, Lord God, have understanding that you, the **Glory of Princes** <note>original: *oþringe wuldres* (corrupted); standard *æþelinga wuldor*</note>, were not far away on the sea-current when I stepped into the ship. Though I, on the wave-path, could not recognize the **Prince of Angels** <note>original: *engla þeoden*</note>—may the Almighty be merciful to me—the blithe, bright King. I sit in the joy of words... that speech... through the mind’s eyes... other floods. That is the Spirit of Comfort to the race of men, that help which goes forth, most merciful in power to every man, a right peace-fold to him who reaches out to Him."

Then a sign was shown to him on the **run-path** <note>the path of his journey or the sea-road</note>, the Prince, until in that same city... by the King’s crafts... everywhere... how the strength of the host... when he spoke with words, the **Lord of Glory**...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Christ and Saint Andrew in the Old English epic *Andreas*. Having revealed His identity to the apostle, Christ now warns Andrew of the physical torments he must endure at the hands of the Mermedonians. He encourages Andrew by recounting His own Passion—specifically the crucifixion and the piercing of His side—explaining that these trials are necessary to bring the heathens to the "light of heaven."</meta>

**N**ow you must quickly prepare an answer in the grasp of enemies. Your war-dress <note>A poetic metaphor for the body or the apostle’s spiritual protection</note> shall be dealt out by hard sword-strokes; your body shall be destroyed by wounds. Most like water, your blood shall flow in a flood. They cannot, however, part you from your **life** <term>original: *feorh* – the vital spirit or soul</term> through death, even though you suffer the blows and strokes of sinful men.

Endure that pain; do not let the power of the heathens turn you aside, nor their violence, so that you should shrink from God. Your strength shall not fail you there. Keep in your mind how it became known to many—famed across many lands—that they bound me with iron bands, held me fast. They told lies there; they accused me with words. The sinful ones rushed and sprang forth, yet they could not, through their speech, reveal the truth when I covered the **red gallows** <note>original: *rædum galgan* – a common Anglo-Saxon kenning for the Cross, stained with blood</note>. There was room for a spear-point; from my side, the blood let out, gore upon the earth.

I endured many waves of strife across the earth. I wished for that with a joyful heart, to kindle a fire <note>Likely referring to the fire of faith or the Holy Spirit</note>. Likewise, in this foreign nation, many shall be redeemed in this famous city—those whom you turned toward the light of heaven through my name, even though they have committed many murders in days of old. To them, the Holy King of all kings shall offer that pure home above as a reward, where mercy is available for every person who knows how to find it.

Then the man was mindful, patient in soul, a warrior battle-hardened. Truly, in his breast, the hero was quickly resolute, bolstered in courage, bold in spirit. He spoke to the **border-land** <note>original: *mærcuðe* – likely referring to the boundary of the city Mermedonia</note>; the Lord shaped the path so that no man could perceive him <note>Referring to the miracle of Andrew’s invisibility as he enters the city</note>.

They kissed and clung to each other; both were dear in heart. A light shone around them, holy from the heavens' heights; the shelter of men <note>A poetic title for God, who protects humanity</note> was filled with joys. Then one began to speak to the other, the noble companion, crying out with words, speaking well to the servant of God; he told him of the good outcome of men's fate. Then he <note>Andrew</note> led out from the hateful bonds, from the prison into the peace of the Lord, **two hundred and twenty** <note>The poem meticulously counts the prisoners Andrew miraculously releases</note> by number; likewise seventy more he also rescued from that enmity. There he left no one fastened in bonds under the city’s locks; in that manner, not one of those thirty men <note>Likely referring to a specific group of guards or remaining captives</note> remained to suffer. Wailing, they <note>the guards</note> quickly departed; they stayed no longer. In that grim hope of battle-clash, Matthew then departed to lead the **multitude** <note>original: "mthigo," a scribal variant of "menigo" (many/multitude)</note> into the grace of God, just as the holy one had commanded him. He traveled under a covering of clouds so that no harm from the enemy's shields should come to them.

There the two elders spoke together; the brave ones held a council. The two companions wept before they parted in two; each of those nobles made known to the other the hope of the heavenly kingdom and the terrors of hell. He armed him with words. Thus the righteous ones, the heroes of spirit, the champions of the King, remained steadfast in their holy purpose. They suffered for the Ruler of the winds. There, glory is never encompassed by the passing time of men.

**holding council**. One kinsman led the other to the border by the wall; they found **seven noble guards** <note>The poem describes Andrew encountering seven guards at the prison gate, whom he strikes down through divine power</note> stationed there. He <note>Andrew</note> left them without breath; their souls went up to the joyful memory of angels. From there, the wise one <note>Andrew</note> went into the city’s locks. Then the leaders of the people, the heathens, gathered in a troop; they came to the fortress in a bold band. They intended to drag out the captives in heaps, thinking in their hostile minds that they would find the foreigners as a **customary feast** <note>The Mermedonians are depicted as cannibals who ritualistically eat the prisoners they capture</note> to serve their needs. Their expectation failed them once they found the house-locks opened and the **guards dead**. They, joyless and deprived of desire, told the bad news to the people: that no one was left alive as a remnant in the prison. Instead, they found their sword-warrior guards lying dead, deprived of speech, their bodies <note>original: "flæsc haman" – literally "flesh-homes" or "flesh-coverings"</note> stripped of life. Many a man among the people was terrified at that news; their minds were sad, for they were oppressed by hunger, those **black-clad heralds of death** <note>original: "blaca beu zufæy" – likely a scribal error for "blace berumas" or similar, referring to the grim fate of starvation</note>. They knew of no better food to sustain their lives. The door was opened; an evil fate had come to them all at once; through a hard play of weapons, a bed of slaughter <note>original: "hild bedd" – a "battle-bed," a poetic way of saying they found only death</note> was prepared. Then I heard that the leaders of the people were summoned; the warriors came, those bold thanes, famous for their bravery, together to the council where the people were gathered; they consulted one another.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Old English poem *Andreas*. On the previous page, the Mermedonian people had chosen a young boy as a sacrificial victim to alleviate their famine and were leading him to the place of execution.</meta>

Edge-sharp and hard-edged, from the enemy’s hand, the sword began to kill the fated youth. Then that seemed hateful to the <gloss>thanes</gloss>, a severe national calamity <note>original: "þeod-bealo" – a disaster affecting the whole people</note> to endure in battle: that one so innocent of life should be led within the... [prison]. Hopeless and stern, the brave young thanes, the retainers of the lord, intended to touch him with spears and destroy him. 

But God protected him, the Holy One from the heights, against the heathen folk. He [God] stood against those punishments; in that slaughter-play <note>original: "beadu-lace" – literally 'battle-play', a common poetic term for combat, here used for the intended sacrifice</note> the weapons all melted away, lest the guilty ravagers should harm him with edges of iron. So it was that the heathens' attack was turned aside by the Lord of Lords, to whom He gives judgment—to each of those men who reach for His help with wisdom. There is always a ready, eternal friend <note>original: "freond unhwilen" – a friend not bound by time</note> for him who can find Him.

Then there was a cry of the army within the cities; everywhere the people complained, lacking food. They stood weary, captive to hunger. The gabled halls <note>original: "horn-selu" – halls decorated with horns or gables, a sign of a high-status Germanic building</note> resounded. The warriors had no joy in their dwellings to build upon in that bitter time. They sat wary-minded, their wounds a source of grief. Nor was there joy in the home for those who had known many hardships, nor did the man have a gracious remnant of wisdom. Now the time has come, a throe of the sword <note>original: "þrea of mæce" – oppression or pain caused by the sword</note>; now there is a great need that we listen to these firm words.

The Almighty has humbled you and cast you into that dark place where the King of Kings covered you in judgment, and the inhabitants of that place never find joy.”

The time is not long that these slaughter-greedy ones <note>original: "wæl-wreowe"</note> will enclose you in cold chains. Manifest yourself, harden your heart, and strengthen your mind so that they may recognize my power in you. They may not, nor are they permitted against my will, to part your body <gloss>the guilty-less one</gloss> from death, though you must endure painful blows. I am with you."

At those words, an immense crowd came, a troop of people armed with shields <term>lind: shields made of linden wood, the standard equipment of a Germanic warrior</term>. They, angry in spirit, carried the holy man out where his hands were bound. And then the nobility’s sin was revealed; with their eyes, they could see the victory-bold one where many a person sought him. On that field of slaughter, the company of people wandered, wondering what they might do to him. 

They then commanded him to be led over the stony land; from time to time, the enemies, as cruelly as they could devise, dragged the brave-hearted one through the mountain-paths, around the stone slopes, as far as the path stretched, the ancient work of giants <note>original: "enta ær-geweorc" – a common Old English phrase referring to Roman ruins or ancient stone structures</note>. 

Within the city, the hard stone path was stained with blood; a great clamor arose among the heathen troop. The holy man’s body was covered in sore wounds; blood <note>original: "swate" – literally 'sweat', but used poetically here to mean blood</note> flowed in waves, and his **bone-house** <note>original: "ban-hus" – a famous Anglo-Saxon kenning for the physical body</note> was broken, as the hot blood welled out...

...so the long day sprang forth; red blood welled in waves, and through the **bone-coffins** <note>original: "ban-cofan" — a poetic kenning (metaphor) for the human body or ribcage</note> the blood swallowed the life-fluid in hot gore. Lay healing upon me, my Source of Life, so that you may be merciful-hearted to me because of your power. Since it was three days ago <note>Andrew notes he has been tortured for three days, echoing the three days Christ spent in the tomb</note> that I was first seized, let me not be abandoned by the Almighty. As I previously vowed while my life remains on earth, I will not yield to these hateful ones. You are the protector against those enemies, the eternal Counselor for all your people. Do not let the killers of mankind mock me now, that most wicked host, through their proud cruelty; let them be humbled, those who wait for your praise."

Then there appeared the hideous one, the worker of lies, the leader of darkness. With his power, the devil of hell, driven by inward cruelty, said: "Strike the sinner across his face <note>original: "ofer hleorswa" — across the cheeks/face</note> with <unclear>...buffets...</unclear>; let his words of woe now play out in misery." 

Now it rose up until the smoke began to glow... night covered all in shadow. Dark over the mountains <gloss>beorgas</gloss> the shadow crept. Then the holy one was led to the building; it was a decreed fate. In that dim building, in the cell of necessity, he must stay for a night-long duration... then a great monster <note>original: "æglæca" — a word famously used for Grendel in *Beowulf*, here referring to the devil or a tormentor</note> came to the hall to walk at large.

Mindful of evils, the **master of sin** <note>original: "manfrea" — a compound word for a lord of wickedness or crime</note>, disgraced by darkness, the death-fierce devil, deprived of his heavenly retinue, then began to speak words of mockery to the holy man: "What did you intend, Andrew, with your journey here into the power of your enemies? [The water] withdrew. Then at dawn, foamy waves <note>original: "fæmige walcan"</note> with [surging] water welled up; the flood increased from its mouth. The earth was fastened and surged. After the feast day, they started from sleep. A <gloss>gripping</gloss> <note>original: "ræpne" — likely meaning binding or inevitable</note> hot bath <note>original: "hat-baðe" — an ironic Old English metaphor; though the water was cold, its destructive power is described as the "heat" of battle or a "bath" of death</note> fell upon them. They wished to flee to the mountains, to the shelter of the caves; but an angel prevented them, spreading a bright fire <note>original: "blidan lige" — the flood is described with the intensity of fire, or perhaps literal fire accompanied the water as a divine omen</note> over the city, a hot battle-surge. The storm raged. The group of thanes could not escape from that fastness. The wood [of the buildings] resounded. The flood-waves welled up. There was the sound of mourning. Wretched songs were sung. The many were terrified. Through the song of death <note>original: "leoð-galdor" — literally a "song-spell," suggesting a supernatural or fatal lament</note>, a terrifying cry resounded through the air. The walls of the buildings were overtaken by the rising flood. The clamor of men grew. There was a hateful gathering; the people were gathered in that place where the flood-power spread. Now they could not find their own escape; they suffered that misery with strife and grief.

# 50 v

[one] may pray to the faithful Father that He might rightly [help]. Protector of sinners, <note>original: "syndigra hleo"</note> a great surging [of water]—since I reckon toward God that you may, by petition, release us from these hateful bonds. From all this storm, <note>original: "unwæder" — literally 'un-weather,' meaning a storm or disastrous conditions</note> haste is best, to ask the holy one for help, for aid and comfort through prayers, so that later the swelling flood might burn <note>The poet ironically uses terms for fire—"singe" or "burn"—to describe the destructive power of the water.</note> less, if the doomed might [turn] to him. So the present [calamity] over the land... for the lost ones it was best to wait, so that there the strength of the brave might be widespread. Wherever they avoided the curse, the dark track <gloss>of death</gloss> followed them. After those words, quickly and promptly, then all those [the people] from that which they... to provide... in their hearts... the falling [flood] created [destruction] with many... among the stone-cliffs <note>The stone-cliffs likely refer to the marble pillars from which the water originally burst.</note>. However, a bold storm stepped out. Lo, then the portion [of water] poured out toward the south. The lowly spirit, left to God, how gracious then, whither they pushed. A sharp [terror] was perceived, [though] reconciled in the embrace [of God]. Where the garment <note>original: "rægl" — possibly referring to the shroud of water or the clothing of the victims</note>... that I might destroy... [it] would be dry from the flood. As the sudden attack shaped them, a turning point occurred; this was a battle-plain <term>beadu-wong: a common poetic compound in Old English meaning a field of war</term> when they came forth there.

Each one ceased [their resistance]; heaven raged so through the company of the holy ones. And the counsel was that a remedy <note>original: "ræd" — can mean both advice/counsel and a saving remedy or plan</note> waited for them, though it did not previously [seem] cruel to load... after the weeping in that place, the power [of the flood] was [given] to the brave. There the waves were divided; all were [made] sound from the sea. By no means did the waves only drench the heathens, <note>The poet is making a grim point: the water didn't just get them wet; it brought them to the point of death.</note> but there death [took them]. There was also such a victory... the standing...

...power afterward in the track... that which... for him... then.

# 51 v

The **protection of the Creator**, the shelter of the multitude, the King of kings <note>original: "cyninga wuldor" — the OCR "cwaþere" is likely a corruption of "wuldor" (glory/king of glory)</note> [granted] then that the mountain split open <note>The poem describes the earth swallowing the floodwaters through a chasm in the hills.</note> and the water flowed into the earth. # 52 r

[Andrew said:] "My [heart is set on travel]." But the **Lord of Hosts** <note>original: "wigþreata hleo" — literally "the protection of warrior-hosts," a heroic title used here for God</note> spoke to him, [commanding that he] remain within this city for a space of **three nights** more. He led them toward the **glorious home** of the kingdom of heaven, where the Father, the Son, and the Spirit of Comfort <note>The Holy Spirit</note> rule in the majesty of the **Trinity** <note>original: "þrynesse þrymme"</note> for ever and ever in glorious dwellings. 

Likewise, the holy man [Andrew] drove away the ancient errors and the debt of idols <note>original: "gild" — pagan sacrifices or idols</note> that had previously filled the land. Through the teaching of Andrew, the people were led from **hell’s pits** <note>original: "helltræfu"</note> to a higher purpose. There the enemy <note>The Devil</note> shall never find a place, nor shall those who walk in the spirit go there. 

Then the day began to dawn when he [Andrew] had to depart from them over the sea-road. He left the spacious city of the Father <note>The newly Christianized city</note>. Then a **great mourning** arose among the people; they suffered in heart, grieving after the man [Andrew]. Another time, the teacher [commanded] that the people should not weep for his departure. He spoke of the **eternal home** in the heights, but in the hell-prison, there is no comfort for friends. I have heard that the people, a host of men, then followed the beloved teacher to the sea-shore.

Then the **God of Glory** himself appeared to him on that journey; the **Lord of Hosts** <note>original: "wereda drihten"</note> spoke that word to the frightened people. They gathered together in a grim mood, and the bold men were lamented, along with their wives; their weeping broke out, and their spirits mourned for the departing one.

**Listen!** We have heard through holy books that among the Ethiopians <note>original: "Sigelwarum" — literally 'Sun-dwellers' or 'dwellers of the scorched land'</note> the truth became manifest: the lordly judgment of God. Those two were not alike [in their paths], those bold-hearted ones; they sought the land of the Persians, **Simon and Jude**. A single day of ending <note>death</note> was appointed for them both together through the rush of weapons. Through a noble death, they changed their earthly work to receive the reward of victory: the joy of the spirit after death. Then life was parted from the body, from this transitory creation. 

Thus those princes reached their end, those good-hearted ones, an unbroken fate. They accepted torment in the face of violence. 

Now I pray to those who take pleasure in this teaching, this path of song, that they pray for me; that they ask help for me from that **holy troop**, for peace and support. How I, an old man <note>original: "frod"</note>, have need of a gentle journey, when I must seek out a long home, an unfamiliar dwelling-place, alone. Grant me today a share... # BUT WITHOUT
If she <note>Refers to the soul or the mind, which is feminine in Old English grammar.</note> always withdraws from medicine <note>original: "leace." Can also mean "leek," frequently used as a metaphor for spiritual healing in early medieval texts.</note> and makes known every mystery, through the love of public things and thoughts, the mind turns to the love of God so that it thirsts for and desires all lovely things. Truly, no excellence <term>geþincð</term> <note>A term for honor, dignity, or spiritual rank earned through virtuous living.</note> can bring forth anything within it [otherwise]. Truly, all the leaders <note>original: "coþþan," likely a corruption of "koppas" or "cempas," meaning heads or champions.</note> of living men were honored through virtue and through teaching, those who now can [exist] within. Among those was Abel in the ancient world, and another, Noah; a man cannot be righteous without virtue among the unrighteous ways. Truly, Noah alone was righteous among all the kin of men, and here another—his righteousness was more thriving in God’s people.

Now, what I tell you: if you are now virtuous, you who have the strengths of many holy men as examples to bear, what do you think? How did he thrive alone among the lawless <note>original: "aplagū," a scribal contraction for "a-lagum," those without law.</note> without any example? You consider now what the winds were at the flood, and how Abraham’s abandonments were <note>Referring to Abraham leaving his kindred and father's house at God's command.</note> when he followed there, and he did not protect himself from the <unclear>fruits of the harvest</unclear>. He showed [an example] to others both in battles and in hungers. Remember also how a courageous man <note>Likely referring to Lot or Isaac.</note> spoke, how he served their customs and other men turned to his virtue and teaching. Remember also Jacob, a man of great power in virtue; concerning those virtues he was speaking to Pharaoh when he said: "Few and evil have been my days, and they have not reached the <note>original: "hyna." Likely "heanna," meaning the high age of his ancestors.</note> [days] of my [fathers]..."

### Homily VII

<note>There is a large ink blot here, obscuring several words.</note> ...in a few days. Beyond that, having been joined among the ten commandments [given to] the apostles, they were all more greatly oppressed <note>original: "geþryncte," likely meaning they were more tested or burdened by their duties.</note> than any other men.

Then let us also follow Christ himself along that path, with whom you have for a long time [dwelled]; he who suffered the heaping of sorrows and prepared a ready gift <note>Likely referring to the Grace of the Crucifixion.</note>. Not at all is he holy who is not as the Lord himself said; quickly we were [lost] when they diminished, so that they hid themselves away and cried out; [they are led] not to life, but he leads them to that dryness <note>A metaphor for spiritual barrenness or the "parched" state of the soul in hell.</note>.

Remember also those who led all their life in this world in idle pleasures; think then of the rich man <note>The text says "Lazarus þone welegan" (Lazarus the wealthy). The scribe has likely confused the names, as Lazarus was the beggar and the unnamed 'Dives' was the rich man.</note>, though he feasted daily and ate sumptuously and abundantly.

And remember those wretched ones who gave their life entirely to their bellies <term>þambe</term> <note>A variant of "wamb," meaning stomach or womb; used here to denote gluttony.</note> as a loss, rather than to the commandments; there it is said that their belly was their god.

Think also of those who, for a small judgment, became [slaves] to their unreleased desires; those who were in the days and nights [faithful] to the commandments, who lived that [higher] life in the earth. It was said that the bread itself <note>The "bread" here likely refers to the Eucharist or the Bread of Life.</note> ... burns like a thorn-bush, which is to be spoken of among the [worldly] partners. Remember how quickly their spirit led into the other [life]... you who [followed] Jesus, by whom your good things were both [given] through...

<meta>This page continues the text of Homily VII. While the previous page introduced the "Apostolic" model of living righteously within the world and touched upon the parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus, this page focuses on the dire consequences for those who choose worldly luxury over spiritual health, specifically citing the pride of ancient kings as a warning.</meta>

...of wretched punishments and tribulations, prepared as medicinal teachings <note>original: "wyrtlarscum," likely referring to the "bitter herbs" of spiritual instruction or penance.</note> for the fall into hell. Such were the kings of Babylon and Assyria; they all arranged their lives very immoderately and boasted in their procreation <term>bearn-team</term> <note>Literally "child-team," referring to the pride taken in dynasties and physical descendants rather than spiritual legacy.</note>.

Hear me what Christ said: that those who offend me were more sinful than the monsters <note>original: "hwallum," literally whales or sea monsters, often used as symbols of heathens or the abyss.</note> among the heathens, compared to those who think rightly and will not have such things. Truly, [this] condemns both the things of this world—not only the unsteadfast but also the steadfast. It consumes the souls of those with sorrowful thoughts.

**F**or it thrives so quickly from their nature, but it perishes from its own soft members. They are so frail because they are always kept inside; they never exert themselves but often bathe with perverse dealings and always delight in some bath <note>A common medieval trope: frequent bathing and soft living were associated with physical luxury and spiritual rot.</note>. But you might understand thereby that they [the prideful]... in what change of law and terrible life they dwell. What other thing seems fairest in a tomb-fast life <note>A life lived "fastened" to the grave, focused only on mortality.</note> and in a dark life? Then you might see that it soon leaves behind the greatness that it had in its former life.

Let each one think about these things, which seem but a trifle. Then you know that it will be a holy year <note>A period of judgment or reckoning.</note> for those who live there in vanity <term>ideln-sse</term>. No vanity benefits the body, nor does it seem at all that it fed the soul; for the soul must of necessity have better [nourishment], whereas the body [is] un... <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence.</note>

Then he [the man] may not weigh the path, nor their strength, nor their growth, but there will always be a path of sorrow; whatever you do there, each becomes more accustomed to the one who rescues it. So also each body is more compliant through the patience of the soul’s <term>scinna</term> <note>original: "scinna," here referring to the soul's inner light or perhaps the illusions/mirages of the world that the soul must navigate.</note> of patience. <summary>This page discusses the theological conflict between the soul's health and the body's comfort, warning that 'flattering' the flesh through luxury leads to the soul's decay. It cites the teachings of the Apostle Paul to advocate for spiritual discipline over physical indulgence.</summary>

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues the seventh homily, which explores the tension between physical indulgence and spiritual health. Having previously discussed how external luxury leads to ruin, the author now focuses specifically on the vice of gluttony (excessive "fullness") and the virtue of moderation. The text shifts from general warnings to a physiological and spiritual analysis of how overeating "darkens" the mind and makes the body "untameable," while clarifying that the goal is not self-destruction through starvation, but the restoration of the soul's authority over the flesh.</meta>

...the body rightly, and so one must not spare those other <term>fullnesses</term> <note>original: "fylle," referring to gluttony or the state of being over-satiated.</note>. One should eat then according to a moderate measure of food; [excessive] food harms the body because the mind slides away from every health into unrighteousness. From foul limbs there arises <unclear>am nnoðe</unclear>; [yet] food helps the soul when it is well-moderated. The power [of the soul] hears well when one restrains that excess, from which come many infirmities. This gluttony is worse than the horse <note>The author employs a common medieval metaphor: the body is a horse and the soul is the rider. If the horse is overfed and "untameable," it will throw the rider.</note> that strikes [its rider]; that horse collapses so that it cannot [carry] the other gluttony. 

It kills the body and <unclear>ralyreð</unclear> from another <unclear>protlica</unclear> life. Gluttony makes the body untameable and causes it to rot; then at the last, it greedily takes away [life]. I do not teach that men should kill themselves with hunger; but that they should help their health so much so that the body desires to follow the intentions of the soul. Truly, that other satiety <note>original: "fyl," gluttony.</note> can provide nothing for the soul, but it darkens and hurts the soul of the body. 

He who will not [restrain] the desires of men, his [soul] perishes; he enjoys himself or performs unrighteousness. Truly, one cannot overcome an unrighteous life through hunger alone, but through moderate satiety. Each of those gluttonies comes with a weight that promises them less; [instead] seek the strength of moderation, which promises a greater reward of purity and peace. Therefore, moderation is the best thing for the body—not that other gluttony—but a moderate [measure] is a light for the soul, whereas the foul, running [excess] is a darkness. Truly, the holy ones lived so wonderfully in that holy [way of life].

<summary>This page warns against the spiritual and physical dangers of gluttony, comparing an overindulged body to a wild horse. It advocates for "gemetzung" (moderation) as the means to keep the soul healthy and the body compliant.</summary>
<keywords>gluttony, moderation, soul vs. body, health, infirmity, spiritual discipline, Anglo-Saxon homily</keywords>
