To the people:

->III<-

**Many** <note>The OCR reads "MENDA," which is likely a scribal error for "Manige" (Many).</note> teachers admonish and remind us in these books; **Saint Gregory**, the holy ruler and wise teacher, is he who directed and spoke this. Therefore, concerning ourselves, it first seems to the teachers who remind us of those words which the Lord spoke in his books: that in this life of the people, words shall be written and words shall be blotted out. And he also tells us that such evil should come upon this **middle-earth** <note>original: "middan geard," the traditional Germanic and Anglo-Saxon term for the inhabited world of men, situated between heaven and hell.</note> and such vengeance upon the nations. He wished to prepare us then, so that we would be ready when he suffered this life. 

Dearest men, let us never let ourselves [be caught] too greatly therein, nor let it seem so very sudden that we are ever ashamed to confess him. For even if a man is ashamed of his sins before a single man, [how much more] then on **Judgment Day** <note>original: "domes dæge," literally "Doomsday."</note> before God himself, and before his angels, and before all that heavenly host.

Regarding this, the noble teacher **Saint Paul** the Apostle spoke and said this: "Dearest men, it is fitting for us that we all appear on Judgment Day before God’s high throne." Then no man may hide his own secrets, nor can he look upon his own face there, nor turn away into the shadow of sins.

About this, almighty God spoke and said thus: "When the Son of Man comes in glory with his angels, then he will reward each one according to his works." <note>original Latin: "Filius hominis ubi venit in gloria cum angelis suis tunc reddet unicuique secundum opera sua."</note> The Son of Man comes in the high clouds in the form of his <term>majesty</term> <note>original: "mægenþrym," a compound of "mægen" (power/strength) and "þrym" (glory/force), specifically used for the divine splendor of God.</note> and in his glory to the angels and with the archangels.

Therefore, dearest ones, let us eagerly protect ourselves and eagerly pray that we may be worthy of that which he then says to his righteous and his chosen ones: **"Come, you blessed ones, dwell with me in my kingdom that you have earned."** <note>A translation of Matthew 25:34.</note>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the necessity of repentance and confession to avoid the pains of hell. It begins a new section, titled Homily IX in the rubric, which enumerates the "threefold death" and contrasts it with the "three lives" of the faithful. The scribe uses standard late Old English orthography with some characteristic spellings like "ƿ" for "w."</meta>

The **threefold death** is the soul's parting from the body. Then the third death is that the souls must be cursed in hell, where there is no man who may rescue his soul, because the soul is held fast within those **torments** <note>original: "þelum"; literally "planks" or "boards," often used metaphorically for the flooring of hell or the structures of suffering</note>. Just as there are these three deaths, even so there are **three lives** according to God’s counsels.

One life is that which is on earth. Then there is a second life that is in **God’s glory**. And the third life is in the world to come with all the saints. And death is to be dreaded for this reason: because no man can flee from it. And death is **inevitable** <term>inevitable (nydislic)</term> <note>literally "necessity-like" or "compulsory"</note>.

Death is inevitable because even if a man **hides himself** <note>original: "geþre hida"; likely a scribal variation of "gehydan"</note> with great **cunning** <note>original: "unscapa"; usually means "innocence" or "harmlessness," but here suggests a lack of means to be harmed or found</note> and in the deepest trench that may be in the middle of the sea, he must nevertheless die;

And death is therefore **unavoidable** <note>original: "unflic"; literally "un-flee-able"</note> even if a man should climb from some **iron-like part** <note>original: "isernican dæl"; a difficult phrase, possibly referring to a fortress or a specific rocky height</note> to the highest holes; nevertheless, death reaches him. And death is very manifold and **various** <note>original: "gerlic"; literally "year-like," meaning recurring or certain as the seasons</note>. It is manifold because even if a man should dwell in the middle of cities, and in the midst of his kinsmen, and between two hundred fighting men, then he must nevertheless die. And death is certain for this reason: that no man can prepare so many weapons [as to provide] protection, yet he cannot flee from death. Death is **impartial** <note>original: "unlic"; literally "unlike," in the sense that it does not distinguish between persons</note> because children and the old die alike. And death is **grievous** <note>original: "swæolic"; likely a variant of "swærlic" meaning heavy or oppressive</note> and it is master over all, for kings die and also enslaved men. And death is **alike** <note>original: "swilic"; likely meaning "the same"</note> and wise-like, for the wise die and the unwise. Therefore death is sorrowful and [also] joyful, because the sinful die...

Thanks be to the land where it is believed that there is great **room** <note>original: "rymet"; refers to the scope or space of the afterlife or divine judgment</note> for all men, for all human works, and for all great perverse works for which we know no one to take heed. Not all the kin of men can recount with their words how fair that **hail** <note>original: "hagol"; a strange descriptor for hail, perhaps metaphorical for divine power or a scribal error for "halig" (holy)</note> [is], as the saint says in the scriptures, [on the] way of the path of the weary ones, filled with prayers and the temperance of the weeping. Then at the last [there is] a cry against all their moisture [of spirits]... whoever now lives, it is revealed that he attains to the room. Ever... there will be for some to find... there is such her choice... yet her fire from the evil thoughts they cannot flee; then they fall. 

Then that quick **parting** <term>parting (gedal)</term> <note>referring to the separation of soul and body</note> from all the course... he does not heed before them nor their boast, unless he may give... God had... that which he gave to men. Then is that **third parting** that a man is parted from... every... and joyful play. For those worldly men [it is] not good. Then he... such foolish brothers... he is known in them. For men he comes to those who now [live] in the field of the world.

...and the ears from hearing, and the lips from speech, and the feet from walking, and the hands from work, and the inner organs from food <note>original: "fæste"; likely referring to the inability to take nourishment or the "fasting" of the body at death</note>.

That is the first likeness of hell.

And the breaths grow sluggish, those that were once quick in the harvest <note>original: "hprice on hripe"; likely a metaphor for the peak of physical activity</note>. The breath is soiled that before fed on [joy].

Then there is no gliding <note>original: "scriþinge"; perhaps referring to the movement of the soul</note> that may awaken him. <meta>This page continues the discourse on the nature of the afterlife from page 63 r. While the previous page focused on the physical sensations of the grave and the sheer scale of the infernal pit, this section transitions from a warning about the "pit of hell" into a vivid contrast with the joys of the heavenly kingdom. It concludes with the beginning of a famous apocryphal dialogue between a devil and an anchorite, a common motif in early medieval homilies used to illustrate the incomprehensible nature of spiritual reality.</meta>

->1,<-

concerning the pit of hell; because it is very great and very difficult to endure. But let us strive for ourselves, at least with inward prayers and with good deeds, so that we never need to go in thither into those fire-filled troubles and the powerful, fitting miseries to be found in hell. Instead, let us go thither up to that heavenly kingdom, for there is the glory that no man may tell with his words. 

**T**he joys there possess a natural life: day; life without death; good without evil; health without pain; peace without malice. 

**A**nd there shall be companionship without sadness; power without cowardice; there where no one can complain of weariness, nor is there any violent laughter <note>original: "swidlicer lohter"; in this context, it refers to discordant, mocking, or excessive mirth that disrupts the peace of heaven</note>. For there is the Almighty Lord, gentler and kinder than all other lights. There forever the wonderful harmonies <note>original: "dreames"; in Old English, "dream" refers to joy, music, or melody, specifically the celestial harmony of the spheres or angels</note> sound for us, and the glorious ranks of saints in their midst; there is the path to the three things of delight, and the satisfaction without any unpleasantness. Nor is there hunger or want there, nor cold nor burning, nor any unpleasant conflict. 

For as the devil spoke to the <term>anchorite (ancyran)</term> <note>A religious recluse or hermit who has retired from the world to live in solitary devotion</note> concerning the mystery of hell, as he <unclear>previously</unclear> testified concerning your needs... so the devil said to the anchorite: "Though you were the most golden of all men, thanking [God] at sunrise in the morning, and the other [part] covered all the earth, and the man might sit as precious as a royal child or a golden prince, and had every many-fold pleasure and wisdom, and to him all the treasures of the world were given in possession, with the joys that are throughout all this <term>middle-earth (middangeard)</term>..."

<meta>This page continues the description of the heavenly kingdom from page 64 r. The previous page detailed the sensory delights of heaven (peace, harmony, and the absence of suffering) as described by a devil to an anchorite. Page 64 v continues this discourse by using metaphors of beauty and stability to describe the celestial realm, specifically focusing on the company of the nine orders of angels and the saints, before concluding with an exhortation to avoid shame at the Last Judgment.</meta>

By that beauty might a bride be led to her bed; so is the beauty of this man <note>original: "monnes wlite"; likely referring to the transfigured soul of a righteous person</note> like the kingly clouds. It is like a stone tile in a wall from which they may fly; there, no other thing may ever perish or be consumed. Though all your earthly ornaments and glories might ring out, leading the way for that need; though all the darknesses were led away from him and all sweetnesses were brought to him, in fellowship with those spirits. 

Then one may live there without any adversity <note>original: "onwædnisse"; meaning change, misfortune, or opposition</note>, because there is only power in the kingdom of heaven. For he who previously believed in this glory in this life, he shall experience that other kingdom of heaven which he previously believed in through that one power, and he shall remember that glory of the heavenly kingdom. In that place, there shall be no joy of this world, because he may live there in a cycle of feasting and speech without any adversity among the nine orders <note>original: "nigone ænde byrdnessa"; referring to the traditional celestial hierarchy of Seraphim, Cherubim, Thrones, Dominions, Virtues, Powers, Principalities, Archangels, and Angels</note> of angels, which were created by God. 

There, for all the saints, it will be as if they are among the angels! 

<margin><gloss>after</gloss></margin> the great <term>apostles</term> who follow with the <term>martyrs</term>, they shall hasten to the coming of this <gloss>heavenly</gloss> <insert>city</insert>. Let it not then cause us shame when He approaches us and shows us how He wishes to give to us. For it is a great shame that a man should be ashamed of his pride at that great assembly <note>original: "myclan gemote"; the Last Judgment, where the secrets of all hearts are revealed</note>. Let us now hasten to recount what we must experience in our course, through those same things we may do good, so that we may clearly and with power behold the beauty of heaven. Since we wish to serve God with a right mind, nothing there is like the beauty of the kingdom of heaven. So be it.

<meta>This page continues the "Prosopopeia" or speech of God at the Last Judgment. Having established the duty of charity on the previous page, the homilist now records God's direct address to the soul of the wealthy sinner, challenging the human assumption of ownership over earthly goods.</meta>

My good man, my beloved <note>original: "minnu goda minnu leofra"</note>, lo, I created you and established you in love, so that everything that you possess, I gave to you. All of it is mine. Before you wasted it, dealing in your own affairs, you should have asked yourself what that same God's counsel did for the prosperous man. That [wealth] which I wrought with my own hands, I intended that you should use to feed my poor when they were begging you. 

My good man, you always refused them at that time; you would not pity them <note>original: "gesyhcan"; likely 'gesican', meaning to sigh for or feel compassion</note>, and you showed them harshness with your outcry. Then you should not have [lived for] the day—not at all—that day you lived for yourself <note>the text suggests a contrast between living for the worldly day and the eternal day</note>. When I created the clay <note>original: "læm"; referring to the 'lam' or loam/dust from which man was made</note> into a person... why did you hold it [wealth] only for yourself and your children? [but] it brings forth, and prospers, and blooms, and brings life [only by my will]. 

Now I take away all my help from you. Keep your stench <note>original: "stynne"; a common metaphor for the corruption of sin or the grave</note>, so that you may [give] to your earthly friends. My gifts—let them be your worth! And I take away from you my mercy <note>original: "mildheortnesse"</note>; then shall be quickly revealed the share of your wounds <note>i.e., the spiritual evidence of sins</note>.

From there I [sent] my gift; I willed that after these powers, [the land] should wither over your earth. If you wish to be... your earth, I hide my [face/mercy]...

->Omelia X<-

<meta>This page continues Homily X from the Blickling Homilies. The previous page established that those with great wealth and power have a greater responsibility before God, using the metaphor of a growing twig to describe the heavy burdens of high status. ...time. Though he may shine with that **splendid purple cloth**, and he may gleam with worldly gold, and though he be hung about on the outside with **precious gemstones**, nevertheless he shall not remain there for any length of time. And though the sorrowful ones build for him **frail vaults** <note>original: "hæccan"; likely referring to a burial chamber or a wooden coffin.</note> and build them from **marble stone** and from other golden ornaments, and [though it be] all fastened and wrought with kinds of gems, and all filled with precious **herbal mixtures** <note>original: "wyrt gemengnessum"; likely referring to embalming spices or aromatic herbs used to mask the scent of decay.</note> and adorned all around with gold leaves—nevertheless **bitter death** scatters all of that. Then is the [wealth] poured out and the [glory] broken; the gems fallen away and the gold scattered; the bodies decayed and turned to dust.

For that reason, there is nothing here in this middle-earth <note>original: "middangearde"; the inhabited world of men in Germanic cosmology.</note> of this world’s wealth; it is **transitory** and **swift** and **perishable**. So let us think where in this world are the **powerful emperors** <note>original: "caseras"; a term derived from 'Caesar,' used by Anglo-Saxons to denote the highest rank of imperial power.</note> and kings who were here long ago? Or the kings whom we once knew? Where are the **high officials** <note>original: "ealdormen"; the highest rank of nobility below the king, responsible for governing shires.</note> who directed the decrees? Where is their judgment seat? Where is their **arrogance** <note>original: "ofermetto"; literally 'over-measure' or excessive pride.</note> except hidden in the earth and turned into [the grave]?

This world is **fleeting** here... <note>The transcription at this point contains several OCR errors ("scni fclıı", "þullıce ꞃecce tunne"), but the context remains the 'ubi sunt' (where are they?) motif common in Old English poetry and sermons.</note> Where has the world’s abundance gone? Where has the **beauty of the earth** gone? Where have they gone, those who most eagerly strove for possessions and left them behind to others after their journey? So fleeting is the love that is allotted here.

Indeed, it is just like **night-showers** when they drop most heavily from the heavens and quickly all glide away; it is [gone] there...

<meta>This page continues the sermon for the second Rogation Day (the "Gang-days"), specifically focusing on the spiritual progression of virtues. The prophet said: **The beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord.** <note>original Latin: "Initium sapientiae timor dñi." (Psalm 111:10)</note> The beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord; from that fear of the Lord, there arises in us the compunction <term>compunction|Old English 'onbyrdnes', a spiritual "pricking" or stirring of the heart toward repentance and devotion.</term> of a humble heart. From that compunction, humility <term>humility|Old English 'æðmodnes'.</term> beckons, until humility physically leads us to cast out all the stains of this world, and then the power of virtue happily begins to grow from the fear of the Lord. This fear provides for us those things that are like a singing light for men, a life after death for those who think no evil, a light for those who were in darkness, and rest for those who were restless or troubled. Therefore, let this power of the body and mind come to us so that we may thrive, for he who lives and moves with the fear of the Lord is led away from the paths of hell <unclear>so that</unclear> He may let us into the heavenly kingdom; <unclear>so that</unclear> He may favor us in every secret thing; <unclear>so that</unclear> He may let us go into the fire of wisdom; <unclear>so that</unclear> He may know us in the judgment through this fear. **A good understanding have all they that do His commandments; His praise endureth for ever and ever. (A combination of Psalm 111:10 and Psalm 34:10)</note>

Let all those who wish to follow Him give thanks <unclear>so that</unclear> they may go to Him in prayer, which remains for ever and ever <term>forever|Old English 'in populda pupula', a phonetic corruption of the Latin 'in saecula saeculorum'.</term>. Because the Lord looks upon them, and they should not be afraid, for He protects them in the high places. Now hear, my beloved brethren <term>brethren|Old English 'hiġo', referring to the members of a religious household or community.</term>, that we should have the fear of the Lord always with us in our hidden eyes, along with the holy relics <term>relics|Old English 'reliqinay', from Latin 'reliquiae'.</term> of the Lord in this congregation. ## ->Omelia XIV<-
### ->Homily XIV<-

That daily, at the spiritual feeding through the mystery <term>geryne</term> <note>The Old English word "geryne" means a secret, a mystery, or a sacrament, specifically referring here to the Eucharist.</note> of our Lord’s holy church, the holy brothers in their necessary assembly have established the heavenly things for their works; so that then, most beloved, there is great necessity for those who dwell there that they pray without ceasing. This is so that through the mystery—away from heathenly, bodily loves—through manifold good and the divine mystery, He may heal and cleanse our soul and the tempering of our body. For that mystery of the Lord’s suffering <term>þrowinge</term> <note>Literally "throes" or "suffering," referring to the Passion of Christ.</note> tells us in many ways to imitate in our daily life that which He did. Then we are truly good servants of God if we wish to offer our spirit to Almighty God.

That is then also a great steadfastness of mind at the time when we pray—as much as we are able to struggle—that we always hold our mind to Almighty God in that beauty and in the right way, lest the idle turning of this world deceive our mind and turn it away from the divine beauty, that blessed power of the world, through neglect.

Likewise, it is for us also to think and to consider that a man should properly and rightly confess his sins. He who asks and desires peace from Almighty God should also, for the love of God, be willing to forgive whatever offense <note>Original: "æbylðe," meaning anger, offense, or cause of indignation.</note> anyone in the world commits against him. Our prayer will not be received by God as a good thing unless we have a heart clean from all malice and from all accusation of wickedness, just as the Lord himself said regarding this: *If you offer your gift at the altar* <note>original: "Si offer rurt muner tuum ad altare." This is a citation of Matthew 5:23.</note>. "You, man," He said, "if you bring your gift <term>lac</term> <note>A "lac" can be a gift, an offering, or a sacrifice.</note> to the altar <term>weofode</term> <note>The "weofod" is the "holy-table" or altar.</note>, and you there [remember that your brother has something against you...]" <unclear>i.e</unclear>

For this reason, our sacrifice will be unpleasing and worthless to God, and no good deeds will profit us at all, unless peace and love are held between us. Afterward, truly our Creator, Almighty God <term>ælmihtiȝ</term>, who searches our hearts and our minds, will understand; then our prayers and our offerings will be acceptable to Him, and He will mercifully release and forgive our sins. Then there was another man, that man’s fellow-servant, who owed him a hundred pence <note>Original: "penninȝa." This refers to the "hundred denarii" in the Gospel, a much smaller, manageable sum.</note>. Then he would not remit any part of that debt. <meta>This page continues the exposition of the Parable of the Unforgiving Servant from the previous page (79 r), moving from the specific requirements of the parable to a general exhortation on the necessity of forgiveness and the nature of the heavenly reward.</meta>

After <note>original: "Æfϯ" (Æfter). The symbol 'ϯ' is a common abbreviation for 'ter'.</note> these good words that we have now heard here, it is desired that we understand every part of them. It has been clearly said to us in those words that if we are not willing to forgive others, releasing the grievances <term>mæccan</term> <note>This term refers to the offenses or "debts" held against companions or equals.</note> of those men who sin against us, then the Lord—the glorious King who is stern over all creatures—will rightly reckon all our deeds in that grace-filled judgment, both the lesser and the greater. These deeds are those which we have committed in word or through our own merits.

Let us then, most beloved, think upon the time, the season of forgiveness <note>The preacher is referring to the current life as a window of opportunity for penance.</note> which now invites us and reminds us of our conversion. The same stern Lord awaits our deeds—He who softens the hardness of our minds with the teeth <note>A metaphor for the "biting" or sharp conviction of conscience.</note> of our sins. This present life is seen to be very troublesome and driven with manifold temptations, as the human condition is tossed about over all the earth. Therefore, there is for us a most great and significant necessity to pray and remind ourselves that we seek, with all our mind and all our strength, the place where the immortal life is. 

There, in heaven, no mourning or misery <term>nendrig</term> will be found, nor will any adversity be met. Instead, there will be eternal peace and mirth <term>mÿrȝþ</term>, bliss and more joy than any man may be able to relate or even know. If we wish to come to that understanding, let us then push away the death-bearing vices, first from our own skin <note>original: "hida". In this context, it means stripping away the outward layer of sin from the self.</note> and from unrighteous desires, and then from all sins in which the devil dwells and in which he trusts within the minds of the sinful.

# ANOTHER HOMILY ON THE DAY OF JUDGMENT.

**I**t tells us in these books how the holy Thomas, God’s apostle <term>apst</term> <note>Abbreviated from "apostol."</note>, asked our Lord when the time of the coming <note>original: "cwide" (speech/saying), here referring to the prophesied arrival of the end times.</note> would be. Then the Lord was speaking to him and thus said: "It shall happen to you that in the final time, when famine and sudden plague <term>fær-cwealm</term> shall appear, and there shall be a great time of distress <note>original: "nit geþonðu," likely referring to oppression or need.</note>, and there shall be many battles of peoples. And in those later times it shall seem more thorny <note>original: "þyrnestre"</note> than it previously seemed. That day is over all the world <term>middangeard</term> <note>The "middle-earth," or the inhabited world of men.</note>, and among young kings, and young popes, and young bishops, and young noblemen. And through that, discord shall then arise between two kings and brothers. It shall happen on that day—before the Great Day comes—that also my priests shall each please the other in his sin <note>original: "syrnece," possibly meaning "sin" or "garment/surplice."</note>, and they will not then have peace between them, but they will then deny me with a very greatly deceitful mind. ### HOMILY ON THE EPIPHANY OF THE LORD

We must now speak with some words concerning the presence of this holy season and this holy day. It was on this day, as is now written, that our Lord wished to be baptized <note>original: "gefulwad," meaning to be consecrated or "filled-full" through the rite of baptism.</note>. He did not do this because there was ever any sin or vice in him, such that the bath of baptism should wash it away; but he did it because he wished to establish an example for all mankind through himself toward eternal salvation. Therefore, Saint Matthew the Evangelist speaks of the day of this divine baptism and the order of this event, saying: "Jesus came from Galilee to the Jordan to John, so that he might be baptized by him" <note>original Latin: "Uenit ihs a galilea in iordane̅ ad iohanne̅ ut baptizaretur ab eo," quoting Matthew 3:13.</note>. The Savior <note>original: "hælend," the standard Old English word for Jesus, meaning "The Healer" or "The Savior."</note> came from the land of Galilee to the river Jordan to John <margin>and that</margin> then he wished to be baptized there by him. John <margin>hþ</margin> why [was] bathed <unclear>---</unclear> John <unclear>---</unclear> who rebuked <note>original: "þrede," likely a form of "þreade," meaning to admonish or rebuke.</note> him and said to him: "Behold, it is fitting <unclear>---</unclear> [that I be baptized by you] and yet you now come to me?" <note>This follows the dialogue in Matthew 3:14 where John the Baptist expresses his unworthiness to baptize Jesus.</note> Jesus responded and said...

->Homily XV and XVI<-

### Homily XVI

...ended and arranged, and so worked over them.

As we learn in the Gospels, he walked over the sea, keeping watch with his feet far out to his **apostles** <note>original: "upostolū," a variation of 'apostolum.'</note>. Likewise we also read in the Psalms that the waves recognized him, and the stream turned entirely backward.

Likewise also the **wood** <note>original: "widu." This refers to the wood of the Cross, which in medieval tradition was said to have trembled at the Crucifixion.</note> recognized him, and it therefore trembled and quaked because the living God suffered for the sake of men. And the **stones** <note>original: "stanas," though the OCR reads "scanas."</note> likewise recognized him, as we learn in books; it happened at the time when he suffered for mankind that the sunlight departed from its hours for a space, and many stones burst apart. **Golgotha** <term>Golgotha</term> <note>The site of the Crucifixion, often called "the place of the skull."</note>, the cliff where he was hanged, all burst apart so that the graves could not remain closed; for he, the Creator of all creatures, had his power within him.

Behold, even the **hell-dweller** <note>original: "hel-wita." This personifies Hell or the abyss as a creature that must yield to Christ.</note> recognized him, because she held those who were captive; then she, because of his coming, broke open the grave. Just as all other creatures recognized and acknowledged our Lord and [venerated] him, yet the **hard-hearted** <note>original: "heard-heortan." A common medieval trope contrasting the responsiveness of inanimate nature with the stubbornness of human disbelief.</note> Jews would not recognize him, but they were harder than any stones. 

For that reason they could never turn to their own salvation, but they wandered in their error. In contrast to that, let us **humbly** <note>original: "earmgallice," meaning wretchedly or with deep humility.</note> thank our Lord with all our hearts, because he through his mercy forgave us and for...

<meta>This page continues the sermon (Homily 16) following the description of Christ’s baptism. ...be [so] that we may more easily recognize and understand [it]. Indeed, we read spiritually that this fire which is placed before us is unique, and yet it fully possesses three things in a threefold manner. First, it has its own appearance, which is light, as we are able to see. Then there is the third <note>The text likely considers the flame itself as the second element, followed by the heat.</note> property: the heat that radiates outward, sometimes much wider than the flame itself. Yet it is still a single thing; no one can separate the heat from the light, nor can the heat be parted from the flame.

But it is one appearance, as we have read. And so, in this way, the **Holy Trinity** <term>Trinity</term> <note>original: "þrynnes," meaning "three-ness."</note> is Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, who is one God. And one divine substance, one might, one majesty, one glory, and one will. And so we must believe in the One alone, who is without any origin or beginning, because He never had an origin or a start. But He is the true God over all **creation** <note>original: "ge-sceaftum," referring to all things shaped or created by God.</note>, and all created things were made through Him and were [perfectly made]; and He now rules over all forever <note>original: "to widan feore," an idiom meaning "for a wide life" or "eternally."</note>. Because His kingdom and His power will never come to an end. And all people who are now here in the world, who wish to do what is truly right and remain in His commandments, may all, after this world, dwell and rejoice with Him eternally in His beautiful kingdom forever. Simeon then held him in his arms, and embraced him with both hands, and with all heart-love <note>original: "mod lufan," literally "mind-love" or "soul-love," expressing deep spiritual affection.</note> pressed him fast to his breast, and blessed and glorified God the Father Almighty and thus said: "My Lord, now let your servant depart in peace according to your words, as you previously promised him: because, Lord, my eyes have seen the salvation <note>original: "hælo," which can mean health, healing, or salvation.</note> which you prepared in the sight of all people, as a comfort to all nations, and the glory of your people, the children of Israel."

It is now for us to consider why Christ's parents <term>parents</term> <note>original: "aldoras," literally "elders," the standard term for parents or guardians in this manuscript.</note> brought him to God's temple on the fortieth day. Because it was God's commandment there, that the virgin of God—and indeed whatever woman who brought forth a child—might not go into the temple of God before the fortieth day. Instead, she should wait during that interval for the cleansing of her body; then, on that fortieth day, she should bring the child to God's temple, because all women who have given birth <note>original: "tymhde," meaning teeming or having brought forth offspring.</note> are subject to those commandments.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Presentation of Christ in the Temple. It concludes the explanation of Mary's ritual purity and moves into an allegorical explanation of the city of Jerusalem and the role of the Church in the believer's life.</meta>

# XIII

She was unblemished and clean without any sins. She remained in pure virginity <term>virginity</term> <note>original: "mægðhade," referring to Mary's state of perpetual virginity before and after the birth.</note>, and gave birth to Christ, so that after the birth she still remained in pure virginity. And for that reason she also brought Christ at that time to God’s temple, because she wanted the word to be fulfilled which Christ said: "I did not come into this world <note>original: "middangeard," literally "middle-earth," the common term for the human world between heaven and hell.</note> for the purpose of overthrowing or breaking God’s law <note>original: "æ," meaning the Old Testament law or covenant.</note>. Instead, I came for the purpose of fulfilling and expanding it."

Then it is also for us to know the name of the city which is named Jerusalem. In our own language, that is translated as the **Vision of Peace** <note>This reflects a common medieval interpretation of the Hebrew name Jerusalem as "visio pacis."</note>. 

So it is always for the steadfastly godly and the God-fearing men: in peace, there is opened up for them reward, and eternal salvation, and eternal excellence, and eternal bliss. This is for those people who, with peace and with faith and with God’s will, come often to God’s temple—that is, to the holy church of God—and there they commit themselves and their needs to Almighty God and His saints with holy prayers. And they praise and glorify God’s name, and they pray to Him with heartfelt repentance, so that these <unclear>......</unclear> from this world and also from these present powers. And through that, they merit from Almighty God that they may come to the true vision of peace, which is to the presence of our Lord Jesus Christ. Therefore, it is fitting for us that we always, at every time, through the love of peace...

<meta>This page continues the sermon on the Presentation of Christ in the Temple (Candlemas). This page applies that teaching to the specific sacrifice of birds mentioned in the Gospel of Luke.</meta>

by custom we ourselves ought to have and hold that within us, which the Lord <term>Lord</term> <note>original: "dryhten," the standard Anglo-Saxon word for God or a noble leader.</note> himself said concerning the peaceful men: "**Blessed are the peacemakers**, for they shall be called God's children" <note>A translation of Matthew 5:9.</note>. 

My brothers, it is for us to know that the sacrifice of the people under the Old Law <term>Old Law</term> <note>original: "ealdan æ," referring to the Mosaic Law of the Old Testament.</note> required that they should dutifully bring two turtledoves or two young pigeons as a sacrifice to God; such a sacrifice Christ’s parents brought with him to God's temple. 

In that, it was signified that our Savior Christ, for love of us, [came into] this world <note>original: "middangeard," literally "the middle-enclosure," the inhabited world of humans.</note> so that he might also, through his power, clothe us with blessed wealth in his eternal kingdom. And for love of us, he descended from the heavens to his birth. And he endured many kinds of trials and <unclear>hardships</unclear> while dwelling among men, until at last he suffered death for love of us. 

Therefore, he comes to that great necessity of love so that he might also redeem us from the death of hell’s torment. And he also gave us life and the eternal [life] after this coming world, if we are willing to merit it from him with good deeds and with noble works. 

The birds which we heard named there earlier—that is, the turtledoves and pigeons—they signify purity of life and innocence <note>original: "un-fecfulneſſe," meaning guilelessness or lack of deceit.</note>. So we should also...

**W**e can now speak with some few words
concerning the honor of this holy season,
and concerning the venerable birth, and concerning the life
of the holy bishop, the blessed Peter <note>original: "petri"; this is a scribal error by the original monk who wrote the manuscript. While the text says "Peter," it is actually the feast of Saint Martin, as the very next line clarifies.</note>, whose festival we now

Martin. He was very much chosen by God in his
virtues. He came into the world in the province of Pannonia <note>A Roman province covering parts of modern-day Hungary and Austria.</note>,
in the town of Sabaria <note>OCR: "æfnea."</note>. He was, however,
brought up in Italy, in the city [of Pavia]. According to the world, he was of
very noble birth; both his father and his mother
were heathens. His father was first a king’s thane <term>thane</term> <note>original: "þegn"; a member of the aristocratic warrior class who served a lord or king.</note> and then at
last he advanced so that he was a tribune <note>original: "tribunus"; a high-ranking Roman military officer.</note>, which is a leader
of the king’s thanes. Therefore, he, Saint Martin <note>original: "sēs martinus"; "sēs" is a standard abbreviation for the Latin "sanctus."</note>,
was forced from then on in his youth to be in the company
of the king’s thanes. And he served in the days of Constantine, and after
that in the day of Julian the Emperor. It was not at all that his
will was set upon following that worldly service, but he was
in God’s service, which he loved much more than
the joys and the wealth of this world. And when he was
ten years old, his parents urged him toward worldly
service; then he fled to God’s church, and prayed that
someone there might make him a catechumen <term>make him a catechumen</term> <note>original: "gecristnode"; this refers to the preliminary rite of being signed with the cross and becoming a candidate for baptism, rather than the baptismal ceremony itself.</note>.
That is the beginning and the first
part of holy baptism <note>original: "fulwihte"; literally "full-consecration."</note>. And then [God’s grace] was
wonderfully manifested throughout all his life in God’s service.

Death had truly taken him away so suddenly that he died <term>unbaptized</term> <note>original: "ungefulpad"; for a medieval audience, dying without baptism was a spiritual tragedy, as it was believed to prevent the soul from entering heaven.</note>.

When Saint Martin saw that the other brothers were all standing around the corpse in such sorrow, he wept and went to him; it seemed to him a very great tragedy that the man should have had to depart thus unbaptized. However, he spoke within his heart to the Almighty God, encouraged in all his mind and with <term>lamentation</term> <note>original: "hēofunge"; a deep, prayerful sobbing or sighing.</note>. When he had been in prayer for a long time, he perceived that a divine power and presence were there. It happened on one occasion <note>original: "þræce"; suggesting a specific event or time of pressure.</note> that he began to set fire to a certain <term>idol-shrine</term> <note>original: "deowol-gild"; literally "devil-offering" or "devil-shrine," reflecting the medieval Christian view that pagan idols were manifestations of demons.</note> that was very honored and famous among the heathen men. When Saint Martin saw that, he ran up onto the house and stood against the wind and <term>made the sign of the cross</term> <note>original: "sen̄e," a contraction of "senode"; a ritual gesture of blessing and protection.</note>. 

Just as the wind struck more strongly against the flame, so the flame broke back even more strongly against the wind, as if the <term>elements</term> <note>original: "gesceafta"; literally "creations" or "creatures," referring here to the natural forces of wind and fire.</note> were fighting between themselves. And thus the flame was manifested through Saint Martin’s prayers so that it did no harm to any other things, except for that idol-shrine alone which had begun to burn there. 

Likewise, another miracle happened similar to this. # XVIII

<margin>99</margin>
The heathen men came to him and they were all very angry. Then one of them was more fierce than the others were. This fierce man drew his sword and intended to slay him. When Saint Martin <note>Saint Martin of Tours (c. 316–397 AD), a former soldier turned bishop known for his humility and miracles.</note> saw that, he immediately moved the garment from his neck and offered it forward to the one who intended to strike him. When the heathen man raised up <gloss>his</gloss> hand and wished <gloss>to slay</gloss> him, he suddenly fell backward so that he had no control over his body; he was severely rebuked by divine power <term>godcunde mihte</term> <note>A phrase used to describe the supernatural intervention of the Christian God against physical threats.</note> until the blessed man, Saint Martin, prayed for his forgiveness. 

Likewise, there was again another miracle similar to this one. It happened once that he wanted to destroy an <term>idol-shrine</term> <note>original: "deofolgild"; literally "devil-offering." Early Christian writers used this to describe any non-Christian place of worship or the idols within.</note>; then one of the heathen men brandished his axe where the heathen man intended to attack him; then suddenly he did not know where the axe went that he previously held in his hands. It often happened then, when he destroyed the empty idols, that the heathens through wise words knew that he—through his teaching and through the gift of wonders—turned their hearts to God, so that they themselves with their own hands wished to break the empty idols. And he was so mighty in healing every infirmity, for such a great gift he received there from God, that...

there was no man so infirm who thought of him that he did not immediately receive health. In these things he was then most of all to be praised: that he never would yield or bow down through hateful <term>flattery</term> <note>original: "olic-tunᵹe"; specifically refers to sycophantic or deceptive praise used to gain favor.</note> to any worldly-powerful men or even to the king himself when it was right <note>Martin was famously independent and often challenged the authority of the Emperors, including Maximus, when it conflicted with Christian ethics.</note>; but he always wished to do what was good for all. For he is truly a blessed man, nor was there ever deceit or malice in his heart. Nor did he judge any unrighteously, nor did he punish, nor did he repay evil with evil to anyone. Nor did any man ever see him angry or fierce, but he was always of one constant mind, somewhat joyful and glad. One might always read his mind and perceive it on his face; and no man ever heard anything else from his mouth but Christ’s praise and wise speech, nor was there anything else in his heart but <term>steadfastness</term> <note>original: "an-fæstnesse"; a combination of "one" and "fastness," implying a singular, unwavering focus.</note> and mercy, and peace, and humility. <meta>The previous page concluded a homily on Saint Martin of Tours from the Vercelli Book (or a similar collection like the Blickling Homilies), transitioning from the rewards of the saints to a moral exhortation. This page begins a famous poetic sequence known as "Soul and Body II," where a condemned soul returns from the afterlife to rebuke its corpse for the sins committed during life.</meta>

INDEED, every man should hope for this: that he himself considers his soul's long journey—how solemn it will be when death comes and sunders the kinship that previously was joined together, the body and the soul. Long it is afterward that the <term>spirit</term> <note>original: "gast"; in Old English, this can mean spirit, ghost, or soul.</note> receives from God himself either punishment or glory, according to what he previously wrought in the world through his works. The spirit must come, crying out in grief, every seven nights to find the soul <note>The poet uses "sawle" (soul) and "gast" (spirit) somewhat interchangeably here to describe the visiting entity.</note> in the body that she long wore for three hundred <term>winters</term> <note>original: "wintra"; the Anglo-Saxons counted years by the number of winters passed.</note>—unless the King of the people, almighty God, the Lord of hosts, wishes to bring the end of the world sooner. And it calls out then with such a sorrowful, cold voice; the spirit speaks grimly to the dust:

"What have you done, wretched one? To what end did you trouble me, you fullness of earth, you all-decayed likeness of loam? Little did you care what should become of your soul's cause after it was led out from the body. Why do you reproach me with punishments, you <gloss>witness of sins</gloss>, you who are surely food for worms? Little did you think, when you satisfied all your grim lusts, how you must become a feast for worms here in the earth. Behold, in the world before, you thought little of how long this journey hither is! Look, the angel from the heavens above sent a soul to you through his own hand, the almighty <term>Measurer</term> <note>original: "meotod"; an ancient Germanic term for God as the one who "measures" out fate or the span of life.</note> from his majesty, and he bought it with his holy blood. And yet you bound me with hard hunger and imprisoned me in the punishments of hell. I dwelt within you; I could not [escape] you..."

# 102 r

encompassed by the coming flesh, and sinful lusts truly crowded me so that it very often seemed to me that it was thirty thousand <term>winters</term> <note>original: "wintra"; the Anglo-Saxons measured time by winters to emphasize the harshness of passing years.</note> until your death-day, so painfully did I await our parting. Indeed, the end now is not good. You were thirty years old, raging and full of pride, and yet thirsty for the spirit's drink <note>original: "gastes drynces"; a reference to the Eucharist or spiritual sustenance that the body neglected in favor of worldly pleasure.</note>, the body of the Good One [Christ]. For you did not consider here in life, while I had to dwell within you in the world, that you were encompassed and made sinful through the flesh and through wicked lusts, and yet you were guided through me; and I was a spirit sent to you from God. Never did you protect me from such hard hell-pangs through your greedy lusts. You shall suffer the shame of my sins on that Great Day <note>A common reference to the Last Judgment or Doomsday.</note> when all the race of men is gathered together. You are then no more dear as a companion to any of the <gloss>lying-ones</gloss> <note>original: "lygid wora"; referring to those who lived deceitfully.</note>, nor to mother nor father, nor to any kinsman, than the <term>glittering frost</term> <note>original: "freapra hwyft"; likely a metaphor for something beautiful but cold and useless to the dead, or perhaps a funeral shroud.</note> after I, alone, wandered out through the hand of that same one who previously placed me there. Neither your household nor your servants can take you hence from here, nor gold nor silver, nor any of your goods, nor your bride’s ring, nor your gold-plate, nor any of the riches that you once owned. But here your bones must wait, stripped and shorn of sinews; and I, your soul, must often seek you against my will—you who <insert>once</insert> reached out to me with words. You are now mute.

<meta>This page continues the speech of the condemned soul to its body from the Vercelli Book, specifically the poem "Soul and Body II." The soul continues its bitter rebuke, describing the body's decay and the impending terror of the Last Judgment where both must account for the body's earthly greed.</meta>

# 102 ᵛ

That your joys shall come to nothing. I must seek you out every four nights, pained by sins; yet I must soon depart from you at <term>cock-crow</term> <note>original: "han cræd"; the time of dawn when spirits were traditionally thought to return to their dwellings and when the morning office of the church began.</note>, when the holy power of life—praise-songs to God—seek those homes which you appointed for me here: those solitary dwelling-places. 

Many earth-worms shall chew upon you here, tearing you painfully; dark, greedy, and gluttonous, you are here. Your possessions, which you displayed here on earth to men, are worth nothing now. For it would have been much better for you by far than all the wealth of the earth—unless you had shared it for the <term>Lord</term> <note>original: "dryhtne"; a common Old English term for God, literally meaning a "warlord" or "noble protector."</note> himself—if you had been created as a bird at peace, or a fish in the sea, or if you had been a beast of the field seeking food, even though without wisdom, or in the wilderness the worst of wild animals, if God so willed it; or even if you were the fiercest of the serpent-kind, if God so willed it, rather than you ever becoming a man on earth or ever having to receive glory. 

For you must answer for us both on that Great Day when the wounds that sinful men formerly wrought in the world will be revealed upon them. Then the Lord himself will hear of the deeds of every person; He will <gloss>strip away the concealment</gloss> <note>original: "hæþþa ſcrippeð"; a difficult phrase suggesting the uncovering or stripping away of secrets or coverings.</note> from the words of every man's mouth, a repayment for the wound. 

Tell me, what will you say then to the Lord on that <term>Day of Judgment</term> <note>original: "dōmdæge"; Doomsday.</note>? When not even the smallest limb will be <gloss>uncovered</gloss> <note>original: "nacoda"; literally "naked," meaning no part of the body's history or actions can be hidden.</note>, you must pay the right penalty for every single one of those wounds.

<meta>This page continues the moralizing tone found in the Vercelli Book and the Exeter Book fragments. While the previous page focused on the specific dialogue between the soul and the body at the grave, this section shifts to a general parenesis—an ethical exhortation or sermon—warning against the hypocrisy of the "double-tongued" and the corruption of the physical world as the Judgment approaches.</meta>

Truly, many a man comes in various ways into the company of men; he who slanders another with malicious thoughts <note>original: "unþancum"; literally "un-thanks" or ill-will.</note> and yet speaks with fair words, speaks behind his back while appearing joyful to his face. Yet he has a hoard of deceit in his heart; that worm-eaten <term>spirit</term> <note>original: "gewit"; the mind or intellect, here described as "wormy" or corrupt.</note> is unclean and acts secretly with wickedness. 

Therefore the prophet <note>A reference to the Psalmist, likely King David.</note> said: "Do not deliver me together with the sinful into ruin, into the company of the wicked; nor leave me in this life with the workers of lies, those who speak so boldly but have evil thoughts hidden in their hearts." <note>This is a vernacular translation of Psalm 28:3.</note> They hold themselves away from holy hope. Woe is in the mind of the one who is stained by perverse joys, mixed with enmity, and filled with deceit, even though he speaks fair words outwardly. Early on he turns away from them... they are never truly together... 

...that <note>original: "zeȝel"; likely a transcription error for "beo" (bee).</note> bee has honey in its mouth, but behind it has a poisonous root <note>original: "fynıȝume ƿirt"; a stinging or venomous plant/part.</note> that sometimes wounds. When the slaughter comes, so it is with those who promise with double tongues and fair words, yet think deceitfully; then at last they cruelly deceive. They have the taste of honey in their speech, but inside they rip with a secret wound through the craft of the devil. 

So is this <term>middle-earth</term> <note>original: "middangeard"; the physical world of humans, famously used by J.R.R. Tolkien but common in Old English to describe the space between Heaven and Hell.</note> now mixed with evil; it wanes, it wastes away, it weakens. The world grows old and goes astray; its days pass quickly. Among the strength of men, honor vanishes; they are plagued by pride. No one trusts another, nor does any man truly hold peace or love with his neighbor. God's message is ignored... because the hope of the world is entirely turned to sin.

All that beacon was covered with gold; gems stood fair at the corners of the earth; likewise there were five up on the shoulder-span <note>The cross-beam of the crucifix.</note>. All the angels of the Lord, fair through eternity, gazed upon it there. That was indeed no felon’s gallows <note>original: "fracoðes gealga"; literally a "wicked man's gallows." The poet contrasts the Roman execution tool with the divine reality.</note>, but holy spirits gazed upon it there—men across the earth and all this glorious creation.

Wondrous was that victory-tree, and I, stained with sins, wounded with blemishes, saw the tree of glory, adorned with garments, shining joyfully, girded with gold; gems had worthily covered the Lord's tree. # XV

...with wide coverings and colors. At times it was drenched with moisture, soaked with the flow of blood <note>original: "swates gange"; literally "flow of sweat," but in Old English poetic tradition, "swat" is a frequent metaphor for blood, emphasizing the physical exertion and agony of the passion.</note>; at times it was adorned with treasure.

"That was long ago—I remember it still—that I was hewn down at the forest’s edge, moved from my trunk. Strong enemies seized me there, made me a spectacle <term>spectacle</term> <note>original: "wæfersyne"; a sight to be gazed at, often referring to a public show or a mocking display.</note> for themselves, commanded me to lift up their criminals. Men bore me there on their shoulders, until they set me on a hill; many enemies fastened me there.

I saw then the Lord of mankind hasten with great courage, because he wished to climb upon me. There I did not dare, against the Lord's word, to bow or burst, when I saw the corners of the earth tremble. I could have felled all the enemies, yet I stood fast.

Then the young hero stripped himself—that was God Almighty—strong and stout-hearted. He climbed onto the high gallows, brave in the sight of many, when he wished to redeem mankind. I trembled when the man <note>original: "beorn"; a term for a warrior or nobleman, here casting Christ in the role of a Germanic hero-king.</note> embraced me; yet I dared not bow to the earth, fall to the corners of the ground, but I had to stand fast.

A rood <term>rood</term> <note>original: "Rod"; the Old English word for a pole or cross, from which we get "Holyrood."</note> was I raised up. I lifted up the mighty King, the Lord of heavens; I dared not stoop. They drove me through with dark nails; on me the wounds are visible, open malicious gashes. I dared not harm any of them. They mocked us both together. I was all drenched with blood, poured from the man's side after he had sent forth his spirit.

Many cruel fates I have endured on that hill. I saw the God of hosts violently stretched out. Darkness had covered the Ruler's corpse with clouds, the bright radiance. A shadow went forth, dark under the clouds. All creation wept, lamented the King's fall; Christ was on the cross.

# XV

...had stood. Those warriors <note>original: "hilde rincas"; literally "battle-warriors." The poem consistently uses military language to describe both Christ's followers and his executioners, framing the Passion as a cosmic battle.</note> did not leave me; I stood there drenched with moisture, all wounded with arrows. They laid him down there, limb-weary; they stood at the head of his body; they beheld there the Lord of Heaven, and he rested himself there for a while, weary after that great struggle.

Then the men began to fashion an earth-dwelling <term>earth-dwelling</term> <note>original: "moldern"; a poetic compound for a grave or tomb.</note> for him in the sight of his slayers; they carved it out of bright stone; they placed the Lord of Victories therein. Then they began to sing a song of sorrow, wretched in the evening-time, when they wished to journey back, weary, from the glorious Prince. He rested there with a small company <note>original: "mæte weorode"; this is an example of *litotes* or Germanic understatement—he is "resting" with a "small company" because he is dead and alone in the tomb.</note>.

Yet we <note>The "we" refers to the three crosses on Calvary.</note> stood there weeping in our places for a good while, after the voices of warriors went up. The corpse grew cold, the beautiful dwelling of the soul. Then someone began to fell us all to the earth. That was a dreadful fate! We were buried in a deep pit; yet the Lord’s thanes, his friends, heard of me there and adorned me with gold and silver.

Now you may hear, my beloved hero, that I have endured the work of evildoers, of sore sorrows. Now the time has come that men far and wide over the earth honor me, and all this glorious creation; they pray to this sign. On me the Son of God suffered for a time; therefore I now tower glorious under the heavens, and I am able to heal each one of those who holds me in awe.

Long ago I was the hardest of punishments, most hateful to people, before I opened the right path of life for them, for those who have voices <term>voice-bearers</term> <note>original: "reord berendum"; a kenning for human beings, defined by their ability to speak.</note>. Lo, the Prince of Glory, the Guardian of the heavenly kingdom, honored me then above all the trees of the forest, just as he, Almighty God, also honored his mother Mary herself for the sake of all men, above all the race of women.

Now I command you, my beloved hero, that you tell this vision to men; reveal in words that it is the tree of glory on which Almighty God suffered for mankind's many sins and Adam's ancient deeds. He tasted death there; yet the Lord arose again with his great might to help mankind. He then ascended into the heavens; he will come back here to this middle-earth to seek mankind on...

# The Day of Judgment and the Dreamer’s Hope

...on the Day of Judgment <note>original: "domdæge"; literally "doom-day."</note>, the Lord himself, Almighty God, and his angels with him, will come so that he who holds the power of judgment may then judge each person according to what they have earned earlier here in this fleeting life. No one there can be without fear at the word which the Ruler shall speak. He shall ask before the multitude where the man might be who, for the Lord’s name, would be willing to taste bitter death, just as he did earlier on that tree.

But they shall then be afraid, and few will think what they might begin to say to Christ. Yet no one needs to be afraid who carries in their breast the best of signs <note>original: "beacna selest"; a reference to the sign of the Cross as a spiritual safeguard.</note>. Instead, through the Cross, every soul who intends to dwell with the Ruler must seek the kingdom from the earthly way.

Then I prayed to that tree with a joyful spirit and great courage, there where I was alone with a small company. My soul was inspired for the journey forward; I have endured many times of longing. Now my life’s hope is that I may seek that tree of victory <term>tree of victory</term> <note>original: "sigebeam"; a poetic compound or 'kenning' for the Cross, emphasizing Christ's triumph over death.</note> alone, more often than all other men, to honor it well. My desire for that is great in my heart, and my hope for protection <note>original: "mundbyrd"; in Anglo-Saxon law, this referred to the protection or guardianship a lord provided to his followers.</note> is directed to the Cross.

I do not have many powerful friends on earth, for they have gone forth from here, departing from the joys of the world; they sought the King of Glory. They live now in heaven with the High Father, dwelling in glory, and I hope every day for the time when the Lord’s Cross, which I previously looked upon here on earth in this fleeting life, may fetch me and bring me then to where there is great bliss, joy in heaven, where the Lord’s people are seated at the feast, where there is perpetual joy. 

And may he then set me where I may afterwards dwell in glory, well among the saints, enjoying their joys. May the Lord be a friend to me, he who previously suffered here on earth on the gallows-tree for the sins of man; he redeemed us and gave us life, a heavenly home. Hope was renewed with blessings and with bliss for those who suffered the fire <note>original: "bryne þolodan"; this likely refers to the souls in the "Harrowing of Hell" who were rescued by Christ after his death.</note>. The Son was victorious on that journey, mighty and successful, when he came with a multitude, a host of spirits, into God’s kingdom—the Almighty Ruler—to the joy of angels and all those saints who previously dwelt in heaven in glory when their Ruler, Almighty God, came to where his homeland was.

<summary>The poem concludes with a vision of Judgment Day and the Dreamer's personal devotion to the Cross as his primary hope for salvation. It ends with an account of Christ's victorious return to heaven after the Harrowing of Hell.</summary>
<keywords>Judgment Day, salvation, sigebeam (victory-tree), mundbyrd (protection), the afterlife, Vercelli Book, Anglo-Saxon devotion</keywords>

->Homily XIX<-

**BELOVED** <note>original: "MENda liofostan"; a standard opening for Old English sermons, literally "Most beloved men."</note> people, it is fitting for us that we remember and think upon how Almighty God created the heavens and the earth and all creatures—He in whom we must believe as threefold in persons and single in power. One is the Father, the Almighty Father. Another is the Almighty Son. Another is the Almighty Holy Spirit. And yet, we must confess one God in majesty, and in power, and in divinity, because the Father is eternal God <note>original: "æðe god"; likely a scribal variant of "ece" (eternal) or "soð" (true).</note>, and the Son is eternal God, and the Holy Spirit is eternal God. All three—the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit—possess one personhood <term>personhood</term> <note>original: "hād"; used here to describe the distinct "persons" of the Trinity, though the scribe here emphasizes their unified state.</note> and one will. And these three are one God. From these things then, all things were brought forth that are manifest, and all those that are hidden <note>original: "ƿyrþne... ungewyrþne"; literally "worthy" and "unworthy," but in this theological context, it refers to the visible and invisible worlds.</note>; no earthly man can recount them all.

First, in majesty He created the heavens and the earth, and then all the things that are in them: all the angels that are in the heavens, and all those who were cast out from these worlds. He blew them all out from Himself with His breath. He who was the most arrogant <note>original: "mænostne"; implying either the most wicked or the most prideful.</note> among them, and all the other angels whom Lucifer has there—that is, in our language, called the "Light-bearer" <term>Light-bearer</term> <note>original: "leoht berend"; a literal Old English translation of the Latin name Lucifer (lux + ferre).</note>. But he was such that he wished to make himself like his Creator, and wished to build for himself a more excellent throne in the north part of the kingdom of heaven. Because of this, he fell down, and all those who were in that counsel were with him and fell after him. They all changed from angels to devils and were thrust into the thick darkness of hell, where they suffer in their own torment because they despised their Creator, Almighty God.

Afterward, He created from the clay <note>original: "lame"; literally "loam" or "mud," referring to the biblical creation of man from the dust of the ground.</note> of the earth the first <unclear>man</unclear> <unclear>Adam</unclear> there, whom He named by name, and He breathed into him a <unclear>living</unclear> spirit. And He <unclear>gave</unclear> into his power all the creatures of the earth, so that they should be obedient to him. And he created names for them all, both for the beasts and the birds and the fish; and they all still <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note> by those names...

And God forbade them that they might have power over all that was on earth, except for the fruit of one tree which is named the Tree of Life. That tree is in the middle of Paradise <note>original: "neorxna wange"; a uniquely Old English term for the Garden of Eden, possibly meaning "the plain of the spirits."</note>. Concerning this, Christ Himself spoke and forewarned both Adam and Eve, and told them that on whatever day they ate the fruit of that tree, they should perish. 

They did so; for the devil did not boldly allow that God had given them so much, because he himself was deceived, along with those who fell with him from the heavens. Then he thought to deceive them, just as he himself was deceived; he changed himself then into the form of a serpent and thus deceived and misled them. He said to them that on whatever day they ate the fruit of the tree, they would thereafter be like the one who created them. 

But it was bitterly repaid to them in their lives, and also afterwards, both to them and to all mankind who came from them until our Lord’s coming. Nine hundred and thirty years <note>original: "wintra"; Old English texts frequently count years as "winters."</note> Adam lived in this world in struggle and in toil, and afterwards went to hell. And there he suffered grim punishments for five thousand and thirty-two years. <note>In medieval theology, the time between Adam's fall and Christ's "Harrowing of Hell" was often calculated to be over 5,000 years.</note>

Now, beloved ones, there is now for us a great necessity, since we are all thus together through God’s power, that we turn from evil to good and hear with all humility these divine teachings. [Let us] help against sins, and pray to God and all His holy powers, lest the devil fight against us after our departure <term>departure</term> <note>original: "forð siðe"; a common euphemism for death, literally meaning a "journey forth."</note>. ### Homily XIX

...companions. But let us turn now, we the living, to better things, and leave all evil things, and do good as far as we are most able, and there eagerly serve God and all His saints. It is necessary for us to eagerly know and to hear for what reason the **Gang-days** <term>Gang-days: Also known as Rogation Days (from the Latin 'rogare', to ask), these are the three days of prayer and fasting preceding Ascension Thursday. The Old English name 'gang-dagas' refers to the processional 'goings' or 'walks' through the parish boundaries.</term> are hallowed, and why these three days first begin before God. The first is hallowed to the Lord Himself for His glorious ascension from earth to heaven, and we must learn with what virtues we ought to observe them. 

We must eagerly know that they should be observed in great **humility** <note>original: "eaðmodnysse"</note> and in great patience, and in good love, and in all purity of body and soul, and in good vigils by night and day, and in fasts, and in holy prayers, and in alms-deeds, and in all goodness and in the love of God and men. To many holy men these Gang-days are very venerable, but it would take us too long to recount all of that; however, let us eagerly shield ourselves against the devil at all times and pray for God's mercy against his evil teachings day and night. 

The devil fights against us day and night with his hateful following. Let us turn against him with faithfulness. He fights with pride; let us [stand] against him with **humility**. He sharpens [lust] for us; let us [stand] against him with **purity**. He weaves wickedness; let us [stand] against him with **righteousness**. He brings anger upon us; let us follow **patience**. He [urges] us to gluttony; let us practice **mercy**. He reminds us of greed; let us go forth there to hold [the faith] against heathenism. And ever against his evil will, let us work God’s will. 

We all should hasten <note>original: "efstan"</note> to God's temples as quickly as the **bees do to their hive**, so that we may knowingly taste the sweetness of the **priestly honey** from the divine words which are taught by the most learned, and which we read in holy books: that whoever in these three days comes to God’s temple and prays to God for the forgiveness of his sins with all his heart's devotion and with all humility, that God will grant him the forgiveness of his sins...

It uses the vivid metaphor of bees seeking a hive to describe how the congregation should hasten to church to hear the "honey" of God's word.</summary>

on the third day of the Easter-month <note>April</note>. It is strongly written that no mortal man <note>original: "nan manna lame," literally "no man of clay"</note> should despair that God will not have mercy on him, even though he may have committed many sins against His will. <meta>This folio marks a transition in the Vercelli Book from the homiletic material (the Rogation Day sermons mentioned on the previous page) to the poetic section. The text here is "Soul and Body I," a dramatic address from a condemned soul to its corpse. While the previous page discussed the ritual of fasting and the story of Jonah, this page shifts to a grim meditation on the afterlife and the physical decay of the body.</meta>

And death shall not avoid your joys at all. I must depart from you at night, forced by necessity, saddened by sins; and soon I must turn away from you at dawn <note>original: "hancred," the time of cock-crow or early morning</note> when holy men sing songs of praise to the living God, to seek those homes which you assigned to me here, and those lonely dwelling-places. And here many earth-worms shall chew upon you; they shall tear at you, dark and sallow creatures, biting, ravenous, and greedy. Your possessions, which you once displayed to men here on earth, shall not help you at all; for they are now a much greater burden than all those empty riches would have been, unless you had shared them for the sake of the Lord Himself. 

It would have been better for you if you had been at the beginning a bird, or a fish in the sea, or if you had gathered food in the wild marshes, a beast of the field <note>original: "feld-gangende," literally "field-going"</note> though without wisdom, or the worst of wild animals in the wilderness—if God had willed it so—or even if you were of the species of worms, the most grim of all—if God had willed it—rather than that you ever became a man on this earth, or ever received **baptism** <term>Baptism: In Old English "fulwihte," meaning full consecration or "full-washing."</term>. 

Because you must answer for us both on that **Great Day** <note>The Day of Judgement</note>, when the wounds of men shall be revealed, those which were previously hidden in the world; then men shall be turned through iron <note>original: "þorh iron"; this is a difficult phrase that may refer to the piercing nature of divine judgment or the "iron" of a soul's chains</note>. Then the Lord Himself will hear the deeds of every hero. Lo! The Creator shall then give to every man a measure of retribution. What will you then say to the Lord on that **Day of Doom**? For then there shall not be any joint so small grown upon a limb <note>original: "nan na to þæs lytel lið on lime awaxen"</note> that you shall not have to account for it individually and rightly.

He eats through the eyes from above in the head, opening a path for other worms, a feast for worms once that doomed body has grown cold. That which was long before covered with garments is then the food of worms in the earth; that may be a reminder of mortality to every wise-minded man.

<meta>This page transitions from the spiritual dialogue between the soul and body to a didactic, moralizing poem. While the previous page focused on the specific fate of a "Blessed Soul," this text (identified as "Ritmo IV" or "Poem IV") shifts to a general warning against human hypocrisy and the dangers of deceitful speech, using nature metaphors to illustrate moral failings.</meta>

<margin>107.</margin>
Many a truth comes in various ways. In the mouth of everyone, it is found that one person blames another with resentment and with **deceitful words**; they speak fairly to one's face, yet behind the back they harbor a deceit that they nevertheless hold as an **unclean hoard** <note>original: "hord unclæne"; Old English poetry often uses "hoard" to describe thoughts or treasures stored in the "mind-chest"</note> in their heart. Then that thorny greed becomes a servant of [dark] things. Therefore the prophet <note>Likely referring to the Psalmist (Psalm 26:9 or 28:3)</note> said: "Do not deliver me together with the sinful into the destruction of punishments, nor leave me in the tracks of the **lie-workers** <note>original: "ligewyrhtum," literally those who 'work' or create lies</note>, those who have very smooth speech and in their thoughts... [they] hold [deceit] faithfully so that their hope does not prosper." Those who are [practiced] in evil ways are stained in their minds with crimes, blended with secret mysteries and filled with **treachery**, though they may show fair words outwardly, like a unique bath. It is just as the **bee** <note>original: "bion"; the bee was a common medieval symbol for the hypocrite—sweet in speech but sharp in deed</note> behaves: it has honey in its mouth, but a stinging tail behind. It has honey in its mouth, a pleasant herb, but sometimes they [sting] with it. When death comes, so it is for those liars who promise with double tongues, but deceitfully sigh with needy words; then at the last, they most nearly betray. They have honey to the taste, a smooth speech of peace on the roof [of the mouth], but inside, through the **devil’s craft**, they carry a secret wound. So now many in this middle-earth <note>original: "middangeard," the inhabited world of men situated between heaven and hell</note> are blended [with evil]; the old one <note>The Devil</note> wanders and deceives, betraying daily with wicked malice... [he] sows malice in the heart... so that no other [person] remembers in their spirit that they should truly and safely hold the **love** <note>original: "luþe," a variant of "lufu"</note> that God commanded him. Therefore, all hope [in this] world-toil [fades]...

# XV

...changing in garments and colors; at times it was soaked with moisture, drenched with the **flow of blood** <note>original: "swates gange"; while "swat" literally means "sweat," in Old English poetry it is a standard metaphor for blood</note>, at times adorned with treasure. "It was long ago—I remember it still—that I was hewn down at the forest's edge, moved from my trunk. Strong enemies seized me there, made me into a **spectacle** <term>spectacle: the original "wafer-syne" implies a public show or sight, often used for a theatrical or mocking display</term> for themselves, commanded me to lift up their criminals. Men bore me there on their shoulders, until they set me upon a hill; many enemies fastened me there. Then I saw the Lord of mankind hasten with great zeal, for he wished to climb upon me.

There I did not dare, against the Lord's word, to bow or burst asunder, when I saw the corners of the earth tremble. I could have felled all those enemies, yet I stood fast. Then the **young hero** <note>original: "geong hæleð"; the poem strikingly depicts Christ not as a passive victim but as a Germanic warrior-hero voluntarily entering battle</note> stripped himself—that was God Almighty—strong and resolute. He ascended onto the high gallows, brave in the sight of many, when he wished to redeem mankind.

I trembled when the **warrior** <term>warrior: the original "beorn" is a poetic term for a man of rank or a soldier, further emphasizing Christ's heroic status</term> embraced me. Yet I dared not bow to the earth, fall to the ground's surface, but I had to stand fast. I was raised as a Cross. I lifted up the mighty King, the Lord of the heavens; I dared not bend.

They drove dark nails through me; on me the wounds are visible, open gashes of malice. I dared not injure any of them. They mocked us both together. I was all drenched with blood, poured from the man's side, after he had sent forth his spirit.

Many cruel fates have I endured on that hill. I saw the God of hosts violently stretched out. Darkness had covered the Ruler’s corpse with clouds, its shining light. A shadow went forth, wan under the clouds. All creation wept, lamented the King's fall; Christ was on the Cross.

The heathen men looked on; and they were, however, so overcome by the divine power to that sanctuary that none of them could or dared withstand him, but they all made known their belief in the Lord. And they spoke thus: "That he was the **true God** who heard Martin, and that their heathen idols were all idle and useless, because they could help neither themselves nor any of those who desired any help from them."

Many of the heathen men <note>original: "hæðenra manna"</note> [came] to him, and they
