--- Page 302 ---
<meta>This page continues the life of Saint Martin of Tours. The previous page recounted how Martin was miraculously protected from physical attacks by heathens—once when a man's arm was paralyzed mid-strike and another when a knife vanished from an attacker's hand.</meta>

No man was so infirm <note>original: "untrum," meaning weak or ill</note> that he thought he [Martin] might not receive healing from him. It also often happened that when someone brought the fringes of his clothing <note>original: "liffeceda," likely referring to the threads or decorative lappets of his cloak</note> to a very sick man, he was then healed through that. In this matter he was the most steadfast of all to be seen: that he never, for any worldly powerful man or for the king himself, would bow in favor through false flattery <term>false flattery: "leas olihtung," a term used to describe insincere praise given to those in power</term> when it was right [to stand firm], but he would do what was right before God for all. For this truly blessed man was never such that he did not live [with God] in his heart; nor did he judge any man unjustly, nor did he punish them. He never repaid any evil with evil, nor did any man ever see him angry or fierce of heart; but he was always of one mind, and rejoiced in a peaceful, heartfelt bliss. One could always see his state of mind by perceiving his countenance. No man ever heard anything else from his mouth but the praise of Christ and speech of peace, nor was there anything else in his heart but piety <term>piety: "arfæstnesse," encompassing both devotion to God and mercy toward others</term>, mercy, peace, and humility <note>original: "eadmodnesse"</note>. Likewise, this blessed man, Saint Martin <note>original: "sce. martinus"</note>, long before he knew the day of his departure <note>original: "forðsiðe," a common Old English euphemism for death, literally a "forth-journey"</note>, the Lord had revealed it to him; he then told his brothers that it was now rightly the time that he should depart from this world.

the heavenly kingdom <note>original: "heofonlican rice"; this phrase completes the thought from the previous page regarding Martin's transition to the afterlife</note> and not merely what you now hear 
concerning how holily this blessed man, Saint Martin, lived his <gloss>life</gloss> for God during the time that he was in this world. Through his fair deeds, he thrived there with our Lord; and now, for as long as this world stands, his goodness is celebrated throughout the whole world <note>original: "middangeard," the middle-dwelling or earth</note> in God’s church. And he now, with all the saints, rejoices eternally in the kingdom of heaven before the sight of the Lord’s presence. But let us strive, then, that we might imitate the life and the deeds of this holy servant, Saint Martin, as far as we are able. Let us pray to him that he might be our intercessor <term>intercessor: "þingere," an advocate or one who pleads on behalf of another, especially in a legal or spiritual sense</term> in heaven with our Lord, now that we honor him on earth throughout the world. May the Lord help us toward that, so that we may live <note>original: "leofas," likely from the root for "beloved" or a variation of "lifian" (to live)</note> forever, world without end. Amen.

<summary>This page concludes the biography of Saint Martin, praising his holy life and eternal reward in heaven while exhorting the faithful to imitate his deeds and seek his intercession. It ends with a traditional liturgical blessing.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Martin, intercession, heavenly kingdom, imitation of saints, eternal life, Blickling Homilies</keywords>

--- Page 306 ---
<meta>This page marks a significant transition in the manuscript from the prose homily concerning Saint Martin of Tours to the Old English poem known as "Soul and Body I." While the previous page focused on the saint's transition to his heavenly reward, this text shifts to a grim "memento mori," reminding all people of the eventual separation of the spirit from the flesh and the accountability of the physical body.</meta>

->SOUL AND BODY I<-
->(The Spirit's Address to the Body)<-

**INDEED**, every man must take heed, every hero, that he himself consider his soul’s journey, how deep it is when death comes and separates the kinship <note>original: "sybbe"; refers to the intimate bond or "peace" between the physical and spiritual components of a person</note> which previously were together—the body and the soul. Long it shall be after the spirit receives from God himself either punishment or glory, exactly the reward that he previously earned in the world. 

The spirit must come, frosty with sorrows <note>original: "geohðum hrimig"; a haunting image suggesting the cold, desolate state of the wandering soul</note>, always every seven nights to find the body <gloss>the corpse</gloss> that it long wore for three hundred winters <term>winters: "wintra," the standard Anglo-Saxon measurement for years</term>, unless the King of the nation, almighty God, the Lord of hosts, wishes to bring an end to the world sooner. And then it calls out so sorrowfully, and speaks with a cold voice; the spirit speaks grimly to the dust: 

"Lo, you weary one, why did you labor so? You foulness of the earth, you are all decayed, a likeness of clay. Little did you consider what would become of your soul’s state after you were led out of the body. Why do you perhaps blame me? Indeed, you morsel for worms! Little did you think when you followed all your terrible lusts how you must become a feast for worms in the earth. Lo, little did you think in the world how long-lasting this is. Behold, the Lord sent a soul into you from the clouds, by His own hand, the Almighty Creator from His majesty, and bought you with His holy blood. But you bound me with hard hunger and forced me, a prisoner, toward the punishments of hell. I dwelt within you, nor could I [leave] you..."

<summary>This passage begins the poem "Soul and Body I," depicting the soul returning to its former body to reproach it for its earthly sins and neglect of the spiritual life. It emphasizes the physical decay of the body and the eternal consequences of earthly conduct.</summary>
<keywords>Soul and Body, Vercelli Book, memento mori, eschatology, spiritual accountability, Old English poetry</keywords>

--- Page 307 ---
<meta>This page continues the Old English poem "Soul and Body I" from the Vercelli Book. The previous page introduced the "frosty spirit" returning to its corpse after death. This page contains the heart of the soul's bitter reproach, where it blames the physical body for their shared damnation, contrasting the body's former worldly pleasures with its current decay.</meta>

->Rhythm III, verses 33-65<-
->(Soul and Body)<-

...enveloped in common flesh. To me, you were the worst of loves; it seemed to me very often that it was thirty thousand winters <term>winters: "wintra," the standard Anglo-Saxon measurement for years</term> until your death-day. I waited for my share of hardships all too painfully; now let [all] here listen to you... <note>The OCR "ande-god" likely refers to "all here in the world" or a corrupted address to the body</note>. You were then firm, you were bold; you thundered with full glory. It is now the worse that it seems [you were] a guest of God's body <note>original: "godes lichoman gustes"; an ironic reversal where the soul feels like an unwelcome guest in the body it was meant to inhabit</note>.

Because you did not consider here in life, while your course among the people was to endure, that you were strongly constrained through flesh and through the lusts of sins. You should have [known] through me—I, a spirit sent from God—that you oppressed me with such hard hellish <unclear>bonds</unclear> through the lust of your mind. 

Now you must suffer such great shame on that great day <note>Judgment Day</note> when all of mankind will necessarily be gathered together; nor can any of those left behind there be a protector for me, neither mother nor father, nor any relative. Then [shall come] the greedy hell, after I, a lone breath, departed through the hand of the Creator <note>original: "seftes," likely a scribal variant for "Scyppendes," the Shaper or Creator</note> in whom I was previously established. 

Now neither ornaments nor robes can take you from here, nor gold nor silver, nor any of your goods, nor your bride’s ring, nor your gold-wealth, nor any of the goods that you possessed. But here your bones shall remain, broken by necessity, which your soul must, often against my will, seek out, struggling with words even as you acted toward me. Now you [taste] death...

<summary>A damned soul continues its verbal assault on its own corpse, lamenting the 30,000 years it felt it suffered in the flesh and mocking the body's now-useless earthly wealth and jewelry.</summary>
<keywords>Soul and Body, Vercelli Book, Judgment Day, mortality, memento mori, corruption of the flesh, Anglo-Saxon poetry</keywords>

--- Page 308 ---
<meta>This page continues the Old English poem "Soul and Body I" from the Vercelli Book, specifically the soul's direct address to its decaying corpse. On the previous page, the soul lamented its imprisonment within the "house of flesh" and the long wait for the body's death. Here, the soul describes the nightly visits it is forced to make to the grave and expresses a wish that the body had been born as a mindless animal rather than a human endowed with a soul and the responsibility of baptism.</meta>

...[your] habits need not humble you at all; yet I must always seek you out in the night, though with hostility, wounded as I am by sins; and then I must immediately turn away from you at cock-crow <note>original: "hancred"; the dawn signaled the time when spirits were traditionally thought to return to their dwellings</note>, when holy men sing songs of praise to the living God. [I must] seek those homes which you appointed for me here, and those solitary dwelling-places. 

And here many earth-worms shall chew upon you; dark creatures, greedy and ravenous, shall tear you painfully. Are your possessions nothing now—those things you displayed to men here on earth? For it would have been much better for you than all the riches of the earth—unless you had distributed them for the sake of the Lord himself—if you had become at your birth a bird, or a fish in the sea, or a beast of the field <term>cattle: "naut," specifically oxen or livestock used for labor</term> that labored, a forest-wanderer though without reason, or the worst of wild beasts in the wilderness, if God had so willed it. 

Even if you were the grimmest of the serpent-kind <term>worm-kind: "wyrmcynna," a broad term covering snakes, dragons, or the very worms now eating the body</term>—if God so willed—it would be better than that you ever became a human being on earth, or ever received baptism. For you must answer for us both on that Great Day <note>Judgment Day</note>, when the wounds shall be revealed to men—those evils which wicked men, workers of deeds, committed in the world. 

Then the Lord himself, the Creator of the heavens, will hear the deeds of every hero; he will demand a just requital for every wound <note>In Old English theology, sins were often conceptualized as visible wounds on the soul that would be "read" at the end of time</note> from every man everywhere. But what will you offer to the Lord there on Judgment Day? For then there will be no joint grown upon a limb, however small, for which you must not render a separate account.

--- Page 309 ---
<meta>This page continues the Old English poem "Soul and Body II" from the Vercelli Book. The soul is concluding its bitter rebuke of its own corpse, detailing the physical destruction of the body by grave-worms, before the poem shifts to contrast this with the experience of a holy soul.</meta>

...when the fierce [Lord] is revealed at the judgment. But what shall we two do? Must we two then suffer woe together afterwards, just as you once did for us here in the world? That accursed hoard of flesh <note>original: "flæschord," a common Old English poetic compound (kenning) for the body</note> shall then decay there; it will seek the bottom of hell, not the joys of heaven, weighed down by its deeds—unless [the Almighty] should promise help or comfort to the sorrowful ones. 

The head is split open, the hands are unjointed, the jaws gape, the palate is parched, and the tongue is overgrown with rot in the earth. The fingers have crumbled away. Fierce worms strip them; they use their tongues like tails—a hungry race <term>race: "cynn," meaning a species or kind of creature</term> of worms, eager for the feast. For they cannot exchange any words with that wretched spirit <note>original: "werigan gast," referring to the soul of the sinner which must watch the body's decay</note>.

"Glutton" <term>Glutton: "Gifer," the name of the personified lead worm who begins the process of decomposition</term> is the name of that worm, whose jaws are as sharp as needles. He slinks to me first of all in the grave <term>grave: "eorðscræfe," literally an "earth-cave"</term>; he tears the tongue through the teeth and crawls through; he eats the eyes out from the head, making room for others to join the worms' banquet, once that weary body has grown cold. That which was long ago adorned with garments is now food for worms in the earth. Every wise-minded person might hear this and take it to heart.

How much more hopeful it is when the holy soul travels to its flesh, wrapped in comfort; that errand is far more blessed! <note>The poem now shifts perspective from the damned soul to the "blessed soul," who visits its body with love rather than loathing</note>. It finds the body and greets it with joy, [recalling] that which it long endured, while the spirits speak the word of God.

<summary>The damned soul describes the grotesque physical decay of the body in the grave, personified by the lead worm "Gifer," before the poem transitions to the joyful reunion of a holy soul and its body.</summary>
<keywords>Soul and Body, Vercelli Book, Gifer, decomposition, Judgment Day, Anglo-Saxon poetry, eschatology</keywords>

--- Page 311 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discourse of the "Blessed Soul." However, the text here shifts from the poetic "Soul and Body" dialogue into a homiletic or "parenetic" fragment (a formal moral exhortation). It warns against the dangers of hypocrisy and the spiritual corruption of the world, using the common medieval metaphor of the bee to describe those who speak sweetly but harbor malice.</meta>

Truth comes in manifold ways. In the press of mouths, one man reproaches another behind his back with malice and with **bitter words**, yet speaks fairly to his face. That deed, however, he holds as an unclean hoard in his heart; it shall be revealed when the scriptures <note>original: "ge-ƿrita"; refers to the heavenly books or records of human deeds</note> accuse the wrongdoers. 

For this reason the prophet <note>Typically referring to King David in the Psalms; this echoes Psalm 28:3, "Draw me not away with the wicked."</note> said: "Do not deliver me together with the sinful into the rush of destruction, the wrongdoers and workers of evil; nor leave me in life with the liars who speak foulness and have <unclear>evil thoughts</unclear> in their chambers." They have some thoughts planned secretly so that their hope does not go where [it appears to]. 

Though he be fierce in his mind, stained with crimes, blended with misery, and filled with deceit, yet outwardly he shows fair words. He is just as the **bees** <note>The bee was a common symbol for a hypocrite: sweet honey at the front, a sharp sting at the back.</note>... [it] has honey in its mouth but a sting <note>original: "tuȝel"; likely a scribal error or variant for a sting or "tail"</note> behind; it works painful wounds at times with it when the slaughter comes. 

So it is likewise with those learned men who promise loyalty with their tongues in fair words, but think deceitfully. When at the last they <unclear>betray</unclear>, they have a honey-taste—a peace-speech—yet within the breast, through the **devil's craft**, they carry secret wounds. 

So is this world <term>middle-earth<gloss>middangeard; the physical world between heaven and hell</gloss></term> now blended with evil... <unclear>wandering</unclear>... the old one deals... daily with malice... unless he truly holds peace and safe love as God commanded him. For all hope in the worldly course is [failing].

->Ritmo IV, v. 1–41<-
->(Parenetic Fragment)<-

<vocab>manifoldly, prophet, middle-earth, devil's, worldly-course</vocab>

<summary>This page contains a moralizing fragment warning against hypocrisy. It compares deceitful people to bees that carry honey in their mouths but stings in their tails, and laments the spiritual corruption of "middle-earth."</summary>
<keywords>hypocrisy, bees, middle-earth, devil's craft, Psalms, judgment, deceit</keywords>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the conclusion of a moralizing homily (Vercelli Homily IV) into the beginning of one of the most famous poems in Old English, "The Dream of the Rood." The previous page discussed hypocrisy and the "sting" of the bee; this page concludes that warning and shifts focus to the ultimate "sign" of salvation: the Cross.</meta>

...abundantly. Counsel is [given]; he is not wise to Christ's soul who [ignores this]. Let us now know how to think and hope toward that **sign** <note>original: "beacnan"; here referring to the Cross as a beacon or guiding light</note> so that we may possess the heavenly light above with the angels as a gift for our help, when God wills to judge <note>original: "ge-pyrcan"; literally to "work" or "act upon," here in the sense of the Final Judgment</note> this life.

**H**arken! I wish to tell of the choicest of dreams, what I dreamed at midnight, after **speech-bearers** <note>original: "reord berend"; a poetic term for human beings, defined by their ability to speak</note> remained at rest. It seemed to me that I saw a most wondrous tree led into the air, enfolded in light, the brightest of beams. All that **sign** was covered in gold; gems stood fair at the corners of the earth, and there were five up on the **cross-beam** <note>original: "eaxle ge spanne"; literally "the span of the shoulders," referring to the horizontal bar of the cross</note>. All the angels of the Lord, fair through eternity, beheld it there; that was certainly no criminal's gallows, but holy spirits beheld it, men over the earth, and all this glorious creation.

Wondrous was that **victory-beam** <term>sige beam: a common Old English compound for the Cross, depicting it as a military standard of victory rather than just an instrument of death</term>, and I, stained with sins, wounded with vices, saw the tree of glory, honored with garments, shining with joys, adorned with gold; gems had worthily covered the Lord's tree. Yet I could perceive through that gold the ancient struggle of wretched men, that it first began to **bleed** <note>original: "swætan"; literally "to sweat," but used poetically here to describe the oozing of blood</note> on the right side. I was entirely troubled with sorrows; I was afraid before that fair sight. I saw that shifting sign <unclear>changing in light and color...</unclear>
